The traveling wave problem for the shallow water equations: well-posedness and the limits of vanishing viscosity and surface tension Thanks: N. Stevenson was supported by an NSF Graduate Research Fellowship Thanks: I. Tice was supported by an NSF Grant (DMS #2204912).
Noah Stevenson, Ian Tice

Abstract
. In this paper we study solitary traveling wave solutions to a damped shallow water system, which is in general quasilinear and of mixed type. We develop a small data well-posedness theory and prove that traveling wave solutions are a generic phenomenon that persist with and without viscosity or surface tension and for all nontrivial traveling wave speeds, even when the parameters dictate that the equations are hyperbolic and have a sound speed. This theory is developed by way of a Nash-Moser implicit function theorem, which allows us to prove strong norm continuity of solutions with respect to the data as well as the parameters, even in the vanishing limits of viscosity and surface tension.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 35Q35, 35C07, 35B30; Secondary 47J07, 76A20, 35M30

1.
Introduction

1.1.
The shallow water equations, traveling formulation, and the role of applied force

We begin by stating the time-dependent formulation of the damped shallow water model under consideration in this work. Let
d\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
denote the spatial dimension, the most physically relevant options for which are
1
and
2
(but the analysis here works more generally). The shallow water system with applied forcing consists of equations coupling the velocity
\overline{\upupsilon}:\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the free surface
\overline{\upeta}:\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
to the applied forcing
\overline{\mathbf{f}}:\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The system reads

(1.1.1)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\overline{\upeta}+\nabla\cdot(\overline{\upeta}\overline{\upupsilon})=0,\\
\overline{\upeta}(\partial_{t}\overline{\upupsilon}+\overline{\upupsilon}\cdot\nabla\overline{\upupsilon})+\alpha\overline{\upupsilon}-\overline{\mu}\nabla\cdot(\overline{\upeta}\mathbb{S}\overline{\upupsilon})+\overline{\upeta}\nabla(g\overline{\upeta}-\overline{\sigma}\Delta\overline{\upeta})+\overline{\mathbf{f}}=0,\end{cases}

where
\mathbb{S}\overline{\upupsilon}
is a viscous stress tensor-like object obeying the formula

(1.1.2)

\mathbb{S}\overline{\upupsilon}=\nabla\overline{\upupsilon}+\nabla\overline{\upupsilon}^{\mathrm{t}}+2(\nabla\cdot\overline{\upupsilon})I_{d\times d},

and
\overline{\mu},\overline{\sigma}\in[0,\infty)
are the viscosity and the surface tension, respectively. The parameter
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
is the characteristic slip speed, which controls the strength of the zeroth order damping effect. The gravitational field strength is
g\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

The shallow water system (
1.1.1
), which is often called the Saint-Venant system when
\overline{\mu}=\overline{\sigma}=0
, is an important model approximating the incompressible free boundary Navier-Stokes system in a regime in which the fluid depth is much smaller than its characteristic horizontal scale, and as such is both physically and practically relevant. A derivation of the system (
1.1.1
) from the free boundary Navier-Stokes system proceeds through an asymptotic expansion and vertical averaging procedure; for full details we refer, for instance, to the surveys of Bresch
[
14
]
or Mascia
[
44
]
.

One can see that system (
1.1.1
) has formal similarities to the barotropic compressible Navier-Stokes equations, with
\overline{\upupsilon}
and
\overline{\upeta}
playing the roles of the fluid velocity and density, respectively, and with a quadratic pressure law (corresponding to
\overline{\upeta}\nabla g\overline{\upeta}=\nabla(\frac{g}{2}\overline{\upeta}^{2})
) and viscosity coefficients proportional to density (corresponding to
\overline{\mu}\overline{\upeta}
). The first equation in (
1.1.1
) is thus analogously dubbed the continuity equation; it dictates how the free surface is stretched and transported by the velocity. Note that this serves as a replacement for incompressibility, which is lost in the shallow limit.

The second equation in the system, which one could call the momentum equation, has several interesting features. The advective derivative
\overline{\upeta}(\partial_{t}\overline{\upupsilon}+\overline{\upupsilon}\cdot\nabla\overline{\upupsilon})
is balanced by both dissipative and hyperbolic terms in addition to the forcing
\overline{\mathbf{f}}
. The dissipation structure, which is
\alpha\overline{\upupsilon}-\overline{\mu}\nabla\cdot(\overline{\upeta}\mathbb{S}\overline{\upupsilon})
, has two parts. The former, zeroth order contribution is the manifestation of the Navier slip boundary condition in the shallow limit; it represents a sort of frictional drag induced by tangential fluid motion at the bottom boundary. The latter, second order contribution is a manifestation of the viscous stress tensor from the incompressible Navier-Stokes system. Analogous to how incompressibility is lost in the shallow water limit, the shallow water viscous stress tensor more closely resembles the viscous stress tensor from compressible Navier-Stokes. On the other hand, the hyperbolic structure, which is
\overline{\upeta}\nabla(g\overline{\upeta}-\overline{\sigma}\Delta\overline{\upeta})
, is directly descended from the gravity-capillary operator in the free boundary Navier-Stokes system’s dynamic boundary condition.

When
\overline{\mathbf{f}}=0
the system (
1.1.1
) admits an equilibrium solution
\overline{\upupsilon}=0
and
\overline{\upeta}=H
for any
H\in\mathbb{R}
. After fixing some
H\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
(it is only physically meaningful to consider positive equilibrium height), we can perform a rescaling and perturbative reformulation to obtain a non-dimensional version of system (
1.1.1
) in which
(\alpha,g,H)\mapsto(1,1,1)
. Indeed, upon defining the characteristic length
\mathrm{L}
and time
\mathrm{T}

(1.1.3)

\mathrm{L}=\frac{\sqrt{gH}}{\alpha}H\text{ and }\mathrm{T}=\frac{H}{\alpha},

along with the unitless quantities
\mu,\sigma\in[0,\infty)
given by

(1.1.4)

\mu^{2}=\frac{\overline{\mu}\mathrm{T}}{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\frac{\overline{\mu}\alpha}{gH^{2}}\text{ and }\sigma^{2}=\frac{\overline{\sigma}}{g\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\frac{\overline{\sigma}\alpha^{2}}{g^{2}H^{3}},

the system (
1.1.1
) transforms to the nondimensional perturbative form

(1.1.5)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\upeta+\nabla\cdot((1+\upeta)\upupsilon)=0,\\
(1+\upeta)(\partial_{t}\upupsilon+\upupsilon\cdot\nabla\upupsilon)+\upupsilon-\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot((1+\upeta)\mathbb{S}\upupsilon)+(1+\upeta)\nabla(\upeta-\sigma^{2}\Delta\upeta)+\mathbf{f}=0,\end{cases}

where
\upupsilon
,
\upeta
, and
\mathbf{f}
in (
1.1.5
) are obtained from
\overline{\upupsilon}
,
\overline{\upeta}
, and
\overline{\mathbf{f}}
in (
1.1.1
) via the rescalings

(1.1.6)

\overline{\upupsilon}(t,x)=\frac{\mathrm{L}}{\mathrm{T}}\cdot\upupsilon(t/\mathrm{T},x/\mathrm{L}),\quad\overline{\upeta}(t,x)=\frac{\alpha\mathrm{L}}{\sqrt{gH}}\cdot(1+\upeta(t/\mathrm{T},x/\mathrm{L})),\\
\text{and }\overline{\mathbf{f}}(t,x)=\alpha\sqrt{gH}\cdot\mathbf{f}(t/\mathrm{T},x/\mathrm{L}),\quad\text{for }(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}.

We emphasize that in the system (
1.1.5
) the nondimensionalized viscosity
\mu^{2}
and surface tension
\sigma^{2}
appear as squares only for the sake of convenience in our subsequent analysis. Furthermore, we see from (
1.1.4
) that
1/\mu^{2}
is the Reynolds number of system (
1.1.5
), while
1/\sigma^{2}
is the Bond number.

In the formulation (
1.1.5
), one further interesting aspect of the equations is revealed: there is either a characteristic wave speed (the ‘speed of sound’) or a more complicated dispersion relation, depending on whether
\sigma=0
or
\sigma\neq 0
. Indeed, if we take the divergence of the second equation, and substitute in the first, we find that

(1.1.7)

\partial_{t}^{2}\eta+(1-4\mu^{2}\Delta)\partial_{t}\eta-\Delta(1-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\eta=\text{forcing and nonlinear terms}.

This reveals that
\eta
obeys a dispersive or wave-like equation with a damping effect (the middle term on the left), driven by applied forces and nonlinear interactions. If we ignore the damping term and focus on the part
\partial_{t}^{2}\eta-\Delta(1-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\eta
, then we arrive at the dispersion relation
\omega(\xi)^{2}=|\xi|^{2}(1+4\pi^{2}\sigma^{2}|\xi|^{2})
. This reveals two notable features of
\sigma
: first, if
\sigma=0
then the group and phase velocities agree and have unit magnitude; second, if
\sigma>0
then the group and phase velocities are colinear and have magnitudes of order
O(\left|\xi\right|)
as
\left|\xi\right|\to\infty
, but the ratio of the group speed to the phase speed converges to
2
, which is the reciprocal of the ratio one obtains from the standard deep water dispersion relation,
\omega(\xi)^{2}=\left|\xi\right|
.

In this work we are interested in traveling wave solutions to (
1.1.5
), which are solitary perturbations of the equilibrium
(\upupsilon,\upeta)=(0,0)
that are stationary when viewed in a coordinate system moving at a fixed velocity. By the rotational invariance of the equations, we lose no generality in assuming that the velocity of the traveling wave is colinear with
e_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the unit vector in the first coordinate direction. We thus fix
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
to be the speed of our traveling wave and posit the traveling ansatz

(1.1.8)

\upupsilon(t,x)=v(x-t\gamma e_{1}),\hskip 10.00002pt\upeta(t,x)=\eta(x-t\gamma e_{1}),\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbf{f}(t,x)=\mathcal{F}(x-t\gamma e_{1}),\text{ for }(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},

for new unknowns
v:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\eta:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and traveling applied forcing
\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Under this ansatz, the system (
1.1.5
) becomes

(1.1.9)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot((1+\eta)(v-\gamma e_{1}))=0,\\
(1+\eta)(v-\gamma e_{1})\cdot\nabla v+v-\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot((1+\eta)\mathbb{S}v)+(1+\eta)(I-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\eta+\mathcal{F}=0.\end{cases}

Due to the fact that the hyperbolic part of (
1.1.5
), which is described in (
1.1.7
), has speed of sound equal to unity when neglecting capillary effects, we expect that the traveling problem (
1.1.9
) behaves quite differently depending on whether or not the speed of the applied forcing is subsonic
(\gamma<1)
or supersonic (
\gamma\geqslant 1
).

We now turn to a discussion of the precise form of the forcing
\mathcal{F}
appearing in the second equation of (
1.1.9
), endeavoring to make a physically meaningful choice. For this one needs consider the derivation of the traveling shallow water equations from the traveling free boundary incompressible Navier-Stokes equations. We perform the relevant calculations in Appendix
B.1
. The idea is that for the latter Navier-Stokes system in traveling form, a forcing term
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
acts on the bulk and a symmetric tensor
\Xi:\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\to\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{sym}}^{(d+1)\times(d+1)}
generates a stress that acts on the free surface. For instance,
\Xi
can model wind or other localized surface stress, and
\phi
can be a local gravity perturbation as in a primitive model of an ocean-moon system.

As we show in (
B.1.31
), in the shallow water limit the applied force and stress dictate that
\mathcal{F}
has the form

(1.1.10)

\mathcal{F}=\int_{0}^{1+\eta}f(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y+\Upxi(\cdot,1+\eta)\nabla\eta-\xi(\cdot,1+\eta)-\upxi(\cdot,1+\eta)\nabla\eta-(1+\eta)\nabla(\upxi(\cdot,1+\eta)),

where

(1.1.11)

\phi=(f,\phi\cdot e_{n}),\;\Xi=\begin{pmatrix}\Upxi&\xi\\
\xi&\upxi\end{pmatrix},\;\Upxi:\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\mathrm{sym}},\;\xi:\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},\;\upxi:\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\to\mathbb{R},\;f:\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}.

While (
1.1.10
) gives the correct physically relevant form of the forcing, it clearly possesses some structural redundancies. Thus, for the purposes of our analysis it is convenient to consider a class of forcing that is symbolically less cumbersome, yet of the type (
1.1.10
). We discuss this now.

As we mentioned previously, the relationship between the wave speed
\gamma
and the speed of sound is important. We elect to distinguish two different parameter regimes, depending on
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, in which the regularity counting schemes for the solutions differ. The first is the
omnisonic
regime, which is wave speed agnostic, and the second is the
subsonic
regime, which is determined via
\gamma\in(0,1)
. Motivated by (
1.1.10
) and the anticipation of differing quantitative regularity, we are thus led us to formulate the forcing terms in the following two different ways, depending on which regime we are in:

(1.1.12)

\mathcal{F}=\begin{cases}\mathrm{Tr}\tau[\eta]\nabla\eta+(\mu+\sigma)\tau[\eta]\nabla\eta+\varphi[\eta]&\text{when~\eqref{traveling wave formulation of the equation} is thought of as omnisonic},\\
\tau[\eta]\nabla\eta+\varphi[\eta]&\text{when~\eqref{traveling wave formulation of the equation} is thought of as subsonic},\end{cases}

where
\tau[\eta]:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
,
\varphi[\eta]:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
are assumed to have the following form

(1.1.13)

\tau[\eta]=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\tau_{i}\eta^{i},\hskip 10.00002pt\varphi[\eta]=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\varphi_{i}\eta^{i},

with
\tau_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
\varphi_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
i\in\{{0,\dotsc,\ell}\}
and fixed
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. These are meant to be an
\ell^{\mathrm{th}}
order approximation of superposition type nonlinearities

(1.1.14)

\tau[\eta]\approx\tau(\cdot,\eta),\hskip 10.00002pt\varphi[\eta]\approx\varphi(\cdot,\eta),\hskip 10.00002pt\tau:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},\hskip 10.00002pt\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}

where for
i\in\{{0,1,\dots,\ell}\}
we make the identification

(1.1.15)

\tau_{i}=(i!)^{-1}(\partial_{d+1}^{i}\tau)(\cdot,0),\hskip 10.00002pt\varphi_{i}=(i!)^{-1}(\partial_{d+1}^{i}\varphi)(\cdot,0).

We elect to have the generators
\tau[\eta]
and
\varphi[\eta]
of the forcing term
\mathcal{F}
in (
1.1.9
) have the form (
1.1.13
) for the following reasons. First, they are strictly more general than the special case of
\ell=0
, which is perhaps the most physically relevant. The flexibility of
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
allows for a very large class of superposition type nonlinearities as in (
1.1.14
) to be considered exactly or approximately. Finally, the appearance of, at worst, polynomial nonlinearities in (
1.1.13
) allows us to avoid a lengthy and unenlightening digression in which we would handle the fully nonlinear compositions. We emphasize that the reader interested in including such compositions in the analysis of (
1.1.9
) could do so by importing techniques from our previous work on the free boundary compressible Navier-Stokes system
[
56
]
.

The difference between the two forms of
\mathcal{F}
in (
1.1.12
) is that in the omnisonic regime we are asking that tensorial part of
\tau
acting on the gradient of
\eta
obeys an extra smallness condition in the limit
\mu
and
\sigma
tending to zero, as quantified by the
(\mu+\sigma)
prefactor in (
1.1.12
). This is purely for technical reasons, and the authors are ignorant of whether or not removing this condition presents a genuine obstruction.

Finally, we discuss the necessity of including the forcing term
\mathcal{F}
. An elementary power-dissipation calculation, which we carry out in Appendix
B.2
, shows that solutions to (
1.1.9
) belonging to an
L^{2}
-based Sobolev space framework satisfy the identity

(1.1.16)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v|^{2}+{\mu^{2}}(1+\eta)\Big(\frac{1}{2}|\mathbb{D}^{0}v|^{2}+2\Big(1+\frac{1}{d}\Big)|\nabla\cdot v|^{2}\Big)+\mathcal{F}\cdot v=0,

where
\mathbb{D}^{0}v=\nabla v+\nabla v^{\mathrm{t}}-(2/d)(\nabla\cdot v)I_{d\times d}
. Therefore, if
1+\eta\geqslant 0
and
\mathcal{F}=0
, then (
1.1.16
) implies that
v=0
. We can then return to the second equation in (
1.1.9
) to deduce that
\nabla\eta=0
, and hence
\eta
is a constant. This tells us that nontrivial solitary traveling wave solutions to the shallow water system studied here cannot exist unless they are generated by traveling applied stress or force. We emphasize that in this identity we are crucially using that the system (
1.1.9
) has a drag term, which yields the
L^{2}
control on the left.

We thus arrive at our main goal in this paper: for every choice of physical parameters
(\gamma,\mu,\sigma)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times[0,\infty)^{2}
we wish to identify an open set of stress and forcing data that determine
\mathcal{F}
and give rise to locally unique and nontrivial solutions to the traveling shallow water system. Moreover, we wish to establish well-posedness in the sense of continuity of the solution as a function of the data and physical parameters, even in the vanishing limit of viscosity or surface tension. See Figure
1
for various plots of some of the traveling wave solutions considered in this work.

Figure 1.

Depicted here are 3D and relief plots of numerical simulations of the free surface component of the solution to (
1.1.9
) generated by a fixed applied Gaussian stress moving to the right for various values of
\gamma\in\{{0.992,1.008}\}
,
\mu\in\{{0,0.0078}\}
, and
\sigma\in\{{0,0.003}\}
. Notice that the wave speed
\gamma
transitioning from subsonic to supersonic leads to a shock wave-like formation in the free surface, though no discontinuity actually forms.

1.2.
Previous work

We now turn to a brief survey of the literature associated with the shallow water systems (
1.1.1
) and (
1.1.9
) and their variants. There is a large body of work, so we shall restrict our attention to those results most closely related to ours.

We emphasize from the start that there are numerous versions of the shallow water equations, all of which are derived from the free boundary Euler or Navier-Stokes systems in one way or another; for various examples, we refer to
[
41
,
23
,
11
,
12
,
43
,
13
,
37
]
, and in particular the articles of Bresch
[
14
]
and Mascia
[
44
]
. As such, it is important that we give at least a rapid review of literature associated with the derived models’ parents.

For a thorough review of the fully dynamic free boundary incompressible Navier-Stokes equations in various geometries we refer to the surveys of Zadrzyńska
[
69
]
and Shibata and Shimizu
[
53
]
. While neglecting surface tension, Beale
[
8
]
established local well-posedness. Beale
[
9
]
then, with surface tension accounted for, established the existence of global solutions and derived their decay properties with Nishida
[
10
]
. In other settings, solutions with surface tension were also constructed by Allain
[
3
]
, Tani
[
60
]
, Bae
[
4
]
, and Shibata and Shimizu
[
54
]
. Similarly, solutions without surface tension were produced by Abels
[
1
]
, Guo and Tice
[
27
,
28
]
, and Wu
[
67
]
.

The inviscid analog of the aforementioned free boundary Navier-Stokes in traveling wave form, which is also known as the traveling water wave problem, has been the subject of major work for more than a century. For a thorough review, see the survey articles of Toland
[
61
]
, Groves
[
26
]
, Strauss
[
58
]
, and Haziot, Hur, Strauss, Toland, Wahlén, Walsh, and Wheeler
[
31
]
.

In contrast, work on the traveling wave problem for the free boundary Navier-Stokes equations has only recently appeared in the literature. Leoni and Tice
[
38
]
built a well-posedness theory for small forcing and stress data, provided
\gamma\neq 0
. This was generalized to multi-layer and stationary (
\gamma=0
) settings by Stevenson and Tice
[
57
,
55
]
and to inclined and periodic configurations by Koganemaru and Tice
[
36
]
. The corresponding well-posedness theory for the free boundary compressible Navier-Stokes equations was developed by Stevenson and Tice
[
56
]
. Traveling waves for the Muskat problem were constructed with similar techniques by Nguyen and Tice
[
47
]
. There are also experimental studies of viscous traveling waves; for details, we refer to the work of Akylas, Cho, Diorio, and Duncan
[
18
,
21
]
, Masnadi and Duncan
[
45
]
, and Park and Cho
[
51
,
52
]
.

To reiterate the goal of this work, we wish to extend the previously mentioned theory on forced traveling wave solutions for viscous or dissipative fluid models to the system of shallow water equations. This then brings us to the next body of literature to be surveyed, which are results on shallow water systems similar to the one considered in this work. For the sake of brevity, we shall only consider the following two families of results: 1) the local and global well-posedness for variations on the time dependent shallow water system (
1.1.1
) and 2) the roll-waves phenomena for a tilted version of the equations.

Ton
[
62
]
established local well-posedness for a version of the viscous equations without surface tension, with small initial data in 2D bounded domains. This result was extended by Kloeden
[
34
]
to give global in time existence for data near equilibrium. The inviscid, undamped, version of the equations without surface tension, also known as the Saint-Venant equations, fits within the realm of hyperbolic conservation laws when restricted to one dimension; consequently, the result of Lions, Perthame, and Souganidis
[
40
]
on the existence and stability of solutions to 1-dimensional hyperbolic conservation laws in particular establishes existence for 1D Saint-Venant. Sundbye
[
59
]
studied global existence for near equilibrium data when considering a Coriolis force and external force in the viscous equations without surface tension in 2D. Bresch and Desjardins
[
15
]
proved the existence of global weak solutions to a viscous shallow water model in 2D and studied the quasi-geostrophic limit. Wang and Xu
[
64
]
obtained local and global solutions for any and small, respectively, initial data for the equations in 2D while neglecting both the drag term and surface tension. Hao
[
29
]
studied global in time existence for near equilibrium data for the Cauchy problem including the effects of surface tension in 2D. Bresch and Nobel
[
16
]
rigorously justified the shallow water limit of the dynamic problem in 2D without surface tension by utilizing ‘higher order’ versions of the equations. Chen and Perepelitsa
[
17
]
studied the inviscid limit in a weak topology without a drag term and without surface tension in 1D. For a two dimensional rotating shallow water model, Wang
[
65
]
analyzed the inviscid limit. Haspot
[
30
]
proved global in time existence for a certain class of large initial data to a related variation on the shallow water equations. Yang, Liu, and Hao
[
68
]
study the simultaneous limit of vanishing viscosity and Froude number for a two dimension system without surface tension.

We now briefly discuss the literature on roll waves, which are a sort of unforced traveling wave phenomenon that can appear in variants on the shallow water system derived with a non-trivially tilted bottom (equivalently, derived with a tangential component of gravity). Dressler
[
22
]
proved the existence of discontinuous roll wave solutions to an inviscid shallow water model in 1D. Barker, Johnson, Rodrigues, Zumbrun and Barker, Johnson, Noble, Rodrigues, Zumbrun
[
7
,
6
]
performed a stability analysis for solitary roll wave solutions to the viscous equations in 1D with various forms of the drag term. Also in 1D, Balmforth and Madre
[
5
]
studied the dynamics and stability of roll waves, including the effects with bottom drag and viscosity while doing so. The linear stability of 1D viscous roll waves was further studied by Noble
[
49
]
. Periodic roll wave trains were studied numerically in 1D, and also for a reduced model in 2D, by Ivanova, Nkonga, Richard
[
32
]
. Johnson, Noble, Rodrigues, Yang, and Zumbrun
[
33
]
studied the stability of discontinuous roll wave solutions to the 1D inviscid Saint-Venant equations with a certain drag term.

Our results for the shallow water system are the first to establish well-posedness of the traveling equations with small forcing; moreover, we find that this can be done regardless whether or not one accounts for the effects of surface tension and viscosity. In particular, we rule out the possibility of unforced roll waves with a normal-pointing gravitational field and establish the generality of traveling waves with forcing, which are a special type of global in time solutions to (
1.1.1
).

We emphasize that, while our analysis is capable of handling viscosity and surface tension either on or off, the drag term is essential and cannot be neglected. Without viscosity or surface tension, this results in a hyperbolic problem with frictional damping. There is a large literature related to the dynamics of such problems, which we now briefly survey to conclude our discussion of prior work. Matsumura
[
46
]
studied a damped quasilinear wave equation and employed the frictional damping term to achieve global existence. MacCamy
[
42
]
proved global well-posedness for an integrodifferential equation related to a damped wave equation. Nohel
[
50
]
studied a forced quasilinear wave equation with a frictional damping term. Greenberg and Li
[
25
]
studied the effects of boundary damping on a quasilinear wave equation. Dafermos
[
20
]
studied a system of damped conservation laws. Levine, Park, and Serrin
[
39
]
studied the Cauchy problem for a nonlinearly damped wave equation.

1.3.
Main results and discussion

In this paper we present three principal results on the system (
1.1.9
), which is the shallow water equations in traveling wave formulation. In order to properly state these results, we must first embark on a brief discussion of a certain nonstandard function space that will serve as our container for the perturbative free surface unknown, as in the prior works
[
36
,
38
,
47
,
57
,
56
]
. Given
\mathbb{R}\ni s\geqslant 0
we define the anisotropic Sobolev space

(1.3.1)

\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\{{\eta\in\mathscr{S}^{\ast}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})\;:\;\mathscr{F}[f]\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})\text{ and }\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}<\infty}\}

equipped with the norm

(1.3.2)

\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}=\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big(|\xi|^{-2}(|\xi|^{4}+\xi_{1}^{2})\mathds{1}_{B(0,1)}(\xi)+\langle\xi\rangle^{2s}\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B(0,1)}(\xi)\big)\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[\eta](\xi)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\xi\Big)^{1/2}.

We refer to Appendix
A.1
for more information on these function spaces. For the purposes of stating our main results, we only mention here that we have the embeddings
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})+C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with the former being strict if and only if
d\geqslant 2
.

Our first two main theorems handle small data well-posedness in the ‘easy cases’ of system (
1.1.9
) in which the equations obey an advantageous elliptic derivative counting and do not exhibit derivative loss. The first of these works in any dimension and requires that both the viscosity and surface tension parameters are different from zero.

Theorem 1

(Proved in Theorem
2.6
)
.

Let
\mathbb{N}\ni s>d/2
. There exist open sets

(1.3.3)

(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}\times\{{0}\}^{\ell+1}\times\{{0}\}^{\ell+1}\subset\mathscr{U}_{s}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}))^{\ell+1}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{\ell+1}

and

(1.3.4)

0\in\mathscr{V}_{s}(\mathbf{p})\subset H^{2+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\text{ for }\mathbf{p}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3},

as well as a smooth mapping

(1.3.5)

\mathscr{U}_{s}\ni(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell})\mapsto(v,\eta)\in\bigcup_{\mathbf{p}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}}\mathscr{V}_{s}(\mathbf{p})

with the following property. For all
(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell})\in\mathscr{U}_{s}
the pair
(v,\eta)
associated through (
1.3.5
) is the unique
(v,\eta)\in\mathscr{V}_{s}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma)
such that system (
1.1.9
) is classically satisfied with wave speed
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, viscosity
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, surface tension
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and forcing
\mathcal{F}
determined by
\eta
and the data
(\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell})
via

(1.3.6)

\mathcal{F}=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\eta^{i}(\tau_{i}\nabla\eta+\varphi_{i}).

As it turns out, if we specialize to one dimension, there is a larger parameter space region in which we can avoid the complications of derivative loss. The point of our next main theorem is to record the refinements that can be made to Theorem
1
under this specialization.

Theorem 2

(Proved in Theorems
2.13
and
2.14
)
.

Let
d=1
and
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 1
. Cover
(\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus\{{0}\})\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}\cup\mathcal{P}_{3}
, where the sets
\mathcal{P}_{i}
are given in the second item of Definition
2.8
, and fix
i\in\{{1,2,3}\}
. There exists an open set

(1.3.7)

\mathcal{P}_{i}\times\{{0}\}^{\ell+1}\times\{{0}\}^{\ell+1}\subset\mathscr{U}_{s}^{i}\subset\mathcal{P}_{i}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))^{\ell+1}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))^{\ell+1}

and a sequence of radii
\{{\varepsilon^{i}_{s,\mathbf{p}}}\}_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}_{i}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that the following hold.

(1)

To each
(\gamma,\mu,\eta,\{{\tau_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\ell})\in\mathscr{U}^{i}_{s}
there exists a unique
\eta
satisfying
P^{i}_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}\eta\in B_{H^{s}}(0,\varepsilon^{i}_{s,(\gamma,\mu,\sigma)})
, where the operators
P^{i}_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}
are given in the first item of Definition
2.8
, such that upon defining
v=\gamma\eta/(1+\eta)
we have that
(v,\eta)
are a solution to (
1.1.9
) with data
\mathcal{F}
determined from
\eta
,
\{{\tau_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\ell}
, and
\{{\varphi_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\ell}
as in (
1.3.6
).

(2)

The following mappings are well-defined and continuous.

(1.3.8)

\mathscr{U}^{i}_{s}\ni(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{\ell})\mapsto\begin{cases}(v,\eta)\in(H^{1+s}(\mathbb{R}))^{2},\\
\ \mu^{2}(v,\eta)\in(H^{2+s}(\mathbb{R}))^{2},\\
\sigma^{2}(v,\eta)\in(H^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}))^{2}.\end{cases}

The proofs of Theorems
1
and
2
proceed via applications of the standard implicit function theorem. As such, the theorems offer the following advantages. The number of derivatives required on the data to produce solutions is relatively low, at least compared to our next two main theorems. Additionally, the methodology is relatively simple and requires a low level of technical finesse. On the other hand, these results say nothing about the boundary of the
(\mu,\sigma)
parameter space nor the corresponding limits. The reason for this defect is essentially because these limiting cases exhibit derivative loss, and thus the standard implicit function theorem does not provide the requisite tools for studying them. By swapping to a Nash-Moser implicit function theorem, we are led to our next main result, which covers what we call the omnisonic case, since it applies equally well for all positive wave speeds.

Theorem 3

(Proved in Section
5.2
)
.

Let
\varsigma=19+2\lfloor d/2\rfloor\in\mathbb{N}
. There exists a nonincreasing sequence of relatively open subsets

(1.3.9)

\mathbb{R}^{+}\times[0,\infty)^{2}\times\{{0}\}^{\ell+1}\times\{{0}\}^{\ell+1}\subset\mathcal{U}_{s}\subset\mathbb{R}^{+}\times[0,\infty)^{2}\times(H^{\varsigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}))^{\ell+1}\times(H^{\varsigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{\ell+1}

for
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant\varsigma
, and a family of open subsets

(1.3.10)

0\in\mathcal{V}(\mathfrak{q})\subset H^{\varsigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{\varsigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\text{ for }\mathfrak{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times[0,\infty)^{2}

such that the following hold.

(1)

Existence and uniqueness:
For all
(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell})\in\mathcal{U}_{\varsigma}
there exists a unique
(v,\eta)\in\mathcal{V}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma)
such that system (
1.1.9
) is classically satisfied with wave speed
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, viscosity
\mu\in[0,\infty)
, surface tension
\sigma\in[0,\infty)
, and data
\mathcal{F}
determined from
\eta
,
\mu+\sigma
, and
(\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell})
via the omnisonic case of (
1.1.12
).

(2)

Regularity promotion:
If
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant\varsigma
and

(1.3.11)

(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell})\in\mathcal{U}_{s}\cap(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}))^{\ell+1}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{\ell+1})

then the corresponding solution
(v,\eta)\in\mathcal{V}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma)
produced by the previous item obeys the inclusions

(1.3.12)

\langle\mu\nabla\rangle^{2}v\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\text{ and }(1+(\mu+\sigma)|\nabla|)\langle\sigma\nabla\rangle^{2}\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(3)

Continuous dependence:
For any
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant\varsigma
the three maps

(1.3.13)

\mathcal{U}_{s}\cap(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}))^{\ell+1}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{\ell+1})\ni(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell})\\
\mapsto\begin{cases}(v,\eta)\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\\
(\mu\nabla v,\mu^{2}\nabla^{2}v)\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d^{2}})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d^{3}})\\
(\mu+\sigma)(\nabla\eta,\sigma\nabla^{2}\eta,\sigma^{2}\nabla^{3}\eta)\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d^{2}})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d^{3}})\end{cases}

are all continuous.

Theorem
3
is only optimal, in the sense of regularity counting in the limiting cases of (
1.3.13
), in the supersonic regime defined via
\gamma\geqslant 1
. In the subsonic case of
0<\gamma<1
, we can invoke the Nash-Moser strategy with improved estimates along the way and arrive at our fourth and final main result.

Theorem 4

(Proved in Section
5.2
)
.

Let
\varpi=17+2\lfloor d/2\rfloor\in\mathbb{N}
. There exists a nonincreasing sequence of relatively open subsets

(1.3.14)

(0,1)\times[0,\infty)^{2}\times\{{0}\}^{\ell+1}\times\{{0}\}^{\ell+1}\subset\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s}\subset(0,1)\times[0,\infty)^{2}\times(H^{\varpi}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}))^{\ell+1}\times(H^{\varpi}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{\ell+1},

for
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant\varpi
, and a family of open subsets

(1.3.15)

0\in\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{sub}}(\mathfrak{q})\subset H^{\varpi}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{1+\varpi}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\text{ for }\mathfrak{q}\in(0,1)\times[0,\infty)^{2}

such that the following hold.

(1)

Existence and uniqueness:
For all
(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell})\in\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{\varpi}
there exists a unique
(v,\eta)\in\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{sub}}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma)
such that system (
1.1.9
) is classically satisfied with wave speed
\gamma\in(0,1)
, viscosity
\mu\in[0,\infty)
, surface tension
\sigma\in[0,\infty)
, and data
\mathcal{F}
determined from
\eta
and
(\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell})
via the subsonic case of (
1.1.12
).

(2)

Regularity promotion:
If
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant\varpi
and

(1.3.16)

(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell})\in\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s}\cap(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}))^{\ell+1}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{\ell+1})

then the corresponding solution
(v,\eta)\in\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{sub}}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma)
produced by the previous item obeys the inclusions

(1.3.17)

\langle\mu\nabla\rangle^{2}v\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}),\text{ and }\langle\sigma\nabla\rangle^{2}\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{1+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(3)

Continuous dependence:
For any
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant\varpi
the three map maps

(1.3.18)

\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s}\cap(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}))^{\ell+1}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{\ell+1})\ni(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell})\\
\mapsto\begin{cases}(v,\eta)\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{1+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\\
(\mu\nabla v,\mu^{2}\nabla^{2}v)\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d^{2}})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d^{3}})\\
(\sigma\nabla\eta,\sigma^{2}\nabla^{2}\eta)\in H^{1+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times H^{1+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d^{2}})\end{cases}

are all continuous.

Theorems
1
,
2
,
3
, and
4
package several different properties of solutions to (
1.1.9
) in a rather technical form. We now pause to elaborate on their content with a series of remarks. A high level summary of our results can be stated as follows. Small amplitude solitary traveling wave solutions to the shallow water equations are a generic phenomenon for all nonnegative values viscosity and surface tension, and all positive wave speeds. These traveling wave solutions depend continuously on the data as well as the physical parameters (wave speed, viscosity, surface tension), even in the vanishing limits of the latter two parameters. Finally, solutions to the traveling shallow water equations (
1.1.9
) have different quantitative regularity depending on whether or not the wave speed is subsonic or supersonic.

Our main theorems also lead to some corollaries worth mentioning. If we fix the physical parameters
(\gamma,\mu,\sigma)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times[0,\infty)^{2}
, then we are assured of the existence of a non-empty open set of forcing data on which we have a well-posedness theory. See Corollary
5.3
for a precise formulation. On the other hand, our main theorems also show that for fixed forcing data we have a continuous two parameter family of solutions depending on
\mu
and
\sigma
; in particular, we can send
\mu\to 0
, which is the inviscid limit, or
\sigma\to 0
, which is the vanishing surface tension limit. See Corollary
5.4
for a formal statement. In thinking about this limit it is perhaps useful to recall that
\mu
and
\sigma
are non-dimensional parameters related to gravity, the slip parameter, the equilibrium height, the dimensional viscosity, and the dimensional surface tension via (
1.1.4
).

We emphasize that our analysis of (
1.1.9
) relies on the wave speed
\gamma
being strictly positive. This is because it allows us to exploit the useful supercritical embedding and algebraic properties of the anisotropic Sobolev spaces
\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and thus close estimates in
L^{2}
-based Sobolev spaces. The fact that the free surface unknown is forced to belong to the anisotropic Sobolev spaces (
1.3.1
) rather than a standard Sobolev space can be seen via an inspection of the linear-in-
\eta
terms in (
1.1.9
). The lowest order differential operators appearing in these linear terms are first order, namely
\gamma\partial_{1}\eta
in the first equation and
\nabla\eta
in the second equation. As it turns out, we shall view the first equation of (
1.1.9
) as an identity in
(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1+s})(\mathbb{R}^{d})
whereas the second equation is an identity in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Hence the low Fourier mode behavior of
\eta
is determined via the inclusions
\nabla\eta\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\gamma\partial_{1}\eta\in\dot{H}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. This is exactly what is captured for
|\xi|\leqslant 1
in the integral of definition (
1.3.2
). While we remain optimistic that similar results for the fully stationary (
\gamma=0
) variant of (
1.1.9
) can be proved in the future, such results will not be possible within this paper’s functional framework. Our recent work on the solitary stationary wave problem for the three dimensional free boundary Navier-Stokes equations
[
55
]
suggests that a framework built from
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-based Sobolev spaces for
1<p<2
might serve as an appropriate replacement for the stationary shallow water problem when
d=2
.

Next, we aim to overview both the main difficulties in proving Theorems
1
,
2
,
3
, and
4
and our strategies for overcoming them. The system (
1.1.9
) is in general quasilinear, does not enjoy a variational formulation, and is posed in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which is non-compact. Consequently, Fredholm, variational, or compactness techniques are unavailable. On the other hand, we have the expectation of a robust theory for the family of linearizations of these equations, which suggests that the production of solutions ought to follow some perturbative argument based on linear theory. This leads us to a strategy modeled on the implicit and inverse function theorems. We begin our discussion of this strategy by examining the linearization of (
1.1.9
) around vanishing data and the trivial solution with a fixed choice of parameters
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
\mu,\sigma\in[0,\infty)
:

(1.3.19)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot v-\gamma\partial_{1}\eta=h,\\
-\gamma\partial_{1}v+v-\mu^{2}(\Delta v+3\nabla\nabla\cdot v)+(I-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\eta=f.\end{cases}

In (
1.3.19
), the unknowns are
v:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\eta:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, while the data are
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
.

The equations (
1.3.19
) constitute a time-independent linear system of differential equations, so it is natural to investigate its ellipticity, in the sense of Agmon-Douglis-Nirenberg
[
2
]
, as a function of the spatial dimension and the parameters
\mu
and
\sigma
. When (
1.3.19
) is ADN elliptic, the parent system (
1.1.9
) in the small data regime is entirely dominated by the linear part (
1.3.19
), provided that the nonlinear part of (
1.1.9
) has order not exceeding that of the linear part. On the other hand, when (
1.3.19
) fails to be ADN elliptic, the trivial linearization (
1.3.19
) cannot tell the full story, and derivative loss is expected.

In order to determine when (
1.3.19
) is ADN elliptic, we rewrite the problem as

(1.3.20)

\mathbf{P}\begin{pmatrix}v\\
\eta\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}f\\
h\end{pmatrix}\text{ for }\mathbf{P}=\begin{pmatrix}(1-\gamma\partial_{1})I-\mu^{2}(\Delta I+3\nabla\otimes\nabla)&(1-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\\
\nabla^{\mathrm{t}}&-\gamma\partial_{1}\end{pmatrix},

and we compute the scalar differential operator

(1.3.21)

\det\mathbf{P}\\
=(1-\gamma\partial_{1}-\mu^{2}\Delta)^{d-1}(\gamma^{2}\partial_{1}^{2}-\Delta+\sigma^{2}\Delta^{2}-\gamma\partial_{1}(1-4\mu^{2}\Delta)).

With these in hand, we can readily determine when the system (
1.3.19
) is ADN elliptic. We refer to the table in Figure
2
for the full details, but in brief: if
d=1
then the system is elliptic unless
\mu=\sigma=0
and
\gamma=1
(the exact sonic speed), and if
d\geqslant 2
then the system is elliptic only when both
\mu>0
and
\sigma>0
.

\begin{array}[]{| c || c | c | c | c |}\hline\cr&\mu>0,\;\sigma>0&\mu>0,\;\sigma=0&\mu=0,\;\sigma>0&\mu=0,\;\sigma=0\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr R&2d+2&2d+1&d+3&d+1\\
\hline\cr r&2d+2&2d+1&d+3&\begin{cases}d+1&\text{if }d\geqslant 2\\
2&\text{if }d=1,\;\gamma\neq 1\\
1&\text{if }d=1,\;\gamma=1\end{cases}\\
\hline\cr(\det\mathbf{P})_{\star}&\sigma^{2}\Delta^{2}(-\mu^{2}\Delta)^{d-1}&-4\gamma\partial_{1}(-\mu^{2}\Delta)^{d}&(-\gamma\partial_{1})^{d-1}\sigma^{2}\Delta^{2}&\begin{cases}(-\gamma\partial_{1})^{d-1}(\gamma^{2}\partial_{1}^{2}-\Delta)&\text{if }d\geqslant 2\\
(\gamma^{2}-1)\partial_{1}^{2}&\text{if }d=1,\;\gamma\neq 1\\
-\partial_{1}&\text{if }d=1,\;\gamma=1\end{cases}\\
\hline\cr\textnormal{Ellipic, }d=1&\textnormal{Yes}&\textnormal{Yes}&\textnormal{Yes}&\textnormal{Yes}\;\Leftrightarrow\;\gamma\neq 1\\
\hline\cr\textnormal{Ellipic, }d\geqslant 2&\textnormal{Yes}&\textnormal{No}&\textnormal{No}&\textnormal{No}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 2.
This table summarizes the information needed to determine whether or not the system (
1.3.19
) is ADN elliptic. For this we use the equivalent formulation of Volevič
[
63
]
(see also chapter 9 of
[
66
]
). The number
R\in\mathbb{N}
is the maximal order of
\prod_{i=d+1}^{n}\mathbf{P}_{i,\uppi(i)}
over all permutations
\uppi
of
\{1,\dotsc,d+1\}
. The number
r\in\mathbb{N}
is the order of the scalar operator
\det\mathbf{P}
, and
(\det\mathbf{P})_{\star}
denotes the principal part of
\det\mathbf{P}
. Volevič showed that
\mathbf{P}
is ADN elliptic if and only if
r=R
and
\det\mathbf{P}
is elliptic.

Our strategy for developing a well-posedness theory for (
1.1.9
) differs depending on whether or not (
1.3.19
) is ADN elliptic. When the former condition holds, a standard inverse function theorem argument - taking also into account the low mode behavior of
\mathbf{P}
and the anisotropic Sobolev spaces (
1.3.1
) - is sufficient to close. The full details of this execution are carried out in Section
2
; this then leads us to Theorems
1
and
2
.

On the other hand, if the trivial linearization (
1.3.19
) is not ADN elliptic, then the previous inverse function theorem strategy fails. The lack of ellipticity is connected to the emergence of derivative loss in our linear estimates; that is, we recover less on
v
or
\eta
than is spent by the forward differential operator. When presented with derivative loss at the boundary of a parameter space in which a standard inverse function theorem is applicable in the interior, it is natural to attempt upgrading and hence using a Nash-Moser inverse to reach the reach the remaining cases. This is what we do in Sections
3
,
4
, and
5
.

The reason we expect a Nash-Moser strategy to work at all when the ellipticity of (
1.3.19
) fails can be seen by closer inspection of the symbol associated with the determinant (
1.3.21
). Regardless of the choice of
(\gamma,\mu,\sigma)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times[0,\infty)^{2}
, we always have the following lower bound for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

(1.3.22)

|\mathscr{F}[\det\mathbf{P}](\xi)|\geqslant\Big(\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}\Big\langle\frac{2\pi\gamma\xi_{1}}{\langle 4\pi\mu\xi\rangle^{2}}\Big\rangle^{-1}\langle 2\pi\sigma\xi\rangle^{2}+2\pi\gamma|\xi_{1}|\langle 4\pi\mu\xi\rangle^{2}\Big)\langle\mu\xi\rangle^{2(d-1)}\geqslant\pi|\xi|^{2}\langle 2\pi\gamma\xi_{1}\rangle^{-1}+2\pi\gamma|\xi_{1}|.

This tells us that not only is the linearized differential operator
\mathbf{P}
of (
1.3.20
) invertible for non-zero frequencies, but
\det\mathbf{P}
is ‘bounded from below’ by an elliptic first order pseudodifferential operator. As a result, inversion of the trivial linearization will result in the recovery of some estimates on the solution, albeit without the correct the number of derivatives needed to close with a standard inverse function theorem argument. We emphasize that the reason that
\det\mathbf{P}
does not degenerate completely is because we are considering the shallow water system with frictional damping, which is responsible for the zeroth order term for
v
in (
1.3.19
), and with gravity, which gives us the full gradient of
\eta
in these equations. In the Nash-Moser framework, one needs estimates on solution operators to some family of linearizations indexed by an open neighborhood of smooth backgrounds. In order to close estimates for these nearby linearizations, the mere existence of the damping and gravity terms is not quite sufficient. We also need that the terms in the system of higher order than our estimate recovery level obey certain cancellations during the energy argument; see, for example, Lemma
4.2
or Proposition
4.3
for more details.

There are numerous versions of the Nash-Moser inverse function theorem in the literature; the one we use in this paper, which is recorded in Appendix
A.3
, was developed in an earlier paper of the authors
[
56
]
for use on the free boundary compressible Navier-Stokes traveling wave problem. The advantage of this version in the context of the shallow water system is that is suffices to do analysis and estimates in finite scales of Banach spaces and that the inverse function granted by the theorem is continuous between spaces obeying an optimal relative derivative counting. A pleasant corollary of this latter fact is that with Nash-Moser we can do more than merely handle the cases on the boundary of the
(\mu,\sigma)
-parameter space; in fact, we are able to understand very well the limiting behavior as one approaches the boundary from the interior of the parameter space.

Section
3
is dedicated to verifying the nonlinear hypotheses of the Nash-Moser theorem and to identifying a minimal essential structure for the linear analysis. Not only do hard inverse function theorems require one to check that the forward mapping is continuously differentiable, but it is typically the case that more regularity is sought and always the case that additional structured estimates on the map and its derivatives are required. In this section of the document we associate to the system (
1.1.9
) two nonlinear mapping and then verify the requisite smoothness and structured estimates. Afterward, we examine certain partial derivatives of these maps and identify a bare bones structure responsible for the derivative loss and prove that what remains can be handled perturbatively.

Section
4
is devoted to the verification of the linear hypotheses of the Nash-Moser theorem. We are tasked with showing that the linearizations of the nonlinear mappings identified in the previous section are left and right invertible and obey certain structured estimates for an open neighborhood of background points. Our strategy for achieving this is to first prove precise a priori estimates for the principal part of the linearizations by carefully exploiting cancellations of the higher-order terms; we then obtain existence for the principal part by combining with the method of continuity. Finally, we synthesize these results with the remainder estimates of the previous section to obtain the full linear hypotheses.

Section
5
combines the previous work on the nonlinear and linear hypotheses to two invocations of the Nash-Moser theorem. These are initially phrased abstractly, so we then unpack the details into the PDE-centric results of Theorems
3
and
4
.

The remainder of the paper consists of appendices. In Appendix
A.1
, we review important properties of the anisotropic Sobolev spaces. Appendix
A.2
is dedicated to the abstraction of the structures involved in the version of Nash-Moser employed here. Appendix
A.3
is the precise statement of this aforementioned theorem. Appendix
A.4
then gives us a concrete tool box for checking structured estimates on the Sobolev-type nonlinearities encountered here. Finally Appendix
B
contains the derivation of the forced-traveling wave formulation of the shallow water system and a power-dissipation calculation.

1.4.
Conventions of notation

The set
\{{0,1,2,\dots}\}
is denoted by
\mathbb{N}
whereas
\mathbb{N}^{+}=\mathbb{N}\setminus\{{0}\}
. We denote
\mathbb{R}^{+}=(0,\infty)
. The notation
\alpha\lesssim\beta
means that there exists
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, depending only on the parameters that are clear from context, for which
\alpha\leqslant C\beta
. To highlight the dependence of
C
on one or more particular parameters
a,\dots,b
, we will occasionally write
\alpha\lesssim_{a,\dots,b}\beta
. We also express that two quantities
\alpha
,
\beta
are equivalent, written
\alpha\asymp\beta
if both
\alpha\lesssim\beta
and
\beta\lesssim\alpha
. Throughout this document, the implicit constants always depend at least on the physical dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and the nonlinearity order
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
(see (
1.1.13
) and (
1.1.14
)). The bracket notation

(1.4.1)

\langle x\rangle=\sqrt{1+|x_{1}|^{2}+\dots+|x_{\ell}|^{2}}\text{ for }x\in\mathbb{C}^{\ell}

is frequently used. The notation
A\Subset B
means that the closure
\overline{A}
is compact and a subset of the interior
\mathrm{int}B
. If
\{{X_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{\upnu}
are normed spaces and
X
is their product, endowed with any choice of product norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{X}
, then we shall write

(1.4.2)

\lVert x_{1},\dots,x_{\upnu}\rVert_{X}=\lVert(x_{1},\dots,x_{\upnu})\rVert_{X}\text{ for }(x_{1},\dots,x_{\upnu})\in X.

The Fourier transform and its inverse on the space of tempered distributions
\mathscr{S}^{\ast}(\mathbb{R}^{d};)
, which are normalized to be unitary on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
, are denoted by
\mathscr{F}
,
\mathscr{F}^{-1}
, respectively. The
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-gradient is denoted by
\nabla=(\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{d})
and the divergence of a vector field
X:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is denoted by
\nabla\cdot X=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\partial_{j}(X\cdot e_{j})
. By
|\nabla|
and
\langle\nabla\rangle
, we mean the operator given by Fourier multiplication with symbols
\xi\mapsto 2\pi|\xi|
and
\xi\mapsto\langle 2\pi\xi\rangle
, respectively.

2.
Analysis of the elliptic cases

In this section we record the linear and nonlinear analysis of system (
1.1.9
) in the regimes for which there is no derivative loss in the sense that the linear problem recovers all of the derivatives spent by the full nonlinear equations. For our equations, this is equivalent to saying that the trivial linearization (
1.3.19
) is ADN elliptic. As it turns out, we do not have derivative loss under the following two (not mutually exclusive) assumptions. First,
\mu>0
and
\sigma>0
. Second, the spatial dimension,
d
, is equal to
1
and the positive wave speed,
\gamma
, is not equal to
1
. We find that in either of these cases, the problem of local well-posedness is felled by a relatively simple application of the implicit function theorem.

We glimpse how this initial strategy fails to handle the boundary cases of the above assumptions (e.g. the limits
\mu\to 0
or
\sigma\to 0
and the corresponding limiting cases for
d\geqslant 2
or
d=1
and
\gamma=1
), due to the emergent derivative loss’s incompatibility with standard inverse function theorems. In what follows, we will instead attack (
1.1.9
) with a Nash-Moser inverse function theorem to continuously recover well-posedness in all of the missing cases.

The plan of this section is as follows. In Section
2.1
we study the linearization of (
1.1.9
) at zero when viscosity and surface tension are both nonzero. We find the right spaces between which the linear operator acts as an isomorphism. Next, in Section
2.2
, we combine these isomorphism results with some basic nonlinear mapping properties and the inverse function theorem. Finally, in Section
2.3
, we have a special look at the case of one spatial dimension in (
1.1.9
). We find that whenever
\gamma\neq 1
, we can perform similar inverse function theorem analysis but this time gleaning more refined estimates.

2.1.
Theory of the trivial linearization

The linearization around zero of the traveling wave formulation for the shallow water system (
1.1.9
) is the following linear system.

(2.1.1)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot v-\gamma\partial_{1}\eta=h,\\
-\gamma\partial_{1}v+v-\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}v+(I-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\eta=f.\end{cases}

Here the data are a given scalar field
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and vector field
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, while the unknowns are the vector field
v:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the scalar
\eta:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
.

Our first result derives a system of equations equivalent to (
2.1.1
), but the unknowns are decoupled from one another. In what follows we shall use
\mathbb{P}
to denote the orthogonal projection in
L^{2}
onto the subspace of solenoidal vector fields.
\mathbb{P}
corresponds to the Fourier multiplier
\xi\mapsto I-|\xi|^{-2}\xi\otimes\xi
. We shall also let
\mathcal{R}=(\mathcal{R}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{R}_{d})
denote the vector of Riesz transforms;
\mathcal{R}
is given by the Fourier multiplier
\xi\mapsto\mathrm{i}|\xi|^{-1}\xi
so that
\mathcal{R}=|\nabla|^{-1}\nabla
and
\mathbb{P}=I+\mathcal{R}\otimes\mathcal{R}
.

Proposition 2.1

(Decoupling reformulation)
.

The following are equivalent for
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
f\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
h\in(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1+s})(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
v\in H^{2+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(1)

System (
2.1.1
) is satisfied by
v
and
\eta
with the data
f
and
h
.

(2)

It holds that

(2.1.2)

\begin{cases}(I-\gamma\partial_{1}-\mu^{2}\Delta)\mathbb{P}v=\mathbb{P}f,\\
(I-\mathbb{P})v=H-\gamma\mathcal{R}\mathcal{R}_{1}\eta,\\
-\gamma(I-4\mu^{2}\Delta)\mathcal{R}\mathcal{R}_{1}\eta+(I+\gamma^{2}\mathcal{R}_{1}^{2}-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\eta=(I-\mathbb{P})f-(I-\gamma\partial_{1}-4\mu^{2}\Delta)H,\end{cases}

where
H=\nabla\Delta^{-1}h\in(I-\mathbb{P})H^{2+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Moreover, we have norm equivalence
\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{H^{2+s}}\asymp\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1+s}}
.

Proof.

The first equation in (
2.1.1
) and the second equation in (
2.1.2
) are manifestly equivalent. The second equation of (
2.1.1
) is equivalent to the pair of equations obtained by applying the projectors
\mathbb{P}
and
I-\mathbb{P}
; the
\mathbb{P}
projection is the first equation of (
2.1.2
), while the third equation of (
2.1.2
) is equivalent to the
I-\mathbb{P}
projection via a substitution of the equivalent forms of the first equation in (
2.1.1
). The inclusion
H=\nabla\Delta^{-1}h\in(I-\mathbb{P})H^{s+2}
and the corresponding norm equivalence follow by decomposing
h
into its high and low frequency parts and using the
\dot{H}^{-1}
and
H^{1+s}
inclusions on each part, respectively.
∎

The benefit of the decoupling reformulation (
2.1.2
) is that the third equation allows us to solve for the
\eta
unknown in terms of the data alone. The following proposition does exactly this.

Proposition 2.2

(Solving for the linear free surface)
.

Given
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varphi\in(I-\mathbb{P})H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, there exists a unique
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

(2.1.3)

-\gamma(I-4\mu^{2}\Delta)\mathcal{R}\mathcal{R}_{1}\eta+(I+\gamma^{2}\mathcal{R}_{1}^{2}-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\eta=\varphi.

Proof.

We propose defining
\eta
via the equation

(2.1.4)

\eta=\big[{\gamma(I-4\mu^{2}\Delta)\mathcal{R}_{1}+(I+\gamma^{2}\mathcal{R}_{1}^{2}-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\mathcal{R}\cdot\nabla}\big]^{-1}\mathcal{R}\cdot\varphi,

but to justify this we must first establish the invertibility of the operator in brackets. The symbol of said operator is
\chi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
given by

(2.1.5)

\chi(\xi)=\mathrm{i}\gamma(1+16\pi^{2}\mu^{2}|\xi|^{2})|\xi|^{-1}\xi_{1}-2\pi(1-\gamma^{2}|\xi|^{-2}\xi_{1}^{2}+4\pi^{2}\sigma^{2}|\xi|^{2})|\xi|,

which obeys the inequalities

(2.1.6)

\gamma(1+16\pi^{2}\mu^{2}|\xi|^{2})|\xi|^{-1}|\xi_{1}|\leqslant|\chi(\xi)|\text{ and }2\pi(1+4\pi^{2}\sigma^{2}|\xi|^{2})|\xi|\leqslant|\chi(\xi)-2\pi\gamma^{2}|\xi|^{-2}\xi_{1}^{2}|\xi||.

Hence,

(2.1.7)

\gamma(1+16\pi^{2}\mu^{2}|\xi|^{2})|\xi|^{-1}|\xi_{1}|+2\pi(1+4\pi^{2}\sigma^{2}|\xi|^{2})|\xi|\leqslant\Big(2+\frac{2\pi\gamma|\xi_{1}|}{1+16\pi^{2}\mu^{2}|\xi|^{2}}\Big)|\chi(\xi)|

and so, for a constant
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
depending on
\gamma
,
\mu
, and
\sigma
, we have the equivalence

(2.1.8)

C^{-1}\langle\xi\rangle^{2}(|\xi_{1}||\xi|^{-1}+|\xi|)\leqslant|\chi(\xi)|\leqslant C\langle\xi\rangle^{2}(|\xi_{1}||\xi|^{-1}+|\xi|).

From this estimate it’s a simple matter to check that the operator
\chi(D):\mathcal{H}^{s+3}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is an isomorphism, and so our proposed definition of
\eta
in (
2.1.4
) is well-defined. Moreover, we have that
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with the estimate
\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{3+s}}\lesssim\lVert\mathcal{R}\cdot\varphi\rVert_{H^{s}}
. To complete the existence proof, we note that
\varphi\in(I-\mathbb{P})H^{s}
results in
\mathcal{R}\mathcal{R}\cdot\varphi=-\varphi
, and hence (
2.1.4
) and the identity
\mathcal{R}\mathcal{R}\cdot\nabla=-\nabla
imply that
\eta
solves (
2.1.3
). Uniqueness follows by noting that if (
2.1.3
) holds with
\varphi=0
, then we may apply
\mathcal{R}
to see that
\chi(D)\eta=0
.
∎

We are now ready to study the linear isomorphism associated with (
2.1.1
).

Theorem 2.3

(Linear well posedness in the case of positive surface tension and viscosity)
.

Given any choice of
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
\gamma,\mu,\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, the mapping

(2.1.9)

L:H^{2+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1+s})(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})

defined via

(2.1.10)

L(v,\eta)=(\nabla\cdot v-\gamma\partial_{1}\eta,-\gamma\partial_{1}v+v-\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}v+(I-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\eta)

is a bounded linear isomorphism.

Proof.

That
L
is well-defined and bounded is clear from the characterizations of the anisotropic Sobolev spaces given in Proposition
A.1
. It remains to establish that
L
is a bijection.

If
L(v,\eta)=0
, then
\eta
satisfies (
2.1.3
) with right hand side identically zero. Hence, by the uniqueness assertion of Proposition
2.2
, we find that
\eta=0
. In turn, the first and second equations in (
2.1.2
) imply that
\mathbb{P}v=(I-\mathbb{P})v=0
. Hence,
(v,\eta)=0
, which demonstrates the injectivity of
L
.

Next, we show that
L
is a surjection. Let
h\in(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1+s})(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
f\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We set
H=\nabla\Delta^{-1}h\in(I-\mathbb{P})H^{2+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
as in Proposition
2.1
and then define

(2.1.11)

\varphi=(I-\mathbb{P})f-(I-\gamma\partial_{1}-4\mu^{2}\Delta)H\in(I-\mathbb{P})H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Then we can construct
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
solving (
2.1.3
) via an application of Proposition
2.2
. In turn, we construct
v\in H^{2+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
via the decomposition
v=\mathbb{P}v+(I-\mathbb{P})v
, where the projections are obtained by solving the equations

(2.1.12)

\begin{cases}(I-\gamma\partial_{1}-\mu^{2}\Delta)\mathbb{P}v=\mathbb{P}f,\\
(I-\mathbb{P})v=H-\gamma\mathcal{R}\mathcal{R}_{1}\eta.\end{cases}

The pair
(v,\eta)\in H^{2+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfy the second item of Proposition
2.1
with the data
f
and
h
. By invoking this equivalence, we find that
L(v,\eta)=(h,f)
.
∎

2.2.
A first pass nonlinear theory

We now port the previous subsection’s linear theory to a nonlinear result via an application of the implicit function theorem. At the end, we shall have Theorem
1
. We begin by checking that the requisite mapping properties of the nonlinear differential operator are satisfied.

Proposition 2.4

(Smoothness of the forward mapping)
.

Given any
\mathbb{N}\ni s>d/2
, the function

(2.2.1)

\Psi:[(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}))^{\ell+1}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{\ell+1}]\times[H^{2+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})]\\
\to(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1+s})(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})

defined via

(2.2.2)

\Psi((\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell}),(v,\eta))\\
=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla\cdot((1+\eta)(v-\gamma e_{1}))\\
(1+\eta)(v-\gamma e_{1})\cdot\nabla v+v-\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot((1+\eta)\mathbb{S}v)+(1+\eta)(1-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\eta+\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\eta^{i}(\tau_{i}\nabla\eta+\varphi_{i})\end{pmatrix}

is smooth.

Proof.

Corollary
A.5
and Proposition
A.23
show that all of the products involved in the definition of
\Psi
are well-defined.
\Psi
is then a polynomial mapping comprised of sums of bounded multilinear maps, and is thus smooth.
∎

The next result is our first version of positive viscosity and positive surface tension well-posedness. This theorem only works perturbatively from a fixed parameter tuple of wave speed, viscosity, and surface tension.

Theorem 2.5

(Well-posedness, I)
.

Let
\mathbb{N}\ni s>d/2
. For each
\mathbf{p}=(\overline{\gamma},\overline{\mu},\overline{\sigma})\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}
there exists
\varepsilon_{s,\mathbf{p}}
,
\varepsilon_{s,\mathbf{p}}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and a unique mapping

(2.2.3)

\upiota_{\mathbf{p}}:B((\mathbf{p},0),\varepsilon_{s,\mathbf{p}})\subset(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}))^{\ell+1}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{\ell+1}\to B(0,\varepsilon_{s,\mathbf{p}}^{\prime})\subset H^{2+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

such that for all
(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell})\in B((\mathbf{p},0),\varepsilon_{s,\mathbf{p}})
if we set

(v,\eta)=\upiota_{\mathbf{p}}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell})\in B(0,\varepsilon_{s,\mathbf{p}}^{\prime})
, then
\Psi(((\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell})),(v,\eta))=0
. Moreover, the map
\upiota_{\mathbf{p}}
is smooth.

Proof.

Thanks to Theorem
2.3
and Proposition
2.4
the hypotheses of the implicit function theorem are satisfied when applied to the map
\Psi
at the point
(\mathbf{p},0)
. We are thus granted a smooth implicit function
\upiota_{\mathbf{p}}
that obeys the stated properties.
∎

We augment Theorem
2.5
with a simple gluing argument that allows us to consider simultaneously all tuples of strictly positive wave speed, viscosity, and surface tension.

Theorem 2.6

(Well-posedness, II)
.

Let
\mathbb{N}\ni s>d/2
. There exists an open set

(2.2.4)

(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}\times\{{0}\}^{\ell+1}\times\{{0}\}^{\ell+1}\subset\mathscr{U}_{s}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{3}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}))^{\ell+1}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{\ell+1}

and a smooth mapping

(2.2.5)

\upiota:\mathscr{U}_{s}\to H^{2+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

such that for all
\mathbf{U}=(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell})\in\mathscr{U}_{s}
we have that
\upiota(\mathbf{U})\in B(0,\varepsilon_{s,\mathbf{p}}^{\prime})
(with
\mathbf{p}=(\gamma,\mu,\sigma)
and
\varepsilon_{s,\mathbf{p}}^{\prime}
as defined in Theorem
2.5
) is the unique solution to
\Psi(\mathbf{U},\upiota(\mathbf{U}))=0
.

Proof.

We set

(2.2.6)

\mathscr{U}_{s}=\bigcup_{\mathbf{p}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{4}}B((\mathbf{p},0),\varepsilon_{s,\mathbf{p}}).

and define
\upiota=\upiota_{\mathbf{p}}
on the set
B((\mathbf{p},0),\varepsilon_{s,\mathbf{p}})
. That this is well-defined and smooth follows from Theorem
2.5
.
∎

2.3.
One dimensional refinements

In this subsection we restrict our attention to system (
1.1.9
) in one spatial dimension. The reason for doing so is that, because the derivative loss is less severe in one dimension, we are able to obtain slightly more refined results with a standard inverse function theorem than what we shall later obtain with a Nash-Moser argument. In this case, we may reformulate the system into one single equation involving the free surface unknown alone. To see how, we note that the first equation in (
1.1.9
) tells us that
\gamma\eta^{\prime}=((1+\eta)v)^{\prime}
. Since we are interested in the class of solitary wave solutions, we require that
\eta
and
v
both vanish at infinity. We also shall be concerned with solutions for which
1+\eta\geqslant 0
. Thus, in this class, we can integrate this identity and obtain the relation

(2.3.1)

v=\gamma\frac{\eta}{1+\eta}.

We can then substitute this into the second equation in (
1.1.9
) and obtain the following equation involving only the free surface:

(2.3.2)

-\gamma^{2}\Big(\frac{\eta}{1+\eta}\Big)^{\prime}+\gamma\frac{\eta}{1+\eta}-4\gamma\mu^{2}\Big((1+\eta)\Big(\frac{\eta}{1+\eta}\Big)^{\prime}\Big)^{\prime}+(1+\eta)(\eta-\sigma^{2}\eta^{\prime\prime})^{\prime}+\mathcal{F}=0.

For the rest of this section we shall consider the wave speed cases of (
2.3.2
) which do not exhibit derivative loss in the limit of
\mu
and
\sigma
tend to zero. As it turns out, this means that we shall only need to avoid the precisely sonic case of
\gamma=1
. Thus, we shall take
\mu,\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
and
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus\{{1}\}
for the remainder of this subsection. This does not mean that we are avoiding the precisely sonic case entirely, indeed in the subsequent sections of the paper where we perform more delicate analysis working towards an application of a Nash-Moser inverse function theorem, the cases absent in this section will be covered.

Our first result studies the mapping properties of the linearization of (
2.3.2
) at the trivial solution.

Proposition 2.7

(One dimensional linear analysis, estimates)
.

Given
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
R\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus\{{1}\}
, there exists a constant
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that whenever
\mu,\sigma\in[-R,R]
,
\psi\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
, and
\eta\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
, satisfy the equation

(2.3.3)

(\gamma(1-4\mu^{2}\partial^{2})+((1-\gamma^{2})-\sigma^{2}\partial^{2})\partial)\eta=\psi,

we have the estimates

(2.3.4)

\begin{cases}\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{1+s}}+\mu^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{2+s}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{3+s}}\leqslant C\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}}&\text{if }\gamma<1,\\
\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{1+s}}+\mu^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{2+s}}\leqslant C\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}}&\text{if }\gamma>1,\;\sigma=0,\\
\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{s}}+\mu^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{2+s}}+(|\mu|+|\sigma|)\sigma^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{3+s}}\leqslant C\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}}&\text{if }\gamma>1,\;|\sigma|+|\mu|>0.\end{cases}

Proof.

We take the Fourier transform of (
2.3.3
) to find the multiplier formulation
m_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}(\xi)\mathscr{F}[\eta](\xi)=\mathscr{F}[\psi](\xi)
for a.e.
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, where

(2.3.5)

m_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}(\xi)=\gamma(1+16\pi^{2}\mu^{2}|\xi|^{2})+2\pi\mathrm{i}\xi((1-\gamma^{2})+4\pi^{2}\sigma^{2}|\xi|^{2}).

By considerations of the real and imaginary parts of
m_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}
, we observe the equivalence

(2.3.6)

|m_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}(\xi)|\asymp 1+\mu^{2}|\xi|^{2}+|\xi|\Big|\frac{1-\gamma^{2}}{4\pi^{2}}+\sigma^{2}|\xi|^{2}\Big|.

If
\gamma<1
, then we evidently have

(2.3.7)

|m_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}(\xi)|\asymp\langle\mu\xi\rangle^{2}+\langle\xi\rangle\langle\sigma\xi\rangle^{2},

and this implies the first case in (
2.3.4
).

On the other hand, if
\gamma>1
and
\sigma=0
, then from (
2.3.6
) we deduce that

(2.3.8)

|m_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}(\xi)|\asymp\langle\mu\xi\rangle^{2}+\langle\xi\rangle

and are thus led to the second case in (
2.3.4
).

Finally, let us assume that
\gamma>1
and
|\mu|+|\sigma|>0
. Then, by breaking to cases, we deduce that

(2.3.9)

|m_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}(\xi)|\gtrsim\langle\mu\xi\rangle^{2}+\langle\xi\rangle\begin{cases}1&\text{if }|\xi|\lesssim|\sigma|^{-1},\\
0&\text{if }|\xi|\asymp|\sigma|^{-1},\\
\langle\sigma\xi\rangle^{2}&\text{if }|\xi|\gtrsim|\sigma|^{-1}.\end{cases}

Then by using that
\langle\sigma\xi\rangle\lesssim 1
for
|\xi|\lesssim|\sigma|^{-1}
, we deduce from the above the lower bounds

(2.3.10)

|m_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}(\xi)|\gtrsim\langle\mu\xi\rangle^{2}\geqslant\langle\mu\xi\rangle\gtrsim\langle\mu\xi\rangle+\langle\sigma\xi\rangle\gtrsim(\langle\mu\xi\rangle+\langle\sigma\xi\rangle)\langle\sigma\xi\rangle^{2}\text{ for }|\xi|\lesssim|\sigma|^{-1}

and

(2.3.11)

|m_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}(\xi)|\gtrsim\langle\xi\rangle\langle\sigma\xi\rangle^{2}\gtrsim(\langle\mu\xi\rangle+\langle\sigma\xi\rangle)\langle\sigma\xi\rangle^{2},\text{ for }|\xi|\gtrsim|\sigma|^{-1}.

Combining (
2.3.10
) and (
2.3.11
) with
|m_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}(\xi)|\gtrsim\langle\mu\xi\rangle^{2}
then reveals that

(2.3.12)

|m_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}(\xi)|\gtrsim\langle\mu\xi\rangle^{2}+\langle\sigma\xi\rangle^{2}(\langle\mu\xi\rangle+\langle\sigma\xi\rangle),

and hence the final case in (
2.3.4
) is shown.
∎

Motivated by the previous result, we make the following definition.

Definition 2.8

(Reparameterization operators and cases for the parameters)
.

We make the following definitions.

(1)

Given
\gamma,\mu,\sigma\in(\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus\{{1}\})\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
i\in\{{1,2,3}\}
, we define the Fourier multiplier operator
P^{i}_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}=p^{i}_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}(\partial/2\pi\mathrm{i})
for

(2.3.13)

p^{i}_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}(\xi)=\langle\mu\xi\rangle^{2}+\begin{cases}\langle\xi\rangle\langle\sigma\xi\rangle^{2}&\text{if }i=1\\
\langle\xi\rangle&\text{if }i=2\\
(\langle\sigma\xi\rangle+\langle\mu\xi\rangle)\langle\sigma\xi\rangle^{2}&\text{if }i=3.\end{cases}

(2)

We define the sets
\mathcal{P}_{i}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus\{{1}\})\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
via

(2.3.14)

\mathcal{P}_{i}=\begin{cases}\{{(\gamma,\mu,\sigma)\;:\;\gamma<1}\}&\text{if }i=1,\\
\{{(\gamma,\mu,\sigma)\;:\;\gamma>1,\;\sigma=0}\}&\text{if }i=2,\\
\{{(\gamma,\mu,\sigma)\;:\;\gamma>1,\;|\mu|+|\sigma|>0}\}&\text{if }i=3.\end{cases}

Notice that
\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}\cup\mathcal{P}_{3}=(\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus\{{1}\})\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, but
\mathcal{P}_{2}\cap\mathcal{P}_{3}\neq\varnothing
.

By combining the previous result and definition, we are led to the following corollary.

Corollary 2.9

(One dimensional linear analysis, isomorphisms)
.

For any choice of
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\setminus\{{1}\}
,
R\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and
\mu,\sigma\in[-R,R]
, the following hold.

(1)

For
i\in\{{1,2,3}\}
and
(\gamma,\mu,\sigma)\in\mathcal{P}_{i}\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[-R,R]^{2})
the maps

(2.3.15)

A_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}^{i}=(\gamma(1-4\mu^{2}\partial^{2})+((1-\gamma^{2})-\sigma^{2}\partial^{2})\partial)\circ(P_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}^{i})^{-1}:H^{s}(\mathbb{R})\to H^{s}(\mathbb{R})

are bounded linear isomorphisms.

(2)

The maps in (
2.3.15
) satisfy the bounds

(2.3.16)

\lVert(A^{i}_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma})^{-1}\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(H^{s})}\leqslant C

and

(2.3.17)

\lVert A_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}^{i}\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(H^{s})}\leqslant C\begin{cases}1&\text{if }i=1,\\
1&\text{if }i=2,\\
(|\mu|+|\sigma|)^{-1}&\text{if }i=3,\end{cases}

for a constant
C
depending only on
\gamma
,
R
, and
s
.

Proof.

A^{i}_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}=a^{i}_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}(\partial/2\pi\mathrm{i})
is a translation-commuting linear operator with symbol

(2.3.18)

a^{i}_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}(\xi)=\frac{m_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}(\xi)}{p^{i}_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}(\xi)},

where the numerator is defined by (
2.3.5
) and the denominator is given in Definition
2.8
. Hence, the first and second items will follow immediately if we can establish the symbol estimates

(2.3.19)

\Big|\frac{m_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}(\xi)}{p^{i}_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}(\xi)}\Big|\lesssim\begin{cases}1&\text{if }i=1,\\
1&\text{if }i=2,\\
(|\mu|+|\sigma|)^{-1}&\text{if }i=3,\end{cases}\text{ and }\Big|\frac{p^{i}_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}(\xi)}{m_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}(\xi)}\Big|\lesssim 1.

The former follows from a simple calculation, while the latter is enforced by Proposition
2.7
and Definition
2.8
.
∎

To complete this subsection, we now consider the one dimensional nonlinear analysis. The following property of the reparameterization operators will be implicitly used numerous times in the proof of Proposition
2.11
below.

Lemma 2.10

(Facts about reparameterization operators)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\{{1,2,3}\}
. The following mappings are continuous.

(2.3.20)

\mathcal{P}_{i}\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R})\ni(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\eta)\mapsto\begin{cases}(P_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}^{i})^{-1}\eta\in H^{1+s}(\mathbb{R}),\\
\mu^{2}(P_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}^{i})^{-1}\eta\in H^{2+s}(\mathbb{R}),\\
\sigma^{2}(P_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}^{i})^{-1}\eta\in H^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}),\end{cases}

where we recall that the sets
\mathcal{P}_{i}
and the operators
P^{i}_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}
are given in Definition
2.8
.

Proof.

The assertions of the Lemma are equivalent to proving that the following maps are continuous. Fix
R\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and define

(2.3.21)

((-R,R)^{3}\cap\mathcal{P}_{i})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R})\ni(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\eta)\mapsto B(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\eta)\\
=(\langle\partial\rangle(P^{i}_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma})^{-1}\eta,\mu^{2}\langle\partial\rangle^{2}(P^{i}_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma})^{-1}\eta,\sigma^{2}\langle\partial\rangle^{3}(P^{i}_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma})^{-1}\eta)\in(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))^{3}.

This is slightly subtle, since
B
is not actually differentiable or even locally Lipschitz. To verify continuity, we first note that for fixed
(\gamma,\mu,\sigma)\in(-R,R)^{3}\cap\mathcal{P}_{i}
the linear mapping

(2.3.22)

H^{s}(\mathbb{R})\ni\eta\mapsto B(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\eta)\in(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))^{3}

is bounded with an operator norm depending only on
s
and
R
. The effect of this is that it suffices to show that for each fixed
\eta\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
the mapping

(2.3.23)

(-R,R)^{3}\cap\mathcal{P}_{i}\ni(\gamma,\mu,\sigma)\mapsto B(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\eta)\in(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))^{3}

is continuous. To verify this claim, we shall consider the following difference decomposition

(2.3.24)

B(\gamma_{0}+\gamma,\mu_{0}+\mu,\sigma_{0}+\sigma,\eta)-B(\gamma_{0},\mu_{0},\sigma_{0},\eta)=B_{1}+B_{2}+B_{3},

where

(2.3.25)

B_{j}=\begin{cases}B(\gamma_{0}+\gamma,\mu_{0}+\mu,\sigma_{0}+\sigma,\eta)-B(\gamma_{0}+\gamma,\mu_{0}+\mu,\sigma_{0}+\sigma,\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}\eta)&\text{if }j=1,\\
B(\gamma_{0}+\gamma,\mu_{0}+\mu,\sigma_{0}+\sigma,\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}\eta)-B(\gamma_{0},\mu_{0},\sigma_{0},\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}\eta)&\text{if }j=2,\\
B(\gamma_{0},\mu_{0},\sigma_{0},\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}\eta)-B(\gamma_{0},\mu_{0},\sigma_{0},\eta)&\text{if }j=3,\end{cases}

for
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
a small mollification parameter,
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}
a mollification operator,
(\gamma_{0},\mu_{0},\sigma_{0})
and
(\gamma_{0}+\gamma,\mu_{0}+\mu,\sigma_{0}+\sigma)
belonging to
(-R,R)^{3}\cap\mathcal{P}_{i}
, and
\eta\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
. For
B_{1}
and
B_{3}
, we simply use linearity in the final argument of
B
:

(2.3.26)

\lVert B_{1}\rVert_{H^{s}}+\lVert B_{3}\rVert_{H^{s}}\lesssim_{R,s}\lVert(I-\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon})\eta\rVert_{H^{s}}.

On the other hand, for
B_{2}
, we instead leverage the smoothness of
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}
and use that
B(\cdot,\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}\eta)
is locally Lipschitz if you pay for more derivatives on
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}\eta
. This results in the bound

(2.3.27)

\lVert B\rVert_{2}\lesssim_{\varepsilon,R,s}\lVert\gamma,\mu,\sigma\rVert_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{s}}.

We combine (
2.3.26
) with (
2.3.24
). By selecting
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
sufficiently small depending on
\eta
, and then choosing
\lVert\gamma,\mu,\sigma\rVert_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
sufficiently small depending on
\varepsilon
and
\eta
, we can make the difference (
2.3.24
) as measured in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
smaller than any desired positive value. So it is shown that the mapping of (
2.3.23
) is continuous, and thus the proof is complete.
∎

The following result is meant to capture the nonlinear mapping properties of an operator derived from the one dimensional equation (
2.3.2
). This will subsequently be paired with the linear estimates and another inverse function theorem argument.

Proposition 2.11

(On the one dimensional nonlinear maps)
.

The following hold for
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 1
.

(1)

There exists
\uprho\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that for all
\eta\in B_{H^{1}}(0,\uprho)
we have
\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant 1/2
and the map

(2.3.28)

B_{H^{1}}(0,\uprho)\cap H^{s}(\mathbb{R})\ni\eta\mapsto\frac{\eta}{1+\eta}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
