is smooth. Moreover, we have the estimate

(2.3.29)

\Big\lVert\frac{\eta}{1+\eta}\Big\rVert_{H^{s}}\leqslant C\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{s}},

for a constant
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
depending only on
s
.

(2)

With
\uprho\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
as in the previous item and
i\in\{{1,2,3}\}
, the maps
\mathcal{N}^{i}:\mathcal{P}_{i}\times B_{H^{s}}(0,\uprho)\to H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
given by

(2.3.30)

\mathcal{N}^{i}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\eta)=-\gamma^{2}\Big(\frac{(P_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}^{i})^{-1}\eta}{1+(P_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}^{i})^{-1}\eta}\Big)^{\prime}+\gamma\frac{(P_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}^{i})^{-1}\eta}{1+(P_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}^{i})^{-1}\eta}-4\gamma\mu^{2}\Big((1+(P_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}^{i})^{-1}\eta)\Big(\frac{(P_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}^{i})^{-1}\eta}{1+(P_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}^{i})^{-1}\eta}\Big)^{\prime}\Big)^{\prime}\\
+(1+(P_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}^{i})^{-1}\eta)((P_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}^{i})^{-1}\eta-\sigma^{2}(P_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}^{i})^{-1}\eta^{\prime\prime})^{\prime}

are well-defined, continuous, differentiable with respect to the final factor with continuous partial derivative.

(3)

The maps
\mathcal{O}^{i}:\mathcal{P}_{i}\times B_{H^{s}}(0,\uprho)\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))^{\ell+1}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))^{\ell+1}\to H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
given by

(2.3.31)

\mathcal{O}^{i}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\eta,\{{\tau_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\ell})=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}(P_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}^{i})^{-1}\eta)^{j}(\tau_{j}\nabla((P_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}^{i})^{-1}\eta)+\varphi_{j})

are continuous, and continuously partially differentiable with respect to the final two factors.

Proof.

The existence of
\uprho\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
as in the first item follows from the embedding
H^{1}(\mathbb{R})\hookrightarrow C^{0}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
along with Theorem D.3 and Corollary D.9 from Stevenson and Tice
[
56
]
.

We next prove the second item. First, we fix a parameter
R\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and consider the auxiliary mapping

(2.3.32)

[-R,R]^{3}\times B_{H^{s+3}}(0,\uprho)\ni(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\zeta)\mapsto\mathcal{M}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\zeta)=-\gamma^{2}\Big(\frac{\zeta}{1+\zeta}\Big)^{\prime}\\
+\gamma\frac{\zeta}{1+\zeta}-4\gamma\mu^{2}\Big((1+\zeta)\Big(\frac{\zeta}{1+\zeta}\Big)^{\prime}\Big)^{\prime}+(1+\zeta)(\zeta-\sigma^{2}\zeta^{\prime\prime})^{\prime}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}).

Thanks to repeated applications of the estimate from the first item, Proposition
A.23
, and simple interpolation inequalities we learn that for
s\geqslant 1

\mathcal{M}
obeys the bounds

(2.3.33)

\lVert\mathcal{M}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\eta)\rVert_{H^{s}}\leqslant C_{s,\uprho,R}(\lVert\zeta\rVert_{H^{s+1}}+\mu^{2}\lVert\zeta\rVert_{H^{2+s}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\zeta\rVert_{H^{3+s}})(1+\lVert\zeta\rVert_{H^{1}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\zeta\rVert_{H^{3}}).

Now we can pair estimate (
2.3.33
) with the relation

(2.3.34)

\mathcal{M}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,(P_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}^{i})^{-1}\eta)=\mathcal{N}^{i}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\eta)\text{ for }i\in\{{1,2,3}\},\;(\gamma,\mu,\sigma)\in\mathcal{P}_{i}

and Lemma
2.10
to deduce the bound

(2.3.35)

\lVert\mathcal{N}^{i}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\eta)\rVert_{H^{s}}\leqslant C_{s,\uprho,R}\begin{cases}\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{s}}&\text{if }i\in\{{1,2}\},\\
(|\mu|+|\sigma|)^{-1}\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{s}}&\text{if }i=3.\end{cases}

Since
R\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
was arbitrary, this shows that
\mathcal{N}^{i}
is well-defined.

Next, we sketch the argument that the
\mathcal{N}^{i}
are continuous. We begin by decomposing the difference

(2.3.36)

\mathcal{N}^{i}(\gamma_{0},\mu_{0},\sigma_{0},\eta_{0})-\mathcal{N}^{i}(\gamma_{1},\mu_{1},\sigma_{1},\eta_{1})=\mathbf{I}_{1}^{i}+\mathbf{I}_{2}^{i}+\mathbf{I}_{3}^{i},

where

(2.3.37)

\mathbf{I}_{j}^{i}=\begin{cases}\mathcal{N}^{i}(\gamma_{0},\mu_{0},\sigma_{0},\eta_{0})-\mathcal{N}^{i}(\gamma_{0},\mu_{0},\sigma_{0},\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}\eta_{0})&\text{if }j=1,\\
\mathcal{N}^{i}(\gamma_{0},\mu_{0},\sigma_{0},\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}\eta_{0})-\mathcal{N}^{i}(\gamma_{1},\mu_{1},\sigma_{1},\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}\eta_{1})&\text{if }j=2,\\
\mathcal{N}^{i}(\gamma_{1},\mu_{1},\sigma_{1},\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}\eta_{1})-\mathcal{N}^{i}(\gamma_{1},\mu_{1},\sigma_{1},\eta_{1})&\text{if }j=3\end{cases}

for
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
a small mollification parameter and
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}
a mollification operator. The terms
\mathbf{I}_{1}^{i}
and
\mathbf{I}_{3}^{i}
are handled by first considering the corresponding differences for the operator
\mathcal{M}
. By arguing as above, we may deduce that

(2.3.38)

\lVert\mathcal{M}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\zeta_{0})-\mathcal{M}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\zeta_{1})\rVert_{H^{s}}\leqslant C_{\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1}}(\lVert\zeta_{0}-\zeta_{1}\rVert_{H^{1+s}}+\mu^{2}\lVert\zeta_{0}-\zeta_{1}\rVert_{H^{2+s}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\zeta_{0}-\zeta_{1}\rVert_{H^{3+s}}),

for some constant
C_{\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1}}
that is a function of
R
,
\lVert\zeta_{k}\rVert_{H^{1+s}}
,
\mu^{2}\lVert\zeta_{k}\rVert_{H^{2+s}}
, and
\sigma^{2}\lVert\zeta_{k}\rVert_{H^{3+s}}
for
k\in\{{0,1}\}
. Recalling the identity (
2.3.34
) and Lemma
2.10
, we then learn that

(2.3.39)

\lVert\mathbf{I}_{1}^{i}\rVert_{H^{s}}+\lVert\mathbf{I}_{3}^{i}\rVert_{H^{s}}\leqslant\widetilde{C}(\lVert(1-\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon})\eta_{0}\rVert_{H^{s}}+\lVert(1-\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon})\eta_{1}\rVert_{H^{s}})\begin{cases}1&\text{if }i\in\{{1,2}\},\\
(|\mu|+|\sigma|)^{-1}&\text{if }i=3,\end{cases}\\
\leqslant\widetilde{C}(\lVert\eta_{0}-\eta_{1}\rVert_{H^{s}}+\lVert(1-\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon})\eta_{0}\rVert_{H^{s}})\begin{cases}1&\text{if }i\in\{{1,2}\},\\
(|\mu|+|\sigma|)^{-1}&\text{if }i=3,\end{cases}

where
\widetilde{C}
is a function of
R
,
\uprho
, and
s
.

On the other hand, for the
\mathbf{I}_{2}
term, we leverage the smoothness
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}\eta_{0}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}\eta_{1}
in order to invoke a cheap bound that spends three derivatives (
\mathcal{M}
is locally Lipschitz if you pay for three derivatives) at the expense of a bad constant depending on
\varepsilon
:

(2.3.40)

\lVert\mathbf{I}_{2}^{i}\rVert_{H^{s}}\lesssim_{R,s,\uprho}\left\lVert\gamma_{0}-\gamma_{1},\mu_{0}-\mu_{1},\sigma_{0}-\sigma_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}+\lVert\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}(\eta_{0}-\eta_{1})\rVert_{H^{3+s}}\\
\lesssim_{R,s,\uprho}\left\lVert\gamma_{0}-\gamma_{1},\mu_{0}-\mu_{1},\sigma_{0}-\sigma_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}+C_{\varepsilon}\lVert\eta_{0}-\eta_{1}\rVert_{H^{s}}.

The difference in (
2.3.36
) can then be made arbitrarily small by first choosing
\varepsilon
depending on
(\gamma_{0},\mu_{0},\sigma_{0},\eta_{0})
and then selecting
(\gamma_{1},\mu_{1},\sigma_{1},\eta_{1})
sufficiently near the former tuple.

The previous argument can be readily adapted to show that the maps
\mathcal{N}^{i}
are continuously partially differentiable with respect to the second factor. This finishes the proof of the second item. The third item is proved in a similar fashion to the second item. We omit the details for brevity.
∎

Once more, we will connect the linear and nonlinear theories via an application of the inverse function theorem. For our purposes here, we need a slightly specialized version of this result. We shall use the following version of the inverse function theorem, as formulated in Theorem A in Crandall and Rabinowitz
[
19
]
(for a verbose proof see Theorem 2.7.2 in Nirenberg
[
48
]
, but note that there is slight misstatement of the uniqueness assertion in the first item there that is correct in
[
19
]
).

Theorem 2.12

(Implicit function theorem)
.

Let
X,Y,Z
be Banach spaces over the same field, and let
U\subset X\times Y
be open. Suppose that
f:U\to Z
is continuous and that
f
has a Fréchet derivative with respect to the first factor,
D_{1}f:U\to\mathcal{L}(X;Z)
, that is also continuous. Further suppose that
(x_{0},y_{0})\in U
and
f(x_{0},y_{0})=0
. If
D_{1}f(x_{0},y_{0})
is an isomorphism of
X
onto
Z
, then there exist balls
B(y_{0},r_{Y})\subset Y
and
B(x_{0},r_{X})\subset X
such that
B(x_{0},r_{X})\times B(y_{0},r_{Y})\subset U
and a continuous function
u:B(y_{0},r_{Y})\to B(x_{0},r_{X})
such that
u(y_{0})=x_{0}
and
f(u(y),y)=0
for all
y\in B(y_{0},r_{Y})
. Moreover, the implicit function
u
is continuous.

We now state our results for (
2.3.2
) in an abstract formulation. Afterward, we will unpack things to reveal a PDE result.

Theorem 2.13

(One dimensional analysis, abstract formulation)
.

Let
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 1
. For
i\in\{{1,2,3}\}
there exists open sets

(2.3.41)

\mathcal{P}_{i}\times\{{0}\}^{\ell+1}\times\{{0}\}^{\ell+1}\subset\mathscr{U}_{s}^{i}\subset\mathcal{P}_{i}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))^{\ell+1}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))^{\ell+1},

radii
\{{\varepsilon^{i}_{s,\mathbf{p}}}\}_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}_{i}}\subset(0,\uprho]
, where
\uprho\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
is from the first item of Proposition
2.11
, and continuous mappings

(2.3.42)

\upiota^{i}:\mathscr{U}^{i}_{s}\to\bigcup_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}_{i}}B_{H^{s}}(0,\varepsilon^{i}_{s,\mathbf{p}})

with the property that for all
\mathbf{U}=(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\ell})\in\mathscr{U}^{i}_{s}
we have that
\eta=\upiota^{i}(\mathbf{U})\in B_{H^{s}}(0,\varepsilon^{i}_{s,\mathbf{p}})
(with
\mathbf{p}=(\gamma,\mu,\sigma)
is the unique solution to

(2.3.43)

\mathscr{N}^{i}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\eta)+\mathcal{O}^{i}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\eta,\{{\tau_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\ell})=0.

Proof.

The strategy of the proof here is much the same as that of Theorems
2.5
and
2.6
; as such, we only give a sketch of the argument. For the nonlinear mapping

(2.3.44)

\mathcal{P}_{i}\times B_{H^{s}}(0,\uprho)\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))^{\ell+1}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))^{\ell+1}\ni(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\eta,\{{\tau_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\ell})\\
\mapsto\mathscr{N}^{i}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\eta)+\mathcal{O}^{i}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\eta,\{{\tau_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\ell})\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R})

we aim to satisfy the hypotheses of the implicit function theorem at a point
(\mathbf{p},0,0,0)
for a fixed
\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}_{i}
. That the mapping in (
2.3.44
) is continuously partially differentiable with respect to the second factor is ensured by Proposition
2.11
. The partial derivatives obey the requisite mapping properties in light of Corollary
2.9
. In this way we obtain local continuous implicit functions
\upiota^{i}_{\mathbf{p}}
that are defined in a small neighborhood of
(\mathbf{p},0,0,0)
. We then glue these maps together, via local uniqueness, and obtain an implicit function
\upiota^{i}
defined on the union of these small neighborhoods.
∎

The next result is the PDE formulation of the previous abstract form.

Theorem 2.14

(One dimensional analysis, PDE formulation)
.

Let
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 1
,
i\in\{{1,2,3}\}
, and
\mathscr{U}_{s}^{i}
be the open set granted by Theorem
2.13
. For
(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{\ell})\in\mathscr{U}^{i}_{s}
we set

(2.3.45)

\eta=(P_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}^{i})^{-1}[\upiota^{i}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{\ell})],\hskip 10.00002ptv=\gamma\frac{\eta}{1+\eta},\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{O}^{i}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,P^{i}_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}\eta,\{{\tau_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\ell}).

The following hold.

(1)

System (
1.1.9
) is satisfied by
(v,\eta)
with
\mathcal{F}
as a forcing term.

(2)

The following mappings are well-defined and continuous:

(2.3.46)

\mathscr{U}^{i}_{s}\ni(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{\ell})\mapsto\begin{cases}(v,\eta)\in(H^{1+s}(\mathbb{R}))^{2},\\
\ \mu^{2}(v,\eta)\in(H^{2+s}(\mathbb{R}))^{2},\\
\sigma^{2}(v,\eta)\in(H^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}))^{2}.\end{cases}

Proof.

We define
\eta
,
v
, and
\mathcal{F}
as in (
2.3.45
). Thanks to the first item of Proposition
2.11
, this definition of
v
makes sense and is a smooth function of
\eta
in matching Sobolev spaces. The first item is then immediate from the derivation of (
2.3.2
) from (
1.1.9
). The continuity of the mappings in the second item are similarly clear thanks to properties of the reparameterization operators
P^{i}_{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}
from Definition
2.8
and Lemma
2.10
.
∎

3.
Nonlinear analysis of the shallow water system

We now aim to understand the behavior of the system (
1.1.9
) in a regime larger than that covered by Section
2
. To do so, we must face the problem of derivative loss. We are thus lead to replace the implicit function theorem strategy of Section
2
with a Nash-Moser inverse function theorem. The version which we elect to use is the one carefully stated in Appendix
A.3
.

The purpose of this section is to both handle the nonlinear hypotheses of the Nash-Moser theorem and to lay the groundwork for the verification of the linear hypotheses. In Section
3.1
we introduce the scales of Banach spaces and parameter dependent norms in play for our Nash-Moser and estimates framework. Next, in Section
3.2
, we associate to (
1.1.9
) some nonlinear differential operators and then verify that these satisfy certain smoothness and tameness assertions. Finally, in Section
3.3
, we take a look at certain derivatives of the previously introduced nonlinear maps and isolate some minimal essential structure.

3.1.
Banach scales for the traveling wave problem

The goal of this subsection is to define the scales of Banach spaces that are relevant for our analysis of the traveling shallow water equations (
1.1.9
) and then to verify basic properties.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
. First, we introduce spaces that will play a role in the domain of our nonlinear map. We write

(3.1.1)

\mathbb{X}_{s}=H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\text{ with norm }\lVert v,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}=(\lVert v\rVert_{H^{s}}^{2}+\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}^{2})^{1/2}

to be our omnisonic container space. We then write

(3.1.2)

\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s}=H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{1+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\text{ with norm }\lVert v,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s}}=(\lVert v\rVert_{H^{s}}^{2}+\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1+s}}^{2})^{1/2}

to denote our subsonic container space.

Next, we introduce spaces that will play a role in the codomain of the nonlinear map.

(3.1.3)

\mathbb{Y}_{s}=(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1+s})(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\text{ with norm }\lVert h,f\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{s}}=([{h}]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}^{2}+\lVert h\rVert_{H^{1+s}}^{2}+\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}}^{2})^{1/2}.

We then give the data spaces

(3.1.4)

\mathbb{W}_{s}=(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}))^{\ell+1}\times(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{\ell+1}\text{ with norm }\lVert\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell}\rVert_{\mathbb{W}_{s}}=\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\big(\lVert\tau_{i}\rVert_{H^{s}}^{2}+\lVert\varphi_{i}\rVert^{2}_{H^{s}}\big)\Big)^{1/2}.

We synthesize the above into the domain and codomain of the nonlinear mapping that we shall associate with (
1.1.9
) in the next subsection. First we have the omnisonic domain and codomain:

(3.1.5)

\mathbb{E}_{s}=\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{W}_{s}\times\mathbb{X}_{s}\times\mathbb{X}_{s}\text{ and }\mathbb{F}_{s}=\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{Y}_{s}\times\mathbb{W}_{s}\times\mathbb{X}_{s}.

Next we have the subsonic domain and codomain:

(3.1.6)

\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s}=\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{W}_{s}\times\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s}\times\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s}\text{ and }\mathbb{F}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s}=\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{Y}_{s}\times\mathbb{W}_{s}\times\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s}.

From these spaces we now denote the following Banach scales:

(3.1.7)

\boldsymbol{\mathbb{X}}=\{{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}},\hskip 10.00002pt\boldsymbol{\mathbb{Y}}=\{{\mathbb{Y}_{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}},\hskip 10.00002pt\boldsymbol{\mathbb{W}}=\{{\mathbb{W}_{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}},\hskip 10.00002pt\boldsymbol{\mathbb{E}}=\{{\mathbb{E}_{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}},\hskip 10.00002pt\boldsymbol{\mathbb{F}}=\{{\mathbb{F}_{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}},

and

(3.1.8)

\boldsymbol{\mathbb{X}}^{\mathrm{sub}}=\{{\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}},\hskip 10.00002pt\boldsymbol{\mathbb{E}}^{\mathrm{sub}}=\{{\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}},\hskip 10.00002pt\boldsymbol{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathrm{sub}}=\{{\mathbb{F}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}.

We have the following result which says that the scales (
3.1.7
) our admissible for our Nash-Moser framework (of Appendix
A.3
).

Lemma 3.1

(LP-smoothability of the Banach scales)
.

Each of the Banach scales
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{X}}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{X}}^{\mathrm{sub}}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{Y}}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{W}}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{E}}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{E}}^{\mathrm{sub}}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{F}}
, and
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathrm{sub}}
is LP-smoothable in the sense of Definition
A.15
.

Proof.

As the product of LP-smoothable scales is again LP-smoothable (see Lemma 2.17 in Stevenson and Tice
[
56
]
), it is sufficient to check that each of the Banach scales
\mathbb{R}
,
\{{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{\ell})}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\{{\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\}
, and
\{{(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{s})(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
is LP-smoothable. Each of these is LP-smoothable as a consequence of Examples
A.16
and
A.17
, along with Proposition
A.2
.
∎

We shall also need norms (and spaces) that measure appropriately the behavior in the limit as
\mu\to 0
or
\sigma\to 0
; thus we make the following definitions.

Definition 3.2

(Parameter dependent norms and spaces)
.

Given
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mu,\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
we make the following definitions of extended norms:

(3.1.9)

{{}_{\mu}}\lVert v\rVert_{H^{s}}=(\lVert v\rVert_{H^{s}}^{2}+\mu^{4}\lVert v\rVert^{2}_{H^{2+s}})^{1/2},\hskip 10.00002pt{{}_{\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}=(\lVert\eta\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}+\sigma^{4}\lVert\eta\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2+s}})^{1/2},

and

(3.1.10)

{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}=(\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}^{2}+\sigma^{4}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{2+s}}^{2}+(\mu^{2}+\sigma^{2})(\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1+s}}^{2}+\sigma^{4}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{3+s}}^{2}))^{1/2},

where
v\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We then make the following definitions of parameter dependent spaces.

(3.1.11)

{{}_{\mu}}H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})=\{{v\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\;:\;{{}_{\mu}}\lVert v\rVert_{H^{s}}<\infty}\},\hskip 10.00002pt{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\{{\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\;:\;{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}<\infty}\},

(3.1.12)

{{}_{\sigma}}\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\{{\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\;:\;{{}_{\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}<\infty}\},

and

(3.1.13)

{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\mathbb{X}_{s}=({{}_{\mu}}H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times({{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\hskip 10.00002pt{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s}=({{}_{\mu}}H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times({{}_{\sigma}}\mathcal{H}^{1+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})).

Remark 3.3
.

We have the following equivalences of Hilbert spaces:

(3.1.14)

{{}_{\mu}}H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})=\begin{cases}H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})&\text{if }\mu=0,\\
H^{2+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})&\text{if }\mu\neq 0,\end{cases}\hskip 10.00002pt{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\begin{cases}\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})&\text{if }\mu=\sigma=0,\\
\mathcal{H}^{1+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})&\text{if }\mu\neq 0,\;\sigma=0,\\
\mathcal{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})&\text{if }\sigma\neq 0,\end{cases}

and

(3.1.15)

{{}_{\sigma}}\mathcal{H}^{1+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\begin{cases}\mathcal{H}^{1+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})&\text{if }\sigma=0,\\
\mathcal{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})&\text{if }\sigma\neq 0.\end{cases}

3.2.
Smooth-tameness of the nonlinear operator

In this part of the document we associate to system (
1.1.9
) nonlinear operators encoding, in particular, the forward mapping of the PDE. We then verify that these operators obey the requisite smoothness and tameness assumptions required by our Nash-Moser scheme. It turns out that this latter task is delightfully easy due to the simple structure of each constituent nonlinearity in the maps defined below.

We shall define two distinct nonlinear operators; the first is meant for a wave speed agnostic analysis that in the end misses optimal derivative counting in the ‘subsonic regime’ while the second is adapted to the case of subsonic wave speed.

Definition 3.4

(Nonlinear maps, omnisonic case)
.

We associate to the system (
1.1.9
) the following operators, which are defined for
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant\max\{{3,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\}
. First, we have
\Uppsi:\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{X}_{s+3}\to\mathbb{Y}_{s}
defined by

(3.2.1)

\Uppsi(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,v,\eta)=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla\cdot((1+\eta)(v-\gamma e_{1}))\\
(1+\eta)(v-\gamma e_{1})\cdot\nabla v+v-\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot((1+\eta)\mathbb{S}v)+(1+\eta)(1-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\eta\end{pmatrix}.

Second, we have
\Upphi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{W}_{s}\times\mathbb{X}_{s+1}\to\mathbb{Y}_{s}
defined by

(3.2.2)

\Upphi(\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},v,\eta)=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\eta^{i}(0,\mathrm{Tr}\tau_{i}\nabla\eta+(\mu+\sigma)\tau_{i}\nabla\eta+\varphi_{i}).

Next, we define
\Upupsilon:\mathbb{R}^{3}\times(\mathbb{X}_{s+3}\times\mathbb{W}_{s})\to\mathbb{Y}_{s}\times\mathbb{W}_{s}
via

(3.2.3)

\Upupsilon(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,v,\eta,\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell})=\big(\Uppsi(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,v,\eta)+\Upphi(\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},v,\eta),\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell}\big).

Finally, we set
\overline{\Uppsi}:\mathbb{E}_{s+3}\to\mathbb{F}_{s}
to be the map

(3.2.4)

\overline{\Uppsi}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},w,\xi,v,\eta)=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma,\mu,\sigma\\
\Upupsilon(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,v,\eta,\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell})\\
\langle\mu\nabla\rangle^{2}v-w\\
(1+(\mu+\sigma)|\nabla|)\langle\sigma\nabla\rangle^{2}\eta-\xi\end{pmatrix}.

Definition 3.5

(Nonlinear maps, subsonic case)
.

Let
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant\max\{{2,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\}
. We note that
\Uppsi:\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s+2}\to\mathbb{Y}_{s}
. In the subsonic case we also associate to system (
1.1.9
) the following additional operators. First we define
\Upphi^{\mathrm{sub}}:\mathbb{W}_{s}\times\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s}\to\mathbb{Y}_{s}
via

(3.2.5)

\Upphi^{\mathrm{sub}}(\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},v,\eta)=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\eta^{i}(0,\tau_{i}\nabla\eta+\varphi_{i}).

Next we define
\Upupsilon^{\mathrm{sub}}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\times(\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s+2}\times\mathbb{W}_{s})\to\mathbb{Y}_{s}\times\mathbb{W}_{s}
via

(3.2.6)

\Upupsilon^{\mathrm{sub}}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,v,\eta,\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell})=(\Uppsi(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,v,\eta)+\Upphi^{\mathrm{sub}}(\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},v,\eta),\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell}).

Finally we set
\overline{\Uppsi}^{\mathrm{sub}}:\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s+2}\to\mathbb{F}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s}
to be the map

(3.2.7)

\overline{\Uppsi}^{\mathrm{sub}}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},w,\xi,v,\eta)=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma,\mu,\sigma\\
\Upupsilon^{\mathrm{sub}}(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,v,\eta,\{{\tau_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell},\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{\ell})\\
\langle\mu\nabla\rangle^{2}v-w\\
\langle\sigma\nabla\rangle^{2}\eta-\xi\end{pmatrix}.

We shall use the Banach scales notation of (
A.4.1
) from Appendix
A.4
. The remaining goals of this subsection are to verify the smooth-tameness of the maps
\overline{\Uppsi}
and
\overline{\Uppsi}^{\mathrm{sub}}
.

Theorem 3.6

(Smooth-tameness)
.

We have that

(1)

\overline{\Uppsi}\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{3,\max\{{3,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\}}(\boldsymbol{\mathbb{E}};\boldsymbol{\mathbb{F}})
,

(2)

\overline{\Uppsi}^{\mathrm{sub}}\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{2,\max\{{2,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\}}(\boldsymbol{\mathbb{E}}^{\mathrm{sub}};\boldsymbol{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathrm{sub}})
.

Proof.

We only prove the first item, as the second item follows from a nearly identical argument. We begin by proving that
\Uppsi\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{3,\max\{{3,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\}}(\mathbb{R}^{4}\times\boldsymbol{\mathbb{X}};\boldsymbol{\mathbb{Y}})
. It suffices to show that each of the multilinear maps that comprise
\Uppsi
is strongly
3-
tame with base
\max\{{3,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\}
, but due to Remark
A.11
we may reduce to proving
3-
tameness. In handling each of these maps we will repeatedly use our results on the preservation of tameness under compositions and products, namely Proposition
A.13
and Corollary
A.14
, without explicit reference.

We first establish that the map

(3.2.8)

\mathbf{H}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\bm{\mathcal{H}}\ni(v,\eta)\mapsto\nabla\cdot((1+\eta)(v-\gamma e_{1}))=-\gamma\partial_{1}\eta+\nabla\cdot v+\nabla\cdot(\eta v)\in\dot{H}^{-1}\cap\mathbf{H}(\mathbb{R})

is
T^{\infty}_{1,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}
by examining each of the three terms in the sum. The map
\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\ni\eta\mapsto\partial_{1}\eta\in(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{s-1})(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is bounded and linear thanks to Proposition
A.1
, while the map
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\ni v\mapsto\nabla\cdot v\in(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{s-1})(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is clearly bounded and linear as well. The bilinear map
(v,\eta)\mapsto\nabla\cdot(v\eta)
is
T^{\infty}_{1,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}
thanks to what we’ve just established and Lemma
A.22
. This completes the analysis of (
3.2.8
).

To see that the map

(3.2.9)

\mathbf{H}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\bm{\mathcal{H}}\ni(v,\eta)\mapsto(1+\eta)(v-\gamma e_{1})\cdot\nabla v\in\mathbf{H}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

is
T^{\infty}_{1,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}
we again expand and examine the resulting linear, bilinear, and trilinear terms. In each case, tameness follows from the boundedness of the linear map
\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\ni\eta\mapsto(\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta,\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta)\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which is Proposition
A.3
, along with Lemma
A.21
.

Similar arguments then verify that the map

(3.2.10)

\mathbb{R}\times\mathbf{H}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbf{H}\ni(\mu,v,\eta)\mapsto v-\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot((1+\eta)\mathbb{S}v)\in\mathbf{H}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

is
T^{\infty}_{2,\max\{{2,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\}}
and that

(3.2.11)

\mathbb{R}\times\bm{\mathcal{H}}\ni(\sigma,\eta)\mapsto(1+\eta)(1-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\eta\in\mathbf{H}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

is
T^{\infty}_{3,\max\{{3,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\}}
. Hence,
\Uppsi
is strongly
3-
tame with base
\max\{{3,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\}
.

The next step of the proof is to verify that
\Upphi\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{1,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\boldsymbol{\mathbb{W}}\times\boldsymbol{\mathbb{X}};\boldsymbol{\mathbb{Y}})
; however, due to the simple structure of this operator, we see that this is a direct consequence of Lemmas
A.20
and
A.22
.

As a consequence of the first and second steps of the proof, we thus learn that

(3.2.12)

\Upupsilon\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{3,\max\{{3,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\boldsymbol{\mathbb{W}}\times\boldsymbol{\mathbb{X}};\boldsymbol{\mathbb{Y}}\times\boldsymbol{\mathbb{W}}),

where we recall that
\Upupsilon
is the mapping defined in (
3.2.3
).

Finally, we can complete the proof of the theorem statement. The first component of
\overline{\Uppsi}
is linear and thus trivially
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{3,\max\{{3,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\}}
. The previous two steps of the proof handle the
\Upupsilon
component of
\overline{\Uppsi}
. Corollary
A.14
handles the final two components.
∎

3.3.
Derivative splitting

Now that we understand the smoothness properties of the maps
\overline{\Uppsi}
and
\overline{\Uppsi}^{\mathrm{sub}}
, we next make a preliminary analysis of their derivatives with the goal of identifying a minimal essential structure. The sets up our linear analysis in the sequel to handle first pleasantly simplified equations.

The following lemma is a technical calculation that ensures we may make a useful change of unknowns in some linear problem. The subscript
\mathrm{WD}
is an acronym for ‘well-defined’, referring to the fact that one can take the reciprocal of
1+\eta
.

Lemma 3.7
.

There exists
\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that the following hold for
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor
.

(1)

For all
\eta\in B_{\mathcal{H}^{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{WD}})
, we have that
\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant 1/2
.

(2)

For all
0<\varepsilon\leqslant\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}
and all
\eta\in B_{\mathcal{H}^{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}(0,\varepsilon)\cap\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have the estimate

(3.3.1)

\lVert 1-(1+\eta)^{-1}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}\lesssim\varepsilon\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}},

where the implicit constant depends only on
s
and
d
.

Proof.

We define
\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}
to be sufficiently small so that for all
\eta\in B_{\mathcal{H}^{1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}(0,\rho_{\mathrm{WD}})
, we have that

(3.3.2)

\max\{{\lVert\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta\rVert_{L^{\infty}},\lVert\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta\rVert_{L^{\infty}}}\}\leqslant 1/4,

which is possible thanks to Proposition
A.3
and the Sobolev embedding. This is the first item.

To prove the second item, we first recall that
r_{d}
is defined in (
A.1.9
) and Taylor expand

(3.3.3)

1-\frac{1}{1+\eta}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{r_{d}}(-\eta)^{\ell}+\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(1-t)^{r_{d}-1}}{(r_{d}-1)!}\Big(\frac{t\eta}{1+t\eta}-\frac{\eta}{1+\eta}\Big)\eta^{r_{d}}\;\mathrm{d}t.

We then estimate the
\mathcal{H}^{s}
norm of the series on the right by using the second item of Corollary
A.5
. In contrast, we estimate the integral term on the right in the
H^{s}
norm, which is a valid strategy in light of the embedding
H^{s}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{s}
(see Proposition 5.3 in Leoni and Tice
[
38
]
). To do so we use the third item of the aforementioned corollary, along with Corollary D.9 of Stevenson and Tice
[
56
]
. Combining these yields (
3.3.1
).
∎

Armed with Lemma
3.7
, we may now decompose a certain partial derivative of the entire nonlinear operator into a principal part and a perturbative remainder.

Proposition 3.8

(Derivative splitting)
.

Let
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant\max\{{3,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\}
,
\varepsilon\in(0,\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}]
,
(\gamma,\mu,\sigma)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
(v_{0},\eta_{0})\in B_{\mathbb{X}_{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}(0,\varepsilon)\cap\mathbb{X}_{\infty}
, and
(v,\eta)\in\mathbb{X}_{s+3}
. Let
\Uppsi:\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{X}_{s+3}\to\mathbb{Y}_{s}
or
\Uppsi:\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s+3}\to\mathbb{Y}_{s}
be as in Definition
3.4
or
3.5
, and write
D_{2}\Uppsi

for its derivative with respect to the second factor (i.e.
\mathbb{X}_{s+3}
or
\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s+2}
). Then the following hold.

(1)

We have the splitting

(3.3.4)

D_{2}\Uppsi(\gamma,\mu,\sigma,v_{0},\eta_{0})[v,\eta]=P^{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}_{v_{0},\eta_{0}}[v,\eta]+(0,R^{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}_{v_{0},\eta_{0}}[v,\eta]),

where
P_{v_{0},\eta_{0}}^{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}[v,\eta]=(h,f)
is given by

(3.3.5)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot((v_{0}-\gamma e_{1})\eta)+\nabla\cdot((1+\eta_{0})v)=h,\\
(1+\eta_{0})(v_{0}-\gamma e_{1})\cdot\nabla v+v-\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot((1+\eta_{0})\mathbb{S}v)+(1+\eta_{0})(1-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\eta=f,\end{cases}

and the remainder piece
R^{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}_{v_{0},\eta_{0}}[v,\eta]\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
is

(3.3.6)

R^{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}_{v_{0},\eta_{0}}[v,\eta]=((1+\eta_{0})v+\eta(v_{0}-\gamma e_{1}))\cdot\nabla v_{0}-\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot(\eta\mathbb{S}v_{0})+\eta(1-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\eta_{0}.

(2)

We have the further decomposition:

(3.3.7)

P^{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}_{v_{0},\eta_{0}}[v/(1+\eta_{0}),\eta]=Q^{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}_{v_{0},\eta_{0}}[v,\eta]+(0,S^{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}_{v_{0},\eta_{0}}[v]),

where
Q^{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}_{v_{0},\eta_{0}}[v,\eta]=(h,f)
is given by

(3.3.8)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot v+\nabla\cdot((v_{0}-\gamma e_{1})\eta)=h,\\
(v_{0}-\gamma e_{1})\cdot\nabla v+v-\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}v+(1+\eta_{0})(1-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\eta=f,\end{cases}

and the remainder
S^{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}_{v_{0},\eta_{0}}[v]\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
is

(3.3.9)

S^{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}_{v_{0},\eta_{0}}[v]=-((1+\eta_{0})^{-1}(v_{0}-\gamma e_{1})\cdot\nabla\eta_{0})v+((1+\eta_{0})^{-1}-1)v\\
+\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot((1+\eta_{0})^{-1}(v\otimes\nabla\eta_{0}+\nabla\eta_{0}\otimes v+2v\cdot\nabla\eta_{0}I)).

Proof.

These follow from elementary calculations.
∎

The following computation verifies that the remainder pieces obtained in Proposition
3.8
are indeed perturbative in the framework of tame estimates.

Proposition 3.9

(Remainder estimates)
.

Let
I\Subset\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
J,K\Subset\mathbb{R}
be bounded, open, and non-empty intervals. Suppose that
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
\varepsilon\in(0,\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}]
,
(v_{0},\eta_{0})\in B_{\mathbb{X}_{3+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}(0,\varepsilon)\cap\mathbb{X}_{\infty}
, and
(\gamma,\mu,\sigma)\in I\times J\times K=\mathcal{Q}
. The following hold.

(1)

The remainder
R^{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}_{v_{0},\eta_{0}}
, defined via (
3.3.6
), extends to a bounded linear maps

(3.3.10)

R^{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}_{v_{0},\eta_{0}}:\;{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\mathbb{X}_{s}\to H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}),\hskip 10.00002ptR^{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}_{v_{0},\eta_{0}}:{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s}\to H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})

that obeys the estimates

(3.3.11)

\lVert R^{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}_{v_{0},\eta_{0}}[v,\eta]\rVert_{H^{s}}\lesssim\varepsilon\lVert v,\eta\rVert_{{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\mathbb{X}_{s}}+\begin{cases}0&\text{if }s\leqslant 1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor,\\
\lVert v_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s+3}}\lVert v,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}&\text{if }s>1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor,\end{cases}

and

(3.3.12)

\lVert R^{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}_{v_{0},\eta_{0}}[v,\eta]\rVert_{H^{s}}\lesssim\varepsilon\lVert v,\eta\rVert_{{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s}}+\begin{cases}0&\text{if }s\leqslant 1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor,\\
\lVert v_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s+2}}\lVert v,\eta\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}&\text{if }s>1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor,\end{cases}

(2)

The remainder
S^{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}_{v_{0},\eta_{0}}
, defined via (
3.3.9
), extends to a bounded linear maps

(3.3.13)

S^{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}_{v_{0},\eta_{0}}:\;{{}_{\mu}}H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\to H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}),

that obeys the estimates

(3.3.14)

\lVert S^{\gamma,\mu,\sigma}_{v_{0},\eta_{0}}[v]\rVert_{H^{s}}\lesssim\varepsilon\;{{}_{\mu}}\lVert v\rVert_{H^{s}}+\begin{cases}0&\text{if }s\leqslant 1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor,\\
\lVert v_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{2+s}}\lVert v\rVert_{H^{1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}&\text{if }s>1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor.\end{cases}

The implicit constants in (
3.3.11
) and (
3.3.14
) depend only on
s
,
\mathcal{Q}
, and
d
.

Proof.

The estimates (
3.3.11
) and (
3.3.12
) are granted by Corollary
A.5
and the second item of Proposition
A.23
. The only potentially delicate term is
\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot(\eta\mathbb{S}v_{0})
in the case of (
3.3.11
), but here we have a derivative of
\eta
appearing with a factor of
\mu^{2}
, and so the norm
{}_{\mu,\sigma}\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}
controls
|\mu|\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s+1}}
, which is more than enough.

The estimate (
3.3.14
) is equally simple; it follows readily from Lemma
3.7
, Corollary
A.5
, and the second item of Proposition
A.23
.
∎

4.
Linear analysis of the shallow water system

This goal of this section is to verify the linear hypotheses of the Nash-Moser inverse function theorem applied to the mappings of Definitions
3.4
and
3.5
. Our strategy is to study first the estimates and existence for a unified principal part of the linear systems. This is done in Sections
4.1
and
4.2
. Then, in Section
4.3
, we carefully combine the analysis of the principal part equations with the derivative splitting calculations of Section
3.3
to reach our main theorems, which are Theorems
4.13
and
4.17
, on the linear analysis of the derivatives of the omnisonic and subsonic nonlinear operators

4.1.
Estimates

This section develops a priori estimates for the following system of equations:

(4.1.1)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot v+\nabla\cdot(X\eta)=h,\\
v+X\cdot\nabla v-\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}v+(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\nabla\eta=f.\end{cases}

Here the given data are
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, while the unknowns are
w:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\eta:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
. The parameters in the problem are
X:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\vartheta,\theta:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, and
\gamma,\mu,\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
. The precise form of
X
,
\vartheta
,
\theta
, and
\gamma,\mu,\sigma
is given by the following definition.

Definition 4.1

(Parameters)
.

In this subsection the tuneable parameters in system (
4.1.1
) are to be taken of following form.

(1)

We fix non-empty, open, and bounded intervals
I\Subset\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
J,K\Subset\mathbb{R}
and enforce that

(4.1.2)

(\gamma,\mu,\sigma)\in I\times J\times K=\mathcal{Q}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}

Note that in Theorem
4.6
we impose a further subsonic restriction on
I
, that is
I\Subset(0,1)
.

(2)

Let
\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
be as in Lemma
3.7
. We let
\varepsilon\in(0,\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}]
,
r=\max\{{6,5+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\}
, and

(4.1.3)

(v_{0},\eta_{0},\beta_{0})\in(B_{H^{r}}(0,\varepsilon)\times B_{\mathcal{H}^{r}}(0,\varepsilon)\times B_{H^{r}}(0,\varepsilon))\cap(H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})).

(3)

We shall take

(4.1.4)

X=v_{0}-\gamma e_{1},\hskip 10.00002pt\vartheta=1+\eta_{0}+\beta_{0},\hskip 10.00002pt\theta=1+\eta_{0}.

The following product estimate exploits integration by parts to crucially save on the number of derivatives appearing in the right hand side of (
4.1.5
).

Lemma 4.2

(A product estimate)
.

We have the estimate

(4.1.5)

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\eta\;\nabla\cdot(X\eta)\Big|\lesssim\varepsilon(\lVert\eta\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{1}})

for all
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
0\not\in\mathrm{supp}\mathscr{F}[\eta]
. The implicit constant depends only on
\mathcal{Q}
and
d
from Definition
4.1
.

Proof.

Using Proposition
A.3
, we decompose
\eta=\eta_{\mathrm{L}}+\eta_{\mathrm{H}}
, for
\eta_{\mathrm{L}}=\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\eta_{\mathrm{H}}=\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta\in H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and expand

(4.1.6)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\eta\;\nabla\cdot(X\eta)=\sum_{(m,n)\in\{{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{H}}\}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\eta_{m}\;\nabla\cdot(X\eta_{n}).

We will deal with each of these four terms separately.

To handle the three of the four terms involving at least one copy of
\eta_{\mathrm{L}}
, we will employ the cancellation

(4.1.7)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\eta_{m}\;\nabla\cdot(X\eta_{n})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\eta_{m}\;\nabla\cdot(X\eta_{n})+\gamma(1-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\eta_{m}\partial_{1}\eta_{n}\\
=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-1-\sigma^{2}(\theta-1)\Delta)\eta_{m}\nabla\cdot(X\eta_{n})+(1-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\eta_{m}\nabla\cdot((X+\gamma e_{1})\eta_{n}),

which actually holds for all
(m,n)\in\{{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{H}}\}^{2}
. The
\mathrm{L}-\mathrm{L}
term is estimated by the right side of (
4.1.5
) by writing

(4.1.8)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\eta_{\mathrm{L}}\;\nabla\cdot(X\eta_{\mathrm{L}})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\min\{{1,|\nabla|}\}(\vartheta-1-\sigma^{2}(\theta-1)\Delta)\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{L}}}\max\{{1,|\nabla|^{-1}}\}\nabla\cdot(X\eta_{\mathrm{L}})\\
+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\min\{{1,|\nabla|}\}(1-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\eta_{\mathrm{L}}\max\{{1,|\nabla|^{-1}}\}\nabla\cdot((X+\gamma e_{1})\eta_{\mathrm{L}}),

and then using Cauchy-Schwarz and Corollary
A.5
on the right hand side’s integrals. In the above we are using
\min\{{1,|\nabla|}\}
and
\max\{{1,|\nabla|^{-1}}\}
to denote the Fourier multiplication operators with symbols
\xi\mapsto\min\{{1,2\pi|\xi|}\}
and
\xi\mapsto\max\{{1,(2\pi|\xi|)^{-1}}\}
, respectively.

A similar strategy can be employed on the mixed
\mathrm{L}-\mathrm{H}
and
\mathrm{H}-\mathrm{L}
terms, but now we rearrange the right side of (
4.1.7
) to
ensure that all of the derivatives fall on
\eta_{\mathrm{L}}
:

(4.1.9)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\eta_{\mathrm{H}}\;\nabla\cdot(X\eta_{\mathrm{L}})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\eta_{\mathrm{H}}((\vartheta-1)\nabla\cdot(X\eta_{\mathrm{L}})-\sigma^{2}\Delta((\theta-1)\nabla\cdot(X\eta_{\mathrm{L}})))\\
+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\eta_{\mathrm{H}}(1-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\cdot((X+\gamma e_{1})\eta_{\mathrm{L}})

and

(4.1.10)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\eta_{\mathrm{L}}\nabla\cdot(X\eta_{\mathrm{H}})=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}X\cdot\nabla((\vartheta-1-\sigma^{2}(\theta-1)\Delta))\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{L}}}\;\eta_{\mathrm{H}}\\
-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(X+\gamma e_{1})\cdot\nabla((1-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\eta_{\mathrm{L}})\;\eta_{\mathrm{H}}.

With these in hand, we may argue as above to bound them in terms of the right side of (
4.1.5
).

It remains only to consider the
\mathrm{H}-\mathrm{H}
term. Thanks to several integrations by parts, we have that

(4.1.11)

-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\nabla\cdot X)(\vartheta\eta_{\mathrm{H}}^{2}+\sigma^{2}\theta|\nabla\eta_{\mathrm{H}}|^{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\eta_{\mathrm{H}}\;\nabla\cdot(X\eta_{\mathrm{H}})+\frac{1}{2}X\cdot(\nabla\vartheta\eta_{\mathrm{H}}^{2}+\sigma^{2}\nabla\theta|\nabla\eta_{\mathrm{H}}|^{2})\\
-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\vartheta(\nabla\cdot X)\eta_{\mathrm{H}}^{2}+\sigma^{2}[\nabla\theta\cdot\nabla\eta_{\mathrm{H}}(X\cdot\nabla)\eta_{\mathrm{H}}+\theta(\nabla\eta_{\mathrm{H}}\otimes\nabla\eta_{\mathrm{H}}):\nabla X+\nabla((\nabla\cdot X)\theta\eta_{\mathrm{H}})\cdot\nabla\eta_{\mathrm{H}}].

We isolate the first term on the right hand side of this equality and then estimate each of the remaining terms using Corollary
A.5
and Proposition
A.23
.
∎

We now utilize the previous lemma in the derivation of the following estimate. The acronym
\mathrm{AP}
stands for ‘a priori’ while the
0
refers to the fact that
f
and
h
belong to
\mathbb{Y}_{0}
.

Proposition 4.3

(A priori estimates, I)
.

Suppose that
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
h\in(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1})(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
v\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
0\not\in\mathrm{supp}\mathscr{F}[\eta]
solve system (
4.1.1
). There exists
\mathbb{R}^{+}\ni\rho_{\mathrm{AP0}}\leqslant\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}
with the property that for all
\mathbb{R}^{+}\ni\varepsilon\leqslant\rho_{\mathrm{AP0}}
and
(v_{0},\eta_{0},\beta_{0})
satisfying (
4.1.3
) we have the estimates

(4.1.12)

\lVert v\rVert_{L^{2}}+|\mu|\lVert v\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{L^{2}}+\varepsilon^{1/2}{{}_{\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\eta\;h\Big|^{1/2}

and

(4.1.13)

|\mu|\lVert\nabla v\rVert_{L^{2}}+\mu^{2}\lVert\nabla v\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{L^{2}}+\varepsilon^{1/2}{{}_{\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+|\mu|\varepsilon^{1/2}{{}_{\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}+|\mu|\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\nabla\eta\cdot\nabla h\Big|^{1/2}.

The implicit constants in (
4.1.12
) and (
4.1.13
), depend only on
\mathcal{Q}
and
d
from Definition
4.1
.

Proof.

We begin with the proof of (
4.1.12
). We take the dot-product of the second equation in (
4.1.1
) with
v
and integrate by parts:

(4.1.14)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|v\right|^{2}\Big(1-\frac{\nabla\cdot X}{2}\Big)+\mu^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{2}\left|\mathbb{D}v\right|^{2}+2\left|\nabla\cdot v\right|^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\cdot v-(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\nabla\eta\cdot v.

Taking
\rho_{\mathrm{AP0}}
sufficiently small, employing the Korn inequality in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and recalling that we allow implicit constants to depend on
\mathcal{Q}
, we have that

(4.1.15)

\lVert v\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mu^{2}\lVert v\rVert^{2}_{H^{1}}\lesssim\lVert v\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mu^{2}\lVert\nabla v\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|v\right|^{2}\Big(1-\frac{\nabla\cdot X}{2}\Big)+\mu^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{2}\left|\mathbb{D}v\right|^{2}+2\left|\nabla\cdot v\right|^{2}.

It remains only to handle the terms on the right side of (
4.1.14
).
The
f
term is trivially handled with Cauchy-Schwarz and an absorbing argument. For the
\eta
-term, we introduce a commutator and integrate by parts:

(4.1.16)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\nabla\eta\cdot v=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\eta\;\nabla\cdot v+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta),\nabla]\eta\cdot v.

The first equation in (
4.1.1
) and Lemma
4.2
yield the bound

(4.1.17)

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\eta\;\nabla\cdot v\Big|\leqslant\varepsilon(\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{1}})+\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\eta\;h\Big|.

On the other hand, thanks to Corollary
A.5
, we have that

(4.1.18)

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta),\nabla]\eta\cdot v\Big|=\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\vartheta\eta\cdot v-\sigma^{2}\nabla\theta\Delta\eta\cdot v\Big|\lesssim\varepsilon(\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{2}})\lVert v\rVert_{L^{2}}.

Synthesizing these with (
4.1.14
) then yields (
4.1.12
).

Next, we turn to the proof of (
4.1.13
). We will prove this initially under the stronger assumption that
v\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and then we will conclude the proof by showing how to reduce the general case to this special case.

We dot the second equation in (
4.1.1
) with
-\mu^{2}\Delta v
and integrate to see that

(4.1.19)

-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(v-\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}v)\cdot\mu^{2}\Delta v=\mu^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}-f\cdot\Delta v+(X\cdot\nabla)v\cdot\Delta v+(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\nabla\eta\cdot\Delta v.

We will deal with the left and right sides separately. Integrating by parts and using Korn’s inequality grants the coercive estimate for the left side:

(4.1.20)

\mu^{2}\lVert\nabla v\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mu^{4}\lVert\nabla v\rVert_{H^{1}}^{2}\lesssim-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(v-\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}v)\cdot\mu^{2}\Delta v.

Now we estimate the right side of (
4.1.19
). The
f
term is easily handled with Cauchy-Schwarz and an absorbing argument since
\lVert\Delta v\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\nabla v\rVert_{H^{1}}
. The
X\cdot\nabla v
term controlled by integrating by parts:

(4.1.21)

\Big|\mu^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(X\cdot\nabla)v\cdot\Delta v\Big|=\Big|-\mu^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\nabla\cdot X}{2}|\nabla v|^{2}+\mu^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\partial_{j}X\cdot\nabla)v\cdot\partial_{j}v\Big|\lesssim\varepsilon\mu^{2}\lVert\nabla v\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}.

The
\eta
-term in (
4.1.19
) is more involved. We have that

(4.1.22)

-\mu^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\nabla\eta\cdot\Delta v=\mu^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\partial_{j}\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\partial_{j}\theta\Delta)\nabla\eta\cdot\partial_{j}v\\
-\mu^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\vartheta\partial_{j}\eta\cdot\partial_{j}v-\sigma^{2}\nabla\theta\Delta\partial_{j}\eta\cdot\partial_{j}v-\mu^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\partial_{j}\eta\;\nabla\cdot\partial_{j}v.

The first two terms on the right here are straightforward to estimate:

(4.1.23)

\mu^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\partial_{j}\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\partial_{j}\theta\Delta)\nabla\eta\cdot\partial_{j}v+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\nabla\vartheta\partial_{j}\eta-\sigma^{2}\nabla\theta\Delta\eta)\cdot\partial_{j}v\Big|\lesssim\varepsilon|\mu|(\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\sigma^{4}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{3}})\cdot|\mu|\lVert\nabla v\rVert_{L^{2}}.

For the third term on the right side of (
4.1.22
), we use the identity
\nabla\cdot\partial_{j}v+\nabla\cdot(X\partial_{j}\eta)=\partial_{j}h-\nabla\cdot(\partial_{j}X\eta)
and appeal to Lemma
4.2
to bound

(4.1.24)

\mu^{2}\Big|\sum_{j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\partial_{j}\eta\;\nabla\cdot\partial_{j}v\Big|\lesssim\mu^{2}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\nabla\eta\cdot\nabla h\Big|+\varepsilon\mu^{2}(\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}^{2}+\sigma^{4}\lVert\eta\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}).

Upon synthesizing these estimates for the various terms in (
4.1.19
), we arrive at the bound

(4.1.25)

\mu^{2}\lVert\nabla v\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mu^{4}\lVert\nabla v\rVert_{H^{1}}^{2}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\mu^{2}\lVert\nabla v\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon|\mu|(\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{3}})\cdot|\mu|\lVert\nabla v\rVert_{L^{2}}\\
+\mu^{2}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\nabla\eta\cdot\nabla h\Big|+\varepsilon\mu^{2}(\lVert\eta\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\sigma^{4}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}^{2}).

Using an absorbing argument and taking
\rho_{\mathrm{AP0}}
small enough, we deduce from this that (
4.1.13
) holds in this special case of additional smoothness assumptions.

To conclude, we now explain how to reduce the general case of (
4.1.13
) to the special case. Suppose, then, that
v\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
0\not\in\mathrm{supp}\mathscr{F}[\eta]
. Given any
\delta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
there exists
v_{\delta}\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\eta_{\delta}\in\mathcal{H}^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\mathrm{dist}(0,\mathrm{supp}\mathscr{F}[\eta_{\delta}])=\mathrm{dist}(0,\mathrm{supp}\mathscr{F}[\eta])
and
\lVert v-v_{\delta}\rVert_{H^{2}}<\delta
,
\lVert\eta-\eta_{\delta}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}<\delta
. The equations satisfied by
v_{\delta}
and
\eta_{\delta}
are (
4.1.1
) with right hand side data

(4.1.26)

h_{\delta}=h-\nabla\cdot(v-v_{\delta})-\nabla\cdot(X(\eta-\eta_{\delta}))

and

(4.1.27)

f_{\delta}=f-(1+X\cdot\nabla-\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S})(v-v_{\delta})-(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\nabla(\eta-\eta_{\delta}).

We invoke the a priori estimate from the special case to deduce that

(4.1.28)

|\mu|\lVert\nabla v_{\delta}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mu^{2}\lVert\nabla v_{\delta}\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim\lVert f_{\delta}\rVert_{L^{2}}+\varepsilon^{1/2}(\lVert\eta_{\delta}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\eta_{\delta}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{2}})\\
+|\mu|\varepsilon^{1/2}(\lVert\eta_{\delta}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\eta_{\delta}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{3}})+|\mu|\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\nabla\eta_{\delta}\cdot\nabla h_{\delta}\Big|^{1/2}.

We now send
\delta\to 0
and observe that
f_{\delta}\to f
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
h_{\delta}\to h
in
(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1})(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
v_{\delta}\to v
in
H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
\eta_{\delta}\to\eta
in
\mathcal{H}^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(actually in
H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
). By passing to the limit in (
4.1.28
), we thus acquire (
4.1.13
) in the general case, which completes the proof.
∎

Our next result obtains closed (when
h=0
) a priori estimates at a base case level of derivatives.

Theorem 4.4

(A priori estimates, II)
.

Suppose that
f\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
h\in(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{2})(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
v\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
0\not\in\mathrm{supp}\mathscr{F}[\eta]
solve system (
4.1.1
). Let
\rho_{\mathrm{AP0}}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
be as in Proposition
4.3
. Then there exists
\mathbb{R}^{+}\ni\rho_{\mathrm{AP1}}\leqslant\rho_{\mathrm{AP0}}
with the property that for all
\mathbb{R}^{+}\ni\varepsilon\leqslant\rho_{\mathrm{AP1}}
and
(v_{0},\eta_{0},\beta_{0})
satisfying (
4.1.3
), we have the estimate

(4.1.29)

{{}_{\mu}}\lVert v\rVert_{H^{1}}+{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{1}}+\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\eta\;h\Big|^{1/2}\\
+\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\partial_{j}\eta\;\partial_{j}h\Big|^{1/2}+|\mu|\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\partial_{j}\eta\cdot\nabla\partial_{j}h\Big|^{1/2}+|\sigma|\lVert\nabla h\rVert_{L^{2}}.

The implicit constant in (
4.1.29
) depends only on
\mathcal{Q}
and
d
from Definition
4.1
.

Proof.

For
j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
we apply
\partial_{j}
to (
4.1.1
) to obtain the system

(4.1.30)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot\partial_{j}v+\nabla\cdot(X\partial_{j}\eta)=\partial_{j}h-\nabla\cdot(\partial_{j}X\eta),\\
\partial_{j}v+X\cdot\nabla\partial_{j}v-\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}\partial_{j}v+(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\nabla\partial_{j}\eta=\partial_{j}f-\partial_{j}X\cdot\nabla v-(\partial_{j}\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\partial_{j}\theta\Delta)\nabla\eta.\end{cases}

To each of these we apply the a priori estimates of Proposition
4.3
and then sum over
j
; we then sum this with the estimate for the non-differentiated problem provided by the same proposition. This results in the bound

(4.1.31)

\lVert v\rVert_{H^{1}}+\mu^{2}\lVert v\rVert_{H^{3}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{1}}+\varepsilon^{1/2}(\lVert v\rVert_{H^{1}}+\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}+|\mu|(\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{2}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{4}}))\\
+\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\partial_{j}\eta\;\nabla\cdot(\partial_{j}X\eta)\Big|^{1/2}+|\mu|\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\partial_{j}\eta\cdot\nabla\nabla\cdot(\partial_{j}X\eta)\Big|^{1/2}+R_{\eta,h},

where

(4.1.32)

R_{\eta,h}=\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\eta\;h\Big|^{1/2}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\partial_{j}\eta\;\partial_{j}h\Big|^{1/2}+|\mu|\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\partial_{j}\eta\cdot\nabla\partial_{j}h\Big|^{1/2}.

It is straightforward to estimate

(4.1.33)

\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\partial_{j}\eta\;\nabla\cdot(\partial_{j}X\eta)\Big|^{1/2}+|\mu|\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\partial_{j}\eta\cdot\nabla\nabla\cdot(\partial_{j}X\eta)\Big|^{1/2}\\
\lesssim\varepsilon^{1/2}(\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{3}})+\varepsilon^{1/2}|\mu|(\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{2}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{4}}).

Thus, if
\rho_{\mathrm{AP1}}
is small enough, this and (
4.1.31
) yield the bound

(4.1.34)

\lVert v\rVert_{H^{1}}+\mu^{2}\lVert v\rVert_{H^{3}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{1}}+\varepsilon^{1/2}(\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}+|\mu|(\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{2}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{4}}))+R_{\eta,h}.

We now turn our attention to higher regularity estimates for
\eta
. We know that
\nabla\eta
satisfies the equation

(4.1.35)

(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\nabla\eta=f-v-X\cdot\nabla v+\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}v,

and, decreasing
\rho_{\mathrm{AP1}}
if necessary, we may assume that the operator
\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta
is uniformly elliptic. Sobolev interpolation allows us to bound

(4.1.36)

\lVert X\cdot\nabla v\rVert_{L^{2}}+|\mu|\lVert X\cdot\nabla v\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim\lVert v\rVert_{H^{1}}+|\mu|\lVert v\rVert_{H^{2}}\lesssim\lVert v\rVert_{H^{1}}+|\mu|\lVert v\rVert_{H^{1}}^{1/2}\lVert v\rVert_{H^{3}}^{1/2}\lesssim\lVert v\rVert_{H^{1}}+\mu^{2}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{H^{3}},

and hence we may employ standard elliptic regularity on (
4.1.35
) to obtain the bound

(4.1.37)

\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{L^{2}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}+|\mu|(\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{1}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{3}})\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{1}}+\lVert v\rVert_{H^{1}}+\mu^{2}\lVert v\rVert_{H^{3}}.

On the other hand, from the identity
\nabla\cdot v+\nabla\cdot(X\eta)=0
, we find that

(4.1.38)

|\gamma|\lVert\mathcal{R}_{1}\eta\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert v\rVert_{L^{2}}+\varepsilon\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}.

Combining these two estimates with the norm equivalence in the third item of Proposition
A.1
and the estimate (
4.1.34
) then shows that

(4.1.39)

\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}+|\mu|(\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{2}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{4}})\lesssim\varepsilon\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+\lVert f\rVert_{H^{1}}+\lVert v\rVert_{H^{1}}+\mu^{2}\lVert v\rVert_{H^{3}}\\
\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{1}}+\varepsilon\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+\varepsilon^{1/2}(\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}+|\mu|(\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{2}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{4}}))+R_{\eta,h}.

Summing (
4.1.34
) and (
4.1.39
) and taking
\rho_{\mathrm{AP1}}
sufficiently small, we may absorb the
\eta
terms on the right with those on the left, resulting in the estimate

(4.1.40)

\lVert v\rVert_{H^{1}}+\mu^{2}\lVert v\rVert_{H^{3}}+\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{3}}+|\mu|(\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{2}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{4}})\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{1}}+R_{\eta,h}.

Thus, to finish the proof of (
4.1.29
) we only need to prove the estimate

(4.1.41)

|\sigma|(\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{2}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{4}})\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{1}}+R_{\eta,h}+|\sigma|\lVert\nabla h\rVert_{L^{2}}.

To this end, we take the divergence of the second equation in (
4.1.1
) and substitute in the first equation to derive the identity

(4.1.42)

(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\Delta\eta=(X\cdot\nabla)\nabla\cdot(X\eta)+\nabla\cdot f-\nabla X^{\mathrm{t}}:\nabla v-(1-4\mu^{2}\Delta)\nabla\cdot v-X\cdot\nabla h\\
-(\nabla\vartheta\cdot\nabla\eta-\sigma^{2}\nabla\theta\cdot\nabla\Delta\eta).

Then we may again use elliptic regularity to bound

(4.1.43)

|\sigma|(\lVert\nabla^{2}\eta\rVert_{L^{2}}+\sigma^{2}\lVert\nabla^{4}\eta\rVert_{L^{2}})\lesssim|\sigma|\lVert(X\cdot\nabla)\nabla\cdot(X\eta)+\nabla\cdot f-\nabla X^{\mathrm{t}}:\nabla v-(1-4\mu^{2}\Delta)\nabla\cdot v-X\cdot\nabla h\rVert_{L^{2}}\\
+|\sigma|\lVert\nabla\vartheta\cdot\nabla\eta-\sigma^{2}\nabla\theta\cdot\nabla\Delta\eta\rVert_{L^{2}},

and from here we can use Sobolev interpolation again (but now with powers of
\sigma
) together with (
4.1.40
) to deduce (
4.1.41
).
∎

We can now use the previous result with a simple differentiation argument to derive a priori estimates for higher derivatives of solutions to (
4.1.1
).

Theorem 4.5

(Principal part a priori estimates, omnisonic case)
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor
,
f\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
h\in(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1+s})(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
v\in H^{2+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
solve system (
4.1.1
). There exists
\mathbb{R}^{+}\ni\rho_{\mathrm{AP}s}\leqslant\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}
, depending only on
\mathcal{Q}
,
\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}
,
r
(from Definition
4.1
) and
s
, with the property that for all
\mathbb{R}^{+}\ni\varepsilon\leqslant\rho_{\mathrm{AP}s}
and
(v_{0},\eta_{0},\beta_{0})
satisfying (
4.1.3
), we have the estimate

(4.1.44)

\lVert v,\eta\rVert_{{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\mathbb{X}_{s}}\lesssim\lVert h,f\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{s}}+\lVert v_{0},\eta_{0},\beta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{1+s}\times H^{1+s}}\lVert h,f\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}

The implicit constant in (
4.1.44
) depends only on
s
,
\mathcal{Q}
, and
d
from Definition
4.1
.

Proof.

We divide the proof into two stages. In the first of these we consider only the special case in which
h=0
and
0\not\in\mathrm{supp}\mathscr{F}[\eta]
. An application of Theorem
4.4
yields the estimate

(4.1.45)

\lVert v,\eta\rVert_{{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\mathbb{X}_{1}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{1}}.

In order to obtain higher-order estimates we will apply this same a priori estimate to the derivatives of (
4.1.1
); it will suffice to consider only pure derivatives of order
s-1
. To this end, we let
q=s-1
,
j\in\{{1,\dots,d}\}
, and consider the equations satisfied by
\partial_{j}^{q}v
and
\partial_{j}^{q}\eta
:

(4.1.46)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot\partial_{j}^{q}v+\nabla\cdot(X\partial_{j}^{q}\eta)=\nabla\cdot([X,\partial_{j}^{q}]\eta),\\
\partial_{j}^{q}v+X\cdot\partial_{j}^{q}v-\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}\partial_{j}^{q}v+(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\nabla\partial_{j}^{q}\eta=\partial_{j}^{q}f+[X,\partial_{j}^{q}]\cdot\nabla v+([\vartheta,\partial_{j}^{q}]-\sigma^{2}[\theta,\partial_{j}^{q}]\Delta)\nabla\eta.\end{cases}

Using Theorem
4.4
again, we acquire the estimate

(4.1.47)

{{}_{\mu}}\lVert\partial_{j}^{q}v\rVert_{H^{1}}+{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\lVert\partial_{j}^{q}\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\lesssim\lVert\partial_{j}^{q}f+[X,\partial_{j}^{q}]\cdot\nabla v+([\vartheta,\partial_{j}^{q}]-\sigma^{2}[\theta,\partial_{j}^{q}]\Delta)\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{1}}\\
+\sum_{k=1}^{d}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\partial_{k}\partial_{j}^{q}\eta\partial_{k}\nabla\cdot([X,\partial_{j}^{q}]\eta)\Big|^{1/2}+|\mu|\sum_{k=1}^{d}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\nabla\partial_{k}\partial_{j}^{q}\eta\cdot\nabla\partial_{k}\nabla\cdot([X,\partial_{j}^{q}]\eta)\Big|^{1/2}\\
+|\sigma|\lVert\nabla\nabla\cdot([X,\partial_{j}^{q}]\eta)\rVert_{L^{2}}.

The rest of our work in this stage consists of obtaining structured estimates for the terms appearing on the right here.

For the first term on the right hand side of (
4.1.47
) we utilize the tame estimates on commutators from Proposition
A.24
to bound

(4.1.48)

\lVert\partial_{j}^{q}f+[X,\partial_{j}^{q}]\cdot\nabla v+([\vartheta,\partial_{j}^{q}]-\sigma^{2}[\theta,\partial_{j}^{q}]\Delta)\nabla\eta\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}}+\varepsilon(\lVert v\rVert_{H^{s}}+{{}_{\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}})\\
+{\lVert v_{0},\beta_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{H^{s}\times H^{s}\times\mathcal{H}^{s}}}(\lVert v\rVert_{H^{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}+{{}_{\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}).

For the remaining terms on the right hand side of (
4.1.47
), we first claim that the following preliminary estimate holds:

(4.1.49)

\lVert[X,\partial_{j}^{q}]\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}\lesssim\varepsilon\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}+\lVert v_{0}\rVert_{H^{s+1}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}.

To see this, we note that
[X,\partial_{j}^{q}]=[v_{0},\partial_{j}^{q}]
and employ the Fourier localization operators from (
A.1.7
) to bound

(4.1.50)

\lVert[X,\partial_{j}^{q}]\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}\leqslant\lVert[v_{0},\partial_{j}^{q}]\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}+\lVert\partial_{j}^{q}(v_{0}\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta)\rVert_{H^{2}}+\lVert v_{0}\partial_{j}^{q}\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}.

Propositions
A.3
,
A.23
, and
A.24
, together with Corollary
A.5
then allow us to estimate

(4.1.51)

\lVert[v_{0},\partial_{j}^{q}]\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}+\lVert\partial_{j}^{q}(v_{0}\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta)\rVert_{H^{2}}+\lVert v_{0}\partial_{j}^{q}\Uppi^{1}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}\lesssim\varepsilon\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}+\lVert v_{0}\rVert_{H^{s+1}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}},

and so we obtain (
4.1.49
) from this and (
4.1.50
), completing the proof of the claim. With the claim in hand, we deduce that

(4.1.52)

\sum_{k=1}^{d}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\partial_{k}\partial_{j}^{q}\eta\partial_{k}\nabla\cdot([X,\partial_{j}^{q}]\eta)\Big|^{1/2}+|\mu|\sum_{k=1}^{d}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\nabla\partial_{k}\partial_{j}^{q}\eta\cdot\nabla\partial_{k}\nabla\cdot([X,\partial_{j}^{q}]\eta)\Big|^{1/2}\\
+|\sigma|\lVert\nabla\nabla\cdot([X,\partial_{j}^{q}]\eta)\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\;{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}^{1/2}\lVert[X,\partial_{j}^{q}]\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}^{1/2}+|\sigma|\lVert[X,\partial_{j}^{q}]\eta\rVert_{H^{2}}.

We may thus combine (
4.1.45
), (
4.1.47
), (
4.1.48
), (
4.1.49
), and (
4.1.52
) to arrive at the bound

(4.1.53)

{{}_{\mu}}\lVert v\rVert_{H^{s}}+{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}\asymp{{}_{\mu}}\lVert v\rVert_{H^{1}}+{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}({{}_{\mu}}\lVert\partial_{j}^{s-1}v\rVert_{H^{1}}+{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\lVert\partial_{j}^{s-1}\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}})\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}}+\varepsilon(\lVert v\rVert_{H^{s}}+{{}_{\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}})\\
+{\lVert v_{0},\eta_{0},\beta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s+1}\times H^{s+1}}}(\lVert v\rVert_{H^{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}+{{}_{\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}})+{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}^{1/2}(\varepsilon\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}+\lVert v_{0}\rVert_{H^{s+1}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}})^{1/2}.

Taking
\rho_{\mathrm{APs}}
sufficiently small to absorb the
\varepsilon
terms, (
4.1.53
) implies that

(4.1.54)

{{}_{\mu}}\lVert v\rVert_{H^{s}}+{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}}+{\lVert v_{0},\beta_{0},\eta_{0}\rVert_{H^{s+1}\times H^{s+1}\times\mathcal{H}^{s+1}}}(\lVert v\rVert_{H^{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}+{{}_{\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}).

This is nearly (
4.1.44
); it remains to remove the
v
and
\eta
terms on the right. For this we consider (
4.1.54
) in the case
s=s_{0}=2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor
and exploit (
4.1.3
) to make another absorption argument in order to obtain the closed estimate

(4.1.55)

{{}_{\mu}}\lVert v\rVert_{H^{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}+{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}.

Thus, by combining (
4.1.54
) and (
4.1.55
), we find that for any
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor
we have the bound

(4.1.56)

{{}_{\mu}}\lVert v\rVert_{H^{s}}+{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{H^{s}}+{\lVert v_{0},\eta_{0},\beta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s+1}\times H^{s+1}}}\lVert f\rVert_{H^{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}},

which is (
4.1.44
). This completes the first stage of the proof.

In the second stage, the task is to add in the dependence of
h
and to remove the extra Fourier support hypotheses on
\eta
. Suppose first that the system (
4.1.1
) is satisfied by
v
,
\eta
, and
f
as in the previous stage, but now with
h\in(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1+s})(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. As in Proposition
2.1
, we define
H\in H^{2+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
via
H=\nabla\Delta^{-1}h
and note that
\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{H^{2+s}}\asymp\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1+s}}
. We then set
w=v-H
and observe that
w
and
\eta
satisfy the system

(4.1.57)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot w+\nabla\cdot(X\eta)=0,\\
w+X\cdot\nabla w-\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}w+(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\nabla\eta=f-(I+X\cdot\nabla-\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S})H,\end{cases}

which means we are in a position to apply estimate (
4.1.56
) from the previous stage. Doing so and handling the
X\cdot\nabla H
term with Proposition
A.23
, we obtain the bound

(4.1.58)

{{}_{\mu}}\lVert w\rVert_{H^{s}}+{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}\lesssim\lVert h,f\rVert_{\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1+s}\times H^{s}}+{\lVert v_{0},\eta_{0},\beta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s+1}\times H^{s+1}}}\lVert h,f\rVert_{\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{3+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\times H^{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}.

Since
{{}_{\mu}}\lVert v\rVert_{H^{s}}\lesssim{{}_{\mu}}\lVert w\rVert_{H^{s}}+\lVert h\rVert_{\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1+s}}
, the estimate (
4.1.58
) implies (
4.1.44
).

The final task in the proof is to remove the auxiliary Fourier support assumption on
\eta
. This is straightforward, since we can frequency split
\eta=\Uppi^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta+\Uppi^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta
with
\kappa\in(0,1)
and consider the equations satisfied by
v
and
\Uppi^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta
:

(4.1.59)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot v+\nabla\cdot(X\Uppi^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta)=h-\nabla\cdot(X\Uppi^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta),\\
v+X\cdot\nabla v-\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}v+(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\nabla\Uppi^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta=f-(\vartheta-\sigma^{2}\theta\Delta)\nabla\Uppi^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta.\end{cases}

To this system we invoke the a priori estimates of the previous stage and send
\kappa\to 0
. Since the right hand side of (
4.1.59
) converges in
(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1+s})(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to
(h,f)
as
\kappa\to 0
, we obtain the estimate (
4.1.44
) as a result of this limiting procedure.
∎

For the final result of this subsection, we provide specialized a priori estimates for (
4.1.1
) in the subsonic regime. The acronym
\mathrm{SAP}
stands for ‘subsonic a priori’.

Theorem 4.6

(Principal part a priori estimates, subsonic case)
.

Assume that
\mathcal{Q}=I\times J\times K
is subsonic in the sense that
I\Subset(0,1)
and suppose that
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor
. Further suppose that
f\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
h\in(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1+s})(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
v\in H^{2+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
solve system (
4.1.1
). There exists
\mathbb{R}^{+}\ni\rho_{\mathrm{SAP}s}\leqslant\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}
, depending only on
\mathcal{Q}
,
\rho_{\mathrm{WD}}
,
r
(from Definition
4.1
) and
s
, with the property that for all
\mathbb{R}^{+}\ni\varepsilon\leqslant\rho_{\mathrm{SAP}s}
and
(v_{0},\eta_{0},\beta_{0})
satisfying (
4.1.3
), we have the estimate

(4.1.60)

\lVert v,\eta\rVert_{{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s}}\lesssim\lVert h,f\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{s}}+\lVert v_{0},\eta_{0},\beta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{1+s}\times H^{1+s}}\lVert h,f\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}.

Here the implicit constant in (
4.1.44
) depends only on
s
,
\mathcal{Q}
, and
d
from Definition
4.1
.

Proof.

We shall seek
\rho_{\mathrm{SAP}s}\leqslant\rho_{\mathrm{AP}s}
, where the latter parameter is the one granted by Theorem
4.1.1
, so that estimate (
4.1.44
) holds for our solution. To prove (
4.1.60
) we only need to promote the regularity of
\eta
, as the required estimates on
v
are already met; our main tool in achieving this promotion is a more careful study of equation (
4.1.42
). We can rewrite said equation as

(4.1.61)

(\Delta-\gamma^{2}\partial_{1}^{2})\eta-\sigma^{2}\Delta^{2}\eta=-\eta_{0}(1-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\Delta\eta-\beta_{0}\Delta\eta+(X\otimes X-\gamma^{2}e_{1}\otimes e_{1}):\nabla^{2}\eta+X\cdot\nabla(\eta\nabla\cdot X)\\
+\nabla\cdot f-\nabla X^{\mathrm{t}}:\nabla v-(1-3\mu^{2}\Delta)\nabla\cdot v-X\cdot\nabla h-(\nabla\vartheta\cdot\nabla\eta-\sigma^{2}\nabla\theta\cdot\nabla\Delta\eta)

in order to make evident the utility of inclusion
\gamma\in I\Subset(0,1)
in the negative definiteness of the symbol of the operator on the left away from zero. Note that this condition also implies the ellipticity of the operator on the left when
\sigma=0
.

We shall take the norm of both sides of equation (
4.1.61
) in the space
H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Due to ellipticity, we obtain the bound

(4.1.62)

\lVert\nabla^{2}\eta\rVert_{{{}_{\sigma}}H^{s-1}}\lesssim\lVert-\eta_{0}(1-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\Delta\eta-\beta_{0}\Delta\eta+(X\otimes X-\gamma^{2}e_{1}\otimes e_{1}):\nabla^{2}\eta\rVert_{H^{s-1}}\\
+\lVert X\cdot\nabla(\eta\nabla\cdot X)+\nabla\cdot f-\nabla X^{\mathrm{t}}:\nabla v-(1-3\mu^{2}\Delta)\nabla\cdot v-X\cdot\nabla h-(\nabla\vartheta\cdot\nabla\eta-\sigma^{2}\nabla\theta\cdot\nabla\Delta\eta)\rVert_{H^{s-1}}\\
=\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{II},

and we will estimate the two terms on the right separately. Thanks to Corollary
A.5
and Proposition
A.23
, we have the bounds

(4.1.63)

\mathbf{I}\lesssim\varepsilon\lVert\nabla^{2}\eta\rVert_{{{}_{\sigma}}H^{s-1}}+\lVert v_{0},\eta_{0},\beta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s-1}\times H^{s-1}}\lVert\nabla^{2}\eta\rVert_{{{}_{\sigma}}H^{1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}

and

(4.1.64)

\mathbf{II}\lesssim\lVert v,\eta,h,f\rVert_{{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\mathbb{X}_{s}\times\mathbb{Y}_{s}}+\lVert v_{0},\eta_{0},\beta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s+1}\times H^{s+1}}\lVert v,\eta,h,f\rVert_{{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\mathbb{X}_{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\times\mathbb{Y}_{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}.

We combine (
4.1.62
), (
4.1.63
), and (
4.1.64
) with (
4.1.44
) to acquire the estimate

(4.1.65)

\lVert\nabla^{2}\eta\rVert_{{{}_{\sigma}}H^{s-1}}\lesssim\varepsilon\lVert\nabla^{2}\eta\rVert_{{{}_{\sigma}}H^{s-1}}+\lVert v_{0},\eta_{0},\beta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{s-1}\times H^{s-1}}\lVert\nabla^{2}\eta\rVert_{{{}_{\sigma}}H^{1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}\\
+\lVert h,f\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{s}}+\lVert v_{0},\eta_{0},\beta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{1+s}\times H^{1+s}}\lVert h,f\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}.

By taking
s=2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor
and
\varepsilon\leqslant\rho_{\mathrm{SAP}s}
sufficiently small in (
4.1.65
), we may use an absorbing argument to learn that

(4.1.66)

\lVert\nabla^{2}\eta\rVert_{{{}_{\sigma}}H^{1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}\lesssim\lVert h,f\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}.

Inputting (
4.1.66
) into (
4.1.65
) for
s\geqslant 2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor
and taking
\varepsilon
smaller, if necessary, we arrive at the bound

(4.1.67)

\lVert\nabla^{2}\eta\rVert_{{{}_{\sigma}}H^{s-1}}\lesssim\lVert h,f\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{s}}+\lVert v_{0},\eta_{0},\beta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{1+s}\times H^{1+s}}\lVert h,f\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}.

Combining (
4.1.67
) with (
4.1.44
) then yields (
4.1.60
).
∎

4.2.
Existence

The first goal of this subsection is to establish the existence of solutions to system (
4.1.1
). The case of
\mu\cdot\sigma\neq 0
is a direct application of the method of continuity along with Theorems
2.3
and
4.5
.

Proposition 4.7

(Preliminary existence result)
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant 2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor
,
\mathbb{R}^{+}\ni\varepsilon\leqslant\rho_{\mathrm{APs}}
, and
(v_{0},\eta_{0},\beta_{0})
satisfy (
4.1.3
). If
(\mu,\sigma)\in(J\setminus\{{0}\})\times(K\setminus\{{0}\})
, and
\gamma\in I\Subset\mathbb{R}
, then for every
(h,f)\in(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1+s})(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
there exists a unique
(v,\eta)\in H^{2+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfying system (
4.1.1
).

Proof.

We consider the affine homotopy of bounded linear operators

(4.2.1)

L_{t}:H^{2+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1+s})(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\text{ for }t\in[0,1]

defined via

(4.2.2)

L_{t}(v,\eta)=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla\cdot w-\gamma\partial_{1}\eta+t\nabla\cdot(v_{0}\eta)\\
v-\gamma\partial_{1}v-\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}v+(1-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\eta+t(v_{0}\nabla\cdot v+(\eta_{0}+\beta_{0}-\sigma^{2}\eta_{0}\Delta)\nabla\eta)\end{pmatrix}.

Theorem
4.5
provides a constant
C>0
such that

(4.2.3)

{{}_{\mu}}\lVert v\rVert_{H^{s}}+{{}_{\mu,\sigma}}\lVert\eta\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}\leqslant C\lVert L_{t}(v,\eta)\rVert_{\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{1+s}\times H^{s}}\text{ for all }t\in[0,1].

On the other hand, Theorem
4.5
shows that
L_{0}
is a linear isomorphism. Hence, the method of continuity (see, for instance, Theorem 5.2 in Gilbarg and Trudinger
[
24
]
) guarantees that the map
L_{1}
is also an isomorphism, and
L_{1}
corresponds to the operator in system (
4.1.1
).
∎

Our main existence theorems are the following two results.

Theorem 4.8

(Omnisonic and subsonic existence result)
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{N}\ni s\geqslant\max\{{3,2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\}
. The following hold.

(1)

If
\mathbb{R}^{+}\ni\varepsilon\leqslant\rho_{\mathrm{APs}}
, where the latter parameter is from Theorem
4.5
,
(v_{0},\eta_{0},\beta_{0})
satisfy (
4.1.3
),
(\mu,\sigma)\in J\times K
, and
\gamma\in I\Subset\mathbb{R}^{+}
, then for every
(h,f)\in\mathbb{Y}_{s}
there exists a unique
(v,\eta)\in\mathbb{X}_{s}
satisfying system (
4.1.1
). Moreover, the solution obeys estimate (
4.1.44
).

(2)

If
\mathbb{R}^{+}\ni\varepsilon\leqslant\rho_{\mathrm{SAPs}}
, where the latter parameter is from Theorem
4.6
,
(v_{0},\eta_{0},\beta_{0})
satisfy (
4.1.3
),
(\mu,\sigma)\in J\times K
, and
\gamma\in I\Subset(0,1)
, then for every
(h,f)\in\mathbb{Y}_{s}
there exists a unique
(v,\eta)\in\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{sub}}_{s}
satisfying system (
4.1.1
). Moreover, the solution obeys estimate (
4.1.60
).

Proof.

We only prove the first item, as the second item follows from a nearly identical argument. We begin by justifying the uniqueness assertion. By shifting to the system (
4.1.57
) as in the proof of Theorem
4.5
, we see that it is sufficient to prove uniqueness in the case that
h=0
. Uniqueness in this case is a consequence of estimate (
4.1.29
) from Theorem
4.4
.

We now prove existence. Let
\{{(\mu_{n},\sigma_{n})}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(J\setminus\{{0}\})\times(K\setminus\{{0}\})
be any sequence satisfying
(\mu_{n},\sigma_{n})\to(\mu,\sigma)
as
n\to\infty
. Thanks to Proposition
4.7
, we obtain a corresponding sequence
\{{(v_{n},\eta_{n})}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset H^{2+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{3+s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

(4.2.4)

\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot v_{n}+\nabla\cdot(X\eta_{n})=h,\\
v_{n}+X\cdot\nabla v_{n}-\mu_{n}^{2}\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}v_{n}+(\vartheta-\sigma_{n}^{2}\theta\Delta)\nabla\eta_{n}=f.\end{cases}

Theorem
4.5
, applied along this sequence, yields the uniform bounds

(4.2.5)

\lVert v_{n},\eta_{n}\rVert_{{{}_{\mu_{n},\sigma_{n}}}\mathbb{X}_{s}}\lesssim\lVert h,f\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{s}}+\lVert v_{0},\eta_{0},\beta_{0}\rVert_{\mathbb{X}_{1+s}\times H^{1+s}}\lVert h,f\rVert_{\mathbb{Y}_{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}.

Thus, by the reflexivity of the container space, we may extract a subsequence that converges weakly to
(v,\eta)\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in the weak
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
topology. Taking weak limits along the subsequence in (
4.2.4
), we deduce that
(v,\eta)
is the desired solution. Sequential weak lower semicontinuity and (
4.2.5
) then provide the bound (
4.1.44
).
∎

4.3.
Synthesis

The goal of this subsection is to combine the principal part linear analysis of Sections
4.1
and
4.2
with the splitting analysis of Section
3.3
