, be Banach scales over the same field. Let
U\subseteq G^{r}
be an open set. Then the following are equivalent for all maps
P:U\times\prod_{j=1}^{k}E^{r}_{j}\to F^{0}
such that
P(g,\cdot)
is
k
-multilinear for all
g\in U
.

(1)

P\in T^{0}_{\mu,r}(U\times\prod_{j=1}^{k}E^{r}_{j};\mathbf{G}\times\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{E}_{j};\mathbf{F})
.

(2)

The restriction of
P
to
(U\cap G^{s})\times\prod_{j=1}^{k}E^{s}_{j}
is continuous as a map into
F^{s-\mu}
for all
\llbracket N\rrbracket\ni s\geqslant r
, and for all
g\in U
there exists a
G^{r}
-open subset
V\subseteq U
such that
g\in V
and whenever
\llbracket N\rrbracket\ni s\geqslant r
,
f\in V\cap G^{s}
, and
h_{i}\in E_{i}^{s}
for
i\in\{{1,\dots,k}\}
we have the estimate

(A.2.3)

\lVert P(f,h_{1},\dots,h_{k})\rVert_{F^{s-\mu}}\lesssim\langle\lVert f\rVert_{G^{s}}\rangle\prod_{i=1}^{k}\lVert h_{i}\rVert_{E_{i}^{r}}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lVert h_{j}\rVert_{E_{j}^{s}}\prod_{i\neq j}\lVert h_{i}\rVert_{E_{i}^{r}}.

A similar equivalence holds for maps
P\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{0}_{\mu,r}(U\times\prod_{j=1}^{k}E^{r}_{j};\mathbf{G}\times\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{E}_{j};\mathbf{F})
if we change the space in the first item to the space of strongly tame maps and we change the second item’s quantification of
V
to ‘for all bounded
G^{r}
-open sets
V\subseteq U
.’

Remark A.11
.

If
P\in T^{0}_{\mu,r}(\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{E}_{j};\mathbf{F})
is a
k
-multilinear mapping, then a simple modification of Lemma
A.10
shows that
P
is actually strongly tame. Moreover, by multilinearity, we have that
P
is automatically a smooth function whose derivatives are also multilinear maps. This combines with the previous fact to show that actually
P\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{\mu,r}(\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{E}_{j};\mathbf{F})
.

Corollary A.12

(Derivative estimates on tame maps)
.

Let
\mathbf{E}=\{{E^{s}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
and
\mathbf{F}=\{{F^{s}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
be Banach scales over a common field. Let
r,\mu\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
with
\mu\leqslant r
,
U\subseteq E^{r}
be an open set, and
P\in T^{k}_{\mu,r}(U,\mathbf{E};\mathbf{F})
. Then for each
f_{0}\in U
, there exists an open set
f_{0}\in V\subseteq U
with the property that for every
\llbracket N\rrbracket\ni s\geqslant r
there exists a constant
C_{s,V}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, depending on
s
and
V
, such that for all
f\in V\cap E^{s}
and all
g_{1},\dots,g_{k}\in E^{s}
we have the estimate

(A.2.4)

\lVert D^{k}P(f)[g_{1},\dots,g_{k}]\rVert_{F^{s-\mu}}\leqslant C_{s,V}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\lVert g_{\ell}\rVert_{E^{s}}\prod_{j\neq\ell}\lVert g_{j}\rVert_{E^{r}}+C_{s,V}\langle\lVert f\rVert_{E^{s}}\rangle\prod_{j=1}^{k}\lVert g_{j}\rVert_{E^{r}}.

Moreover, if
P\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{k}_{\mu,r}(U,\mathbf{E};\mathbf{F})
, then a similar assertion holds for every bounded and open subset
V\subseteq U
.

Proof.

This is a direct application of Lemma
A.10
.
∎

Proposition A.13

(Composition of tame maps)
.

Let
\mathbf{E}=\{{E^{s}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
,
\mathbf{F}=\{{F^{s}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
, and
\mathbf{G}=\{{G^{s}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
be a triple of Banach scales over a common field (
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
), and let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mu,\mu^{\prime},r,r^{\prime}\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
be such that
\mu\leqslant r
,
\mu^{\prime}\leqslant r^{\prime}
,
r^{\prime}+\mu\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
. Suppose that
U\subseteq E^{r}
,
U^{\prime}\subseteq F^{r^{\prime}}
are open sets,
P\in T^{k}_{\mu,r}(U,\mathbf{E};\mathbf{F})
, and
Q\in T^{k}_{\mu^{\prime},r^{\prime}}(U^{\prime},\mathbf{F};\mathbf{G})
. Set
V=U\cap E^{\max\{{r,r^{\prime}+\mu}\}}
. If
P(V)\subseteq U^{\prime}
, then
Q\circ P\in T^{k}_{\mu+\mu^{\prime},\max\{{r,\mu+r^{\prime}}\}}(V,\mathbf{E};\mathbf{G})
. A similar assertion holds for the composition of strongly tame maps.

Corollary A.14

(Tame products of tame
C^{k}
maps)
.

Let
\ell,k\in\mathbb{N}
with
\ell\geqslant 1
. For
j\in\{{1,\dots,\ell}\}
let
\mathbf{E}_{j}=\{{E^{s}_{j}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
and
\mathbf{F}_{j}=\{{E^{s}_{j}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
be Banach scales over a common field, and let
\mathbf{G}=\{{G^{s}}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
be a Banach scale over the same field. Let
r_{1},\dots,r_{\ell}\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
,
\mu_{j}\in\llbracket r_{j}\rrbracket
for
j\in\{{1,\dots,\ell}\}
,
r^{\prime}\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
, and
\mu^{\prime}\in\llbracket r^{\prime}\rrbracket
. Finally, suppose that
U_{j}\subseteq E^{r_{j}}_{j}
is open,
P_{j}\in T^{k}_{\mu_{j},r_{j}}(U_{j},\mathbf{E}_{j};\mathbf{F}_{j})
, and
B\in T^{0}_{\mu^{\prime},r^{\prime}}(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{F}_{j};\mathbf{G})
is
k
-multilinear. If we set

(A.2.5)

\mu=\mu^{\prime}+\max\{{\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{\ell}}\}\text{ and }r=\max\{{r_{1},\dots,r_{\ell},\mu_{1}+r^{\prime},\dots,\mu_{\ell}+r^{\prime}}\}

and assume that
r\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
, then the product map satisfies the inclusion

(A.2.6)

B(P_{1},\dots,P_{\ell})\in T^{k}_{\mu,r}\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}U_{j}\cap E^{r}_{j},\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{E}_{j};\mathbf{G}\Big).

A similar assertion holds for products of strongly tame maps.

Definition A.15

(Smoothable and LP-smoothable Banach scales)
.

Let
\mathbf{E}=\{E^{s}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
be a Banach scale.

(1)

We say that
\mathbf{E}
is smoothable if for each
j\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
there exists a linear map
S_{j}:E^{0}\to E^{N}
satisfying the following smoothing conditions for every
u\in E^{0}
:

(A.2.7)

\left\lVert S_{j}u\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\text{ for all }s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket,

(A.2.8)

\left\lVert S_{j}u\right\rVert_{E^{t}}\lesssim 2^{j(t-s)}\left\lVert S_{j}u\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\text{ for all }s,t\in\llbracket N\rrbracket\text{ with }s<t,

(A.2.9)

\left\lVert(I-S_{j})u\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\lesssim 2^{-j(t-s)}\left\lVert(I-S_{j})u\right\rVert_{E^{t}}\text{ for all }s,t\in\llbracket N\rrbracket\text{ with }s<t,

(A.2.10)

\left\lVert(S_{j+1}-S_{j})u\right\rVert_{E^{t}}\lesssim 2^{j(t-s)}\left\lVert(S_{j+1}-S_{j})u\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\text{ for all }s,t\in\llbracket N\rrbracket,

where the implicit constants are independent of
j
and are increasing in
s
and
t
.

(2)

We say that
\mathbf{E}
is LP-smoothable if
\mathbf{E}
is smoothable and the smoothing operators satisfy the following Littlewood-Paley condition: for
s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
and
u\in E^{s}
we have that

(A.2.11)

\lim_{j\to\infty}\lVert(I-S_{j})u\rVert_{E^{s}}=0,

and there exists a constant
A>0
, possibly depending on
s
, such that

(A.2.12)

A^{-1}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\leqslant\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left\lVert\Updelta_{j}u\right\rVert_{E^{s}}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\leqslant A\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{E^{s}},

where the operators
\{\Updelta_{j}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
are defined via
\Updelta_{j}=S_{j+1}-S_{j}
with the convention that
S_{0}=0
.

Example A.16

(A fixed Banach space)
.

Suppose that
X
is a Banach space and
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. Then the scale
\{{X}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
, generated by
X
alone, is LP-smoothable in the sense of Definition
A.15
, provided we take
S_{j}=I
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
.

Example A.17

(Sobolev spaces on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
)
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional real vector space and
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. The real Banach scales of Sobolev spaces
\{{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
and
\{{(\dot{H}^{-1}\cap H^{s})(\mathbb{R}^{d};V)}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
are LP-smoothable for the smoothing operators
\{{S_{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
given by
S_{j}=\mathds{1}_{B(0,2^{j})}(\nabla/2\pi\mathrm{i})
.

A.3.
A variation on the Nash-Moser inverse function theorem

The following definition gives us a sense of the mapping hypotheses one needs to verify to invoke a Nash-Moser inverse function theorem.

Definition A.18

(Mapping hypotheses)
.

We say that a triple
(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{F},\Psi)
satisfies the RI mapping hypotheses with parameters
(\mu,r,R)\in\llbracket N\rrbracket^{3}
if
\mathbf{E}=\{E^{s}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
and
\mathbf{F}=\{F^{s}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
are Banach scales over a common field,
1\leqslant\mu\leqslant r<R<\infty
,
R+\mu\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
, and there exists
0<\delta_{r}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\Psi:B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{r})\to F^{r-\mu}
is a map satisfying the following.

(1)

\Psi(0)=0
.

(2)

\mu
-tamely
C^{2}
: For every
r-\mu\leqslant s\in\llbracket N-\mu\rrbracket
we have that
\Psi:B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{r})\cap E^{s+\mu}\to F^{s}
is
C^{2}
, and for every
u_{0}\in B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{r})\cap E^{s+\mu}
we have the tame estimate

(A.3.1)

\left\lVert D^{2}\Psi(u_{0})[v,w]\right\rVert_{F^{s}}\leqslant C_{1}(s)(\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{E^{r}}+\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{E^{r}}\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}+\left\langle\left\lVert u_{0}\right\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\right\rangle\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{E^{r}}\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{E^{r}}).

Here the constant
C_{1}(s)
is increasing in
s
. In other words
\Psi\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{2}_{\mu,r}(B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{r}),\mathbf{E};\mathbf{F})
according to the notation from Definition
A.9
.

(3)

Derivative inversion: There exists
\delta_{R}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying
0<\delta_{R}\leqslant\delta_{r}
such that for every
u_{0}\in B_{E^{r}}(0,\delta_{R})\cap E^{N}
there exists a bounded linear operator
L(u_{0}):F^{r}\to E^{r}
satisfying the following three conditions.

(a)

For every
s\in\mathbb{N}\cap[r,R]
we have that the restriction of
L(u_{0})
to
F^{s}
defines a bounded linear operator with values in
E^{s}
, i.e.
L(u_{0})\in\mathcal{L}(F^{s};E^{s})
.

(b)

D\Psi(u_{0})L(u_{0})f=f
for every
f\in F^{r}
.

(c)

We have the tame estimate

(A.3.2)

\left\lVert L(u_{0})f\right\rVert_{E^{s}}\leqslant C_{2}(s)(\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{F^{s}}+\left\langle\left\lVert u_{0}\right\rVert_{E^{s+\mu}}\right\rangle\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{F^{r}})

for every
f\in F^{s}
and
r\leqslant s\leqslant R
, where again the constant
C_{2}(s)
is increasing in
s
.

Here the use of the prefix RI- is meant to indicate that the maps
L(u_{0})
are only required to be right inverses. We say that the triple
(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{F},\Psi)
satisfies the LRI mapping hypotheses if condition
(b)
in the third item is augmented by the left-inverse condition
(b^{\prime}):

(A.3.3)

L(u_{0})D\Psi(u_{0})v=v\text{ for every }v\in E^{r+\mu}.

We now state a slightly specified version of the Nash-Moser inverse function theorem. For a proof and a more general formulation, the reader is referred to Section 2 of Stevenson and Tice
[
56
]
.

Theorem A.19

(Inverse function theorem)
.

Let
\mathbf{E}=\{E^{s}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
and
\mathbf{F}=\{F^{s}\}_{s\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}
be Banach scales over the same field consisting of reflexive spaces, and assume that
\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{F}
are LP-smoothable (see Definition
A.15
). Assume the triple
(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{F},\Psi)
satisfies the LRI mapping hypotheses of Definition
A.18
with parameters
(\mu,r,R)
satisfying
2(r+\mu)+1<(r+R)/2
, and set
\beta=2(r+\mu)+1\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
. Then there exist
\varepsilon,\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}>0
such that the following hold.

(1)

Existence of local inverse: For every
g\in B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)
there exists a unique
u\in B_{E^{\beta}}(0,\kappa_{1}\varepsilon)
such that
\Psi(u)=g
.

(2)

Estimates of local inverse: The induced bijection
\Psi^{-1}:B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)\to\Psi^{-1}(B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon))\cap B_{E^{\beta}}(0,\kappa_{1}\varepsilon)
obeys the estimate

(A.3.4)

\lVert\Psi^{-1}(g)\rVert_{E^{\beta}}\leqslant\kappa_{1}\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta}}\text{ for all }g\in B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon).

Moreover, if
\mathbb{N}\ni\nu\leqslant R+r-2\beta
, then we have that

(A.3.5)

\Psi^{-1}:B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)\cap F^{\beta+\nu}\to E^{\beta+\nu}

with the estimate

(A.3.6)

\lVert\Psi^{-1}(g)\rVert_{E^{\beta+\nu}}\lesssim\lVert g\rVert_{F^{\beta+\nu}},

where the implied constant is independent of
g
.

(3)

Continuity: For
s\in[\beta,R+r-\beta-\mu)\cap\mathbb{N}
the map

(A.3.7)

\Psi^{-1}:B_{F^{\beta}}(0,\varepsilon)\cap F^{s}\to E^{s}

is continuous.

A.4.
Toolbox for smooth-tameness verification

We begin this appendix by recording some facts about products. In the subsequent three results, the following notation for specific Banach scales are used:

(A.4.1)

\mathbf{H}(W)=\{{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};W)}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}},\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbf{W}(W)=\{{W^{s,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};W)}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}},\hskip 10.00002pt\bm{\mathcal{H}}=\{{\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}},

for
W
a finite dimensional real vector space. The following is Lemma 3.3 from Stevenson and Tice
[
56
]
.

Lemma A.20

(Smooth tameness of products, 1)
.

Let
V_{1},V_{2}
and
W
be real finite dimensional vector spaces,
B:V_{1}\times V_{2}\to W
be a bilinear map. The following inclusions hold when viewing
B
as a product for functions taking values in
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
:
B\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}(\mathbf{H}(V_{1})\times\mathbf{H}(V_{2});\mathbf{H}(W))
, and
B\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,0}(\mathbf{H}(V_{1})\times\mathbf{W}(V_{2});\mathbf{H}(W))
.

Our next product result is proved in Lemma 3.4 in Stevenson and Tice
[
56
]
.

Lemma A.21

(Smooth tameness of products, 2)
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,
V
,
W
, and
V_{1},\dots,V_{m}
be real finite dimensional vector spaces,
B:V_{1}\times\cdots\times V_{m}\times V\to W
be
(m+1)
-linear. The
(m+1)
-linear map
P_{B}
defined via
P_{B}((g_{j},\psi_{j})_{j=1}^{m},\varphi)=B(g_{1}+\psi_{1},\dots,g_{m}+\psi_{m},\varphi)
satisfies the inclusion

(A.4.2)

P_{B}\in{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{m}(\mathbf{W}(V_{j})\times\mathbf{H}(V_{j}))\times\mathbf{H}(V);\mathbf{H}(W)\Big).

Lemma A.22

(Smooth tameness of products, 3)
.

Let
W
be a real finite dimensional vector space. Then the product map
(\eta,u)\mapsto\eta u
belongs to
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}(\bm{\mathcal{H}}\times\mathbf{H}(W);\mathbf{H}(W))
. Also, the product map
(\eta,\xi)\mapsto\eta\xi
belongs to
{{}_{\mathrm{s}}}T^{\infty}_{0,1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}(\bm{\mathcal{H}}\times\bm{\mathcal{H}};\bm{\mathcal{H}})
.

Proof.

The claimed mapping properties follow from Remark
A.11
and Corollary
A.5
.
∎

The remaining results of this sub-appendix are slightly more quantitative tame estimates.

Proposition A.23

(Tame estimates on simple multipliers)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varphi\in H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The following hold.

(1)

Suppose that
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
are such that
|\alpha|+|\beta|=k
and
\psi\in H^{\max\{{1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor,k}\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then the product
\partial^{\alpha}\psi\partial^{\beta}\varphi
belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and obeys the bound

(A.4.3)

\lVert\partial^{\alpha}\psi\partial^{\beta}\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\psi\rVert_{H^{1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}\lVert\psi\rVert_{H^{k}}+\begin{cases}0&\text{if }k\leqslant\lfloor d/2\rfloor,\\
\lVert\psi\rVert_{H^{k}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}&\text{if }1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor<k.\end{cases}

(2)

Suppose that
\psi\in H^{\max\{{1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor,k}\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then the product
\psi\varphi
belongs to
H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and obeys the bound

(A.4.4)

\lVert\psi\varphi\rVert_{H^{k}}\lesssim\lVert\psi\rVert_{H^{1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}\lVert\psi\rVert_{H^{k}}+\begin{cases}0&\text{if }k\leqslant 1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor,\\
\lVert\psi\rVert_{H^{k}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}&\text{if }1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor<k.\end{cases}

(3)

Suppose that
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
are such that
|\alpha|+|\beta|=k
and
\psi\in W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then the product
\partial^{\alpha}\psi\partial^{\beta}\varphi
belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and obeys the bound

(A.4.5)

\lVert\partial^{\alpha}\psi\partial^{\beta}\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\psi\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{k}}+\lVert\psi\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}}.

(4)

Suppose that
\psi\in W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then the product
\psi\varphi
belongs to
H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and obeys the bound

(A.4.6)

\lVert\psi\varphi\rVert_{H^{k}}\lesssim\lVert\psi\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{k}}+\lVert\psi\rVert_{W^{k,\infty}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}}.

Proof.

This is Corollary D.7 in Stevenson and Tice
[
56
]
.
∎

Proposition A.24

(Tame estimates on commutators)
.

Let
k,m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m\geqslant 1
,
j\in\{{1,\dots,d}\}
, and
\varphi\in H^{k+m-1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The following hold.

(1)

Suppose that
\psi\in H^{\max\{{2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor,k+m}\}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfies
\partial_{j}\psi\in H^{\max\{{1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor,k+m-1}\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then the commutator
[\partial_{j}^{m},\psi]\varphi
belongs to
H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and obeys the bound

(A.4.7)

\lVert[\partial_{j}^{m},\psi]\varphi\rVert_{H^{k}}\lesssim\lVert\partial_{j}\psi\rVert_{H^{1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{k+m-1}}+\begin{cases}0&\text{if }k+m\leqslant 2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor,\\
\lVert\partial_{j}\psi\rVert_{H^{k+m-1}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{1+\lfloor d/2\rfloor}}&\text{if }2+\lfloor d/2\rfloor<k+m.\end{cases}

(2)

Suppose that
\psi\in W^{k+m,\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfies
\partial_{j}\psi\in W^{k+m-1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then the commutator
[\partial_{j}^{m},\psi]\varphi
belongs to
H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and obeys the bound

(A.4.8)

\lVert[\partial_{j}^{m},\psi]\varphi\rVert_{H^{k}}\lesssim\lVert\partial_{j}\psi\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{k+m-1}}+\lVert\partial_{j}\psi\rVert_{W^{k+m-1,\infty}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}}.

Proof.

This is Corollary D.8 in Stevenson and Tice
[
56
]
.
∎

Appendix B
The shallow water system

B.1.
Derivation of the forced-traveling model

In this subsection we present a derivation of the forced-traveling formulation of the shallow water equations, which is system (
1.1.9
). While there are numerous derivations of the damped shallow water equations in the literature (see, for example, Bresch
[
14
]
), to best of the authors’ knowledge, there does not exist a derivation with the goal of determining the correct or physically relevant form of the forcing, nor is there a derivation for which the starting point is the traveling free boundary incompressible Navier-Stokes equations, rather than the dynamic equations. What we present here is a derivation, largely inspired by the one of Bresch
[
14
]
, in which these two gaps are filled. The resulting derivation then informs the structure of the system we study throughout the main body of the paper.

B.1.1.
Free boundary Navier-Stokes in traveling wave form

We assume a viscous incompressible fluid with viscosity
\overline{\mu}>0
fills a layer-like domain in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
n=d+1
for
d\geqslant 1
the dimension of the fluid’s cross-section. We posit a traveling wave ansatz moving in the
e_{1}
direction with speed
\overline{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}
,
and we assume that in the moving coordinate system the fluid occupies the stationary domain
\Omega_{\overline{\eta}}=\{{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\;:\>0<y<\overline{\eta}(x)}\}
with free boundary
\Sigma_{\overline{\eta}}=\{{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\;:\;y=\overline{\eta}(x)}\}
for an unknown free surface function
\overline{\eta}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
. We assume the equilibrium height of the fluid is
H>0
with the corresponding equilibrium domain
\Omega_{H}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,H)
, flat upper boundary
\Sigma_{H}
, and flat lower boundary
\Sigma_{0}
. The equilibrium domain can then be conveniently mapped to the moving one via
\mathfrak{F}:\Omega\to\Omega_{\overline{\eta}}
given by
\mathfrak{F}(x,y)=(x,\overline{\eta}(x)y/H)
. Following the derivation in Section 1 of Leoni and Tice
[
38
]
(see also
[
35
]
) with minor modification, we may use
\mathfrak{F}
to pose the traveling wave free boundary Navier-Stokes system in the fixed domain
\Omega_{H}
:

(B.1.1)

\begin{cases}(\overline{V}-\overline{\gamma}e_{1})\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\overline{V}-\overline{\mu}\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}\overline{V}+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\overline{p}=\overline{f}\circ\mathfrak{F}&\text{in }\Omega_{H}\\
\Div_{\mathcal{A}}{\overline{V}}=0&\text{in }\Omega_{H}\\
(\overline{p}I-\overline{\mu}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}\overline{V})\mathcal{N}=(\overline{g}\overline{\eta}-\overline{\sigma}\mathcal{H}(\overline{\eta}))\mathcal{N}+\overline{\Xi}\circ\mathfrak{F}\mathcal{N}&\text{on }\Sigma_{H}\\
\overline{V}\cdot\mathcal{N}+\overline{\gamma}\partial_{1}\overline{\eta}=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{H}\\
\overline{V}\cdot e_{n}=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{0}\\
(\overline{\mu}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}\overline{V}e_{n})^{\prime}=\overline{\alpha}\overline{V}^{\prime}&\text{on }\Sigma_{0}.\end{cases}

Here, in addition to
\overline{\eta}
, the unknowns are the velocity field
\overline{V}:\Omega_{H}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and the pressure
\overline{p}:\Omega_{H}\to\mathbb{R}
. In these equations and in what follows we employ the standard notation of
′
to indicate the first
d=n-1
components of a vector in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The vector field
\overline{f}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a given applied force, and
\overline{\Xi}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}_{\text{sym}}
is a given applied symmetric stress tensor.
\mathcal{N}=(-\nabla^{\prime}\overline{\eta},1)
is the non-unit normal vector. The surface tension coefficient is
\overline{\sigma}\geqslant 0
and
\mathcal{H}
refers to the following mean curvature

(B.1.2)

\mathcal{H}(\overline{\eta})=\mathrm{div}^{\prime}(\nabla^{\prime}\overline{\eta}(1+|\nabla^{\prime}\overline{\eta}|^{2})^{-1/2}).

The map
\mathcal{A}=(\nabla\mathcal{F})^{-\mathrm{t}}:\Omega_{H}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is

(B.1.3)

\mathcal{A}(x,y)=\begin{pmatrix}I_{d\times d}&-y\nabla^{\prime}\overline{\eta}(x)/\overline{\eta}(x)\\
0_{1\times d}&H/\overline{\eta}(x)\end{pmatrix},

and the associated
\mathcal{A}-
differential operators are given via

(B.1.4)

(\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\overline{p})_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{ij}\partial_{j}\overline{p},\;\Div_{\mathcal{A}}\overline{V}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{ij}\partial_{j}\overline{V}_{i},\text{ and }(\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}\overline{V})_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{m=1}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{jk}\partial_{k}(\mathcal{A}_{jm}\partial_{m}\overline{V}_{i})

and (for a given vector field
X
)

(B.1.5)

(X\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\overline{V})_{i}=\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}X_{j}\mathcal{A}_{jk}\partial_{k}\overline{V}_{i}\text{ and }(\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}\overline{V})_{ij}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(\mathcal{A}_{ik}\partial_{k}\overline{V}_{j}+\mathcal{A}_{jk}\partial_{k}\overline{V}_{i}).

B.1.2.
Scaling

We introduce the scale
\varepsilon>0
and introduce the rescaled functions

(B.1.6)

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\overline{V}^{\prime}(x,y)=\varepsilon u(\varepsilon x,y),&\overline{V}_{n}(x,y)=\varepsilon^{2}v(\varepsilon x,y),&\overline{p}(x,y)=\varepsilon^{2}p(\varepsilon x,y),\\
\overline{\eta}(x)=\eta(\varepsilon x),&\overline{f}^{\prime}(x,y)=\varepsilon^{3}f^{\prime}(\varepsilon x,y),&\overline{f}_{n}(x,y)=\varepsilon^{4}f_{n}(\varepsilon x,y).\end{array}

This handles everything except for
\overline{\Xi}
, which we further decompose as

(B.1.7)

\overline{\Xi}=\begin{pmatrix}\overline{\Xi}^{\prime}&\overline{\xi}\\
\overline{\xi}&\overline{\Xi}_{nn}\end{pmatrix}\text{ for }\overline{\Xi}^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\text{ and }\overline{\xi}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},

and then rescale according to

(B.1.8)

\overline{\Xi}^{\prime}(x,y)=\varepsilon^{2}\Xi^{\prime}(\varepsilon x,y),\;\overline{\xi}(x,y)=\varepsilon^{3}\xi(\varepsilon x,y),\;\overline{\Xi}_{nn}(x,y)=\varepsilon^{2}\Xi_{nn}(\varepsilon x,y).

Finally, we rescale the physical parameters via

(B.1.9)

\overline{\gamma}=\varepsilon\gamma,\;\overline{g}=\varepsilon^{2}g,\;\overline{\mu}=\mu,\;\overline{\sigma}=\sigma,\;\overline{\alpha}=\varepsilon^{2}\alpha.

Plugging in these rescaled quantities yields the following. The momentum equation’s horizontal part is

(B.1.10)

\varepsilon^{3}f^{\prime}(\cdot,y\eta/H)=\varepsilon^{3}\nabla^{\prime}p-\varepsilon^{3}(y/\eta)\nabla^{\prime}\eta\partial_{n}p+\varepsilon^{3}(u-\gamma e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\prime}u+\varepsilon^{3}((-y/\eta)\nabla^{\prime}\eta\cdot(u-\gamma e_{1})(H/\eta)v)\partial_{n}v\\
-\mu(\varepsilon^{3}\Delta^{\prime}u+\varepsilon((H/\eta)^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}(y/\eta)^{2}\char 12\relax{\nabla^{\prime}\eta}^{2})\partial_{n}^{2}u-\varepsilon^{3}2(y/\eta)\nabla\eta\cdot\nabla\partial_{n}u-\varepsilon^{3}(y/\eta^{2})(\eta\Delta^{\prime}\eta-2\char 12\relax{\nabla^{\prime}\eta}^{2})\partial_{n}u),

and its vertical part is

(B.1.11)

\varepsilon^{4}f_{n}(\cdot,y\eta/H)=\varepsilon^{2}(H/\eta)\partial_{n}p+\varepsilon^{4}(u-\gamma e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\prime}v+\varepsilon^{4}((-y/\eta)\nabla^{\prime}\eta\cdot(u-\gamma e_{1})+(H/\eta)v)\partial_{n}v\\
-\mu(\varepsilon^{4}\Delta^{\prime}v+\varepsilon^{2}((H/\eta)^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}(y/\eta)^{2}\char 12\relax{\nabla^{\prime}\eta}^{2})\partial_{n}^{2}v-\varepsilon^{4}2(y/\eta)\nabla\eta\cdot\nabla\partial_{n}v-\varepsilon^{4}(y/\eta^{2})(\eta\Delta^{\prime}\eta-2\char 12\relax{\nabla^{\prime}\eta}^{2})\partial_{n}v).

The balance of mass becomes

(B.1.12)

\varepsilon^{2}\Div^{\prime}u-\varepsilon^{2}(y/\eta)\nabla^{\prime}\eta\cdot\partial_{n}u+\varepsilon^{2}(H/\eta)\partial_{n}v=0.

The dynamic boundary condition’s normal part gives

(B.1.13)

\varepsilon^{2}p-\varepsilon^{2}g\eta+\sigma\Big(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\Delta^{\prime}\eta}{(1+\varepsilon^{2}\char 12\relax{\nabla^{\prime}\eta}^{2})^{1/2}}-\frac{\varepsilon^{4}(\nabla^{\prime})^{2}\eta\nabla^{\prime}\eta\cdot\nabla^{\prime}\eta}{(1+\varepsilon^{2}\char 12\relax{\nabla^{\prime}\eta}^{2})^{3/2}}\Big)-\mu(\varepsilon^{4}\mathbb{D}^{\prime}u\nabla^{\prime}\eta\cdot\nabla^{\prime}\eta-2\varepsilon^{4}\nabla^{\prime}\eta\cdot\nabla^{\prime}v\\
-\varepsilon^{4}2(y/\eta)\char 12\relax{\nabla^{\prime}\eta}^{2}\nabla^{\prime}\eta\cdot\partial_{n}u-\varepsilon^{2}(H/\eta)\nabla^{\prime}\eta\cdot\partial_{n}u+2\varepsilon^{4}(y/\eta)\char 12\relax{\nabla^{\prime}\eta}^{2}\partial_{n}v+2\varepsilon^{2}(H/\eta)\partial_{n}v)(1+\varepsilon^{2}\char 12\relax{\nabla^{\prime}\eta}^{2})^{-1}\\
-(\varepsilon^{4}\Xi^{\prime}(\cdot,\eta)\nabla^{\prime}\eta\cdot\nabla^{\prime}\eta-\varepsilon^{4}2\xi(\cdot,\eta)\cdot\nabla^{\prime}\eta+\varepsilon^{2}\Xi_{nn}(\cdot,\eta))(1+\varepsilon^{2}\char 12\relax{\nabla^{\prime}\eta}^{2})^{-1}=0,

while its tangential part yields (suppressing higher order terms)

(B.1.14)

\varepsilon\mu(H/\eta)\partial_{n}u+\varepsilon^{3}\mu(-\mathbb{D}^{\prime}u\nabla^{\prime}\eta+2(H/\eta)\partial_{n}u\char 12\relax{\nabla^{\prime}\eta}^{2}+\nabla^{\prime}v+(H/\eta)\partial_{n}v\nabla^{\prime}\eta-(H/\eta)\nabla^{\prime}\eta\cdot\partial_{n}u\nabla^{\prime}\eta)\\
+\varepsilon^{3}(-\Xi^{\prime}(\cdot,\eta)\nabla^{\prime}\eta+\xi(\cdot,\eta)+\Xi_{nn}(\cdot,\eta)\nabla^{\prime}\eta)=O(\varepsilon^{4})

and

(B.1.15)

\varepsilon^{2}\mu(H/\eta)\nabla^{\prime}\eta\cdot\partial_{n}u=O(\varepsilon^{4}).

The kinematic boundary condition becomes

(B.1.16)

\varepsilon^{2}\gamma\partial_{1}\eta-\varepsilon^{2}\nabla^{\prime}\eta\cdot u+\varepsilon^{2}v=0.

The Navier-slip boundary conditions on the bottom (where
y=0
) read

(B.1.17)

v=0\text{ and }\varepsilon\mu(H/\eta)\partial_{n}u=\varepsilon^{3}\alpha u.

B.1.3.
Formal expansion and equations of motion

We now make the assumption that for
F\in\{\eta,u,v,p,f,\Xi\}
we have a formal expansion in powers of
\varepsilon^{2}
:
F=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{2k}F_{k}
. These expansions can then be plugged into the equations (
B.1.10
)–(
B.1.17
) to derive a hierarchy of equations. As it turns out, for our purposes here we only need to track
\eta_{0},u_{0},v_{0},p_{0},f_{0},\Xi_{0}
and
u_{1}
. In order to simplify notation we will drop the zero subscripts and denote
u_{1}
by
\overline{u}
.

We begin by recording the lowest order terms. The order
\varepsilon^{2}
terms in the equations (
B.1.10
)–(
B.1.12
) yield the following in
\Omega_{H}
:

(B.1.18)

(H/\eta)^{2}\partial_{n}^{2}u=0,\;(H/\eta)^{2}\partial_{n}^{2}v+(H/\eta)\partial_{n}p=0,\text{ and }\Div^{\prime}u+(H/\eta)\partial_{n}v-(y/\eta)\nabla^{\prime}\eta\cdot\partial_{n}u=0.

On
\Sigma_{H}
the
\varepsilon^{2}
part of (
B.1.13
) and (
B.1.14
) yield

(B.1.19)

p-g\eta+\sigma\Delta^{\prime}\eta-\Xi_{nn}(\cdot,\eta)-\mu(H/\eta)(-\nabla^{\prime}\eta\cdot\partial_{n}u+2\partial_{n}v)=0,\text{ and }\mu(H/\eta)\partial_{n}u=0.

The
\varepsilon^{2}
terms in (
B.1.16
) and (
B.1.17
) show that

(B.1.20)

\gamma\partial_{1}\eta-\nabla^{\prime}\eta\cdot u+v=0\text{ on }\Sigma_{H},\text{ and }v=0\text{ and }\mu(H/\eta)\partial_{n}u=0\text{ on }\Sigma_{0}.

The equation (
B.1.18
), together with (
B.1.19
) and (
B.1.20
) imply that
\partial_{n}u=0
, or in other words,
u=u(x)
. We then integrate the third equation in (
B.1.18
) vertically and use (
B.1.20
) to compute
(H/\eta)v=-y\Div^{\prime}u
. In turn, this implies that
\partial_{n}^{2}v=0
, and so (
B.1.18
) implies that
\partial_{n}p=0
, so

(B.1.21)

p=p(\cdot,H)=g\eta-\sigma\Delta^{\prime}\eta+\Xi_{nn}(\cdot,\eta)+2\mu(H/\eta)\partial_{n}u=g\eta-\sigma\Delta^{\prime}\eta+\Xi_{nn}(\cdot,\eta)-2\mu\Div^{\prime}u.

Finally, plugging into (
B.1.20
) shows that
\gamma\partial_{1}\eta-\nabla^{\prime}\eta\cdot u=-v(\cdot,H)=\eta\Div^{\prime}u
, and hence

(B.1.22)

\Div^{\prime}(\eta(u-\gamma e_{1}))=\gamma\partial_{1}\eta-\Div^{\prime}(\eta u)=0.

This is all we learn at the lowest level of the expansion in
\varepsilon
, so we now proceed to the next.

At order
\varepsilon^{3}
(
B.1.10
) gives an equation for
u
, coupled to
\overline{u}
:

(B.1.23)

f^{\prime}(\cdot,y\eta/H)=(u-\gamma e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\prime}u-\mu\Delta^{\prime}u-\mu(H/\eta)^{2}\partial_{n}^{2}\overline{u}+\nabla^{\prime}p\\
=(u-\gamma e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\prime}u-\mu\Delta^{\prime}u-\mu(H/\eta)^{2}\partial_{n}^{2}\overline{u}+\nabla^{\prime}(g\eta-\sigma\Delta^{\prime}\eta+\Xi_{nn}(\cdot,\eta)-2\mu\Div^{\prime}u).

At order
\varepsilon^{3}
(
B.1.14
) yields

(B.1.24)

\Xi^{\prime}(\cdot,\eta)\nabla^{\prime}\eta-\xi(\cdot,\eta)-\Xi_{nn}(\cdot,\eta)\nabla^{\prime}\eta=\mu(H/\eta)\partial_{n}\overline{u}+\mu(-\mathbb{D}^{\prime}u\nabla^{\prime}\eta+(H/\eta)\partial_{n}v\nabla^{\prime}\eta+\nabla^{\prime}v)\\
=\mu(H/\eta)\partial_{n}\overline{u}-\mu\mathbb{D}^{\prime}u\nabla^{\prime}\eta-\mu\nabla^{\prime}(\eta\Div^{\prime}{u})-\mu(\Div^{\prime}{u})\nabla^{\prime}\eta,

and (
B.1.17
) yields
\mu(H/\eta)\partial_{n}\overline{u}=\alpha u
, which together show that

(B.1.25)

\int_{0}^{H}\mu(H/\eta)^{2}\partial_{n}^{2}\overline{u}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y=(H/\eta)(\Xi^{\prime}(\cdot,\eta)\nabla^{\prime}\eta-\xi(\cdot,\eta)-\Xi_{nn}(\cdot,\eta)\nabla^{\prime}\eta\\
+\mu\mathbb{D}^{\prime}u\nabla^{\prime}\eta+\mu\nabla^{\prime}(\eta\Div^{\prime}{u})+\mu(\Div^{\prime}{u})\nabla^{\prime}\eta-\alpha u).

Thus, upon averaging (
B.1.23
) over
y\in(0,H)
and using (
B.1.25
), we eliminate
\overline{u}
and get

(B.1.26)

\frac{1}{H}\int_{0}^{H}f^{\prime}(\cdot,y\eta/H)\;\mathrm{d}y=(u-\gamma e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\prime}u-\mu\Delta^{\prime}u-(\mu\mathbb{D}^{\prime}u\nabla^{\prime}\eta+\mu\nabla^{\prime}(\eta\Div^{\prime}{u})+\mu(\Div^{\prime}{u})\nabla^{\prime}\eta-\alpha u)/\eta\\
-(\Xi^{\prime}(\cdot,\eta)\nabla^{\prime}\eta-\xi(\cdot,\eta)-\Xi_{nn}(\cdot,\eta)\nabla^{\prime}\eta)/\eta+\nabla^{\prime}(g\eta-\sigma\Delta^{\prime}\eta+\Xi_{nn}(\cdot,\eta)-2\mu\Div^{\prime}u).

Note that

(B.1.27)

\frac{1}{H}\int_{0}^{H}f^{\prime}(\cdot,y\eta/H)\;\mathrm{d}y=\frac{1}{\eta}\int_{0}^{\eta}f^{\prime}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y,

so (
B.1.26
) can be rewritten as

(B.1.28)

\int_{0}^{\eta}f^{\prime}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y=\eta(u-\gamma e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\prime}u-\mu\eta\Delta^{\prime}u-(\mu\mathbb{D}^{\prime}u\nabla^{\prime}\eta+\mu\nabla^{\prime}(\eta\Div^{\prime}{u})+\mu(\Div^{\prime}{u})\nabla^{\prime}\eta-\alpha u)\\
-(\Xi^{\prime}(\cdot,\eta)\nabla^{\prime}\eta-\xi(\cdot,\eta)-\Xi_{nn}(\cdot,\eta)\nabla^{\prime}\eta)+\eta\nabla^{\prime}(g\eta-\sigma\Delta^{\prime}\eta+\Xi_{nn}(\cdot,\eta)-2\mu\Div^{\prime}u).

Upon computing

(B.1.29)

\Div^{\prime}(\eta\mathbb{D}^{\prime}u+2\eta(\Div^{\prime}u)I)=\mathbb{D}^{\prime}u\nabla^{\prime}\eta+\eta\Delta^{\prime}u+2\Div^{\prime}{u}\nabla^{\prime}\eta+3\eta\nabla^{\prime}\Div^{\prime}u\\
=\mathbb{D}^{\prime}u\nabla^{\prime}\eta+\eta\Delta^{\prime}u+\nabla^{\prime}(\eta\Div^{\prime}u)+\Div^{\prime}{u}\nabla^{\prime}\eta+2\eta\nabla^{\prime}\Div^{\prime}u,

we may rewrite the prior equation as

(B.1.30)

\eta(u-\gamma e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\prime}u+\alpha u+\eta\nabla^{\prime}(g\eta-\sigma\Delta^{\prime}\eta)-\mu\Div^{\prime}(\eta\mathbb{D}^{\prime}u+2\eta(\Div^{\prime}u)I)\\
=\int_{0}^{\eta}f^{\prime}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y+\Xi^{\prime}(\cdot,\eta)\nabla^{\prime}\eta-\xi(\cdot,\eta)-\Xi_{nn}(\cdot,\eta)\nabla^{\prime}\eta-\eta\nabla(\Xi_{nn}(\cdot,\eta)).

We now combine (
B.1.22
) and (
B.1.30
) and drop the primes on the differential operators to arrive at the forced-traveling shallow water system:

(B.1.31)

\begin{cases}\gamma\partial_{1}\eta-\Div(\eta u)=0,\\
\eta(u-\gamma e_{1})\cdot\nabla u+\alpha u+\eta\nabla(g\eta-\sigma\Delta\eta)-\mu\Div(\eta\mathbb{D}u+2\eta(\Div u)I)\\
\quad=\int_{0}^{\eta}f^{\prime}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y+\Xi^{\prime}(\cdot,\eta)\nabla\eta-\xi(\cdot,\eta)-\Xi_{nn}(\cdot,\eta)\nabla\eta-\eta\nabla(\Xi_{nn}(\cdot,\eta)).\end{cases}

B.2.
Dissipation calculation

The goal of this subsection is to justify the role of the forcing term
\mathcal{F}
in the generation of nontrivial traveling wave solutions to the shallow water system.

Proposition B.1

(Power-dissipation of the shallow water system)
.

Suppose that
v\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
\mathcal{F}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
obey system (
1.1.9
) for some choice of
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
\mu,\sigma\in[0,\infty)
. Then identity (
1.1.16
) is valid.

Proof.

The strategy is to test the second equation in (
1.1.9
) with
v
and integrate by parts. The term involving
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{F}\cdot v
is left alone, but we recompute the rest. First, for the dissipation term we readily obtain

(B.2.1)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(v-\mu^{2}\nabla\cdot((1+\eta)\mathbb{S}v))\cdot v=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v|^{2}+\mu^{2}\Big(\frac{1}{2}|\mathbb{D}^{0}v|^{2}+2\Big(1+\frac{1}{d}\Big)|\nabla\cdot v|^{2}\Big).

Next, consider the advective derivative term:

(B.2.2)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\eta)((v-\gamma e_{1})\cdot\nabla v)\cdot v=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\cdot((1+\eta)(v-\gamma e_{1}))|v|^{2}=0,

where the final equality follows via applying the first equation in (
1.1.9
). Finally, we consider the gravity capillary term. Fix a frequency cut-off parameter
\kappa\in(0,1)
and split
\eta=\Uppi^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta+\Uppi^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta
according to (
A.1.7
). We then have the identities

(B.2.3)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\eta)(I-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\eta\cdot v=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\eta)(I-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\Uppi^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{L}}\eta\cdot v-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\Uppi^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta\nabla\cdot((1+\eta)v)

and, thanks again to the first equation in (
1.1.9
) and orthogonality considerations,

(B.2.4)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\Uppi^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta\nabla\cdot((1+\eta)v)=\gamma\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\Uppi^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta\partial_{1}\Uppi^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{H}}\eta=0.

We combine (
B.2.3
) and (
B.2.4
) and then take the limit as
\kappa\to 0
. The first term on the right hand side of (
B.2.3
) vanishes and we are left with

(B.2.5)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\eta)(I-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\eta\cdot v=0.

By synthesizing (
B.2.1
), (
B.2.2
), and (
B.2.5
), we obtain (
1.1.16
).
∎

Corollary B.2

(On the uniqueness of the trivial solution)
.

Suppose that
v\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
\mathcal{F}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
obey system (
1.1.9
) for some choice of
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
\mu,\sigma\in[0,\infty)
. If
\mathcal{F}=0
and
1+\eta\geqslant 0
a.e., then
v=0
and
\eta=0
.

Proof.

We apply Proposition
B.1
and invoke the additional hypotheses to see that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v|^{2}\leqslant 0
, and hence
v=0
. We then use the second equation in (
1.1.9
) to deduce that
(I-\sigma^{2}\Delta)\nabla\eta=0
,
which implies that
\eta
is a constant. By hypothesis,
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and this space does not contain nontrivial constants; therefore,
\eta=0
as well.
∎
